


sqlglot Composer 8.2: How-tos


sqlglot Composer 8.2: How-tos

Each how-to walks through one job from start to finish: its goal, when you'd use it, its
steps, how to check it worked, the mistakes people make first, and what to read next. Its steps
are one notebook: paste them in order, starting with the import. Each step shows its Python,
then what it gives: its Hive (ready to paste into another program), its result on the Example
database, or a file it writes. The Example database is nine made-up tables that ship inside the
Toolbox. Every Hive, result, refusal and Warning here comes from running the steps.

The Example database holds two days of ops.job_runs, 2026-09-23 and
2026-09-24 (the three tables the Intermediate how-tos use hold 14 days), and the steps here were
run as if today were 2026-09-25. Where a step uses last_n_days, it counts back from
your real today, so in your own notebook it finds no rows on the Example database: write
between with the dates instead.

The Example gallery, beside this page in the
sqlglot_composer folder, holds every Worked example, and the how-tos link to it.

A Statement is built along a chain of types, each made by a call on the one before. A step
says the type of each value it makes in a comment, such as # -> Statement.


You writeIt givesWhich is

ops.jobs, a Table referenceTablea table: its columns, its Partitions and its Key

ops.jobs.job_idColumnone of its columns

a test, such as equals(...)Conditiona test of each row, for WHERE or ON

SELECT(...), FROM(...), WHERE(...) and the othersClauseone part of a query

statement(...)Statementits Clauses, checked together as one query

to_hive(...)strthe Hive: the query's text, ready to paste

run_query(..., query)DataFramethe rows your query gives back, in pandas




Show only the how-tos holding every word:

Getting started


1. Start a Project Getting started

Goal

Get a Project ready. A Project is your folder at work that holds the Toolbox and your own scripts in one fixed layout: warehouse.py, where your own two functions, query and execute, run Hive SQL on your warehouse, and, as you add them, your tables' Table references (a file describing one table), the Building blocks your notebooks share (pieces of code reused across notebooks) and a notebook per Saved table (a table your own Statements write). You install what the Toolbox needs, copy it into the folder, let project.start() lay the folder out, learn which call makes each file of a Project and what you fill in, and set your connection.

When you'd use it

If you are about to write Hive with the Toolbox for your team's tables, start a Project once, as the steps below do: every notebook after it lives in the Project and finds the Toolbox and your query and execute there.
If you have done this how-to, go on with the Getting started how-tos after it: each works in the Project this one makes, one step at a time.
If you want to see a Project filled in, read the Starter Example project, much like the one those how-tos leave.


Steps

1. Install what the Toolbox needs

Goal: the packages the Toolbox runs on. The Toolbox needs Python 3.11 or newer, pandas, numpy and sqlglot. Download the Toolbox and extract it; in its top folder, requirements.txt pins each package to the version it is tested on. Install them from a terminal in that folder, with pip install -r requirements.txt, or from a notebook, with %pip install -r followed by the path to that file.

A package your notebooks already have, such as pandas, needn't be installed again: delete its line from requirements.txt first, so pip keeps the version you have.

Done when pip says each package is installed. Then step 2.

2. Make the Project's folder and copy the Toolbox in

Goal: a folder holding the Toolbox. Make an empty folder for the Project, such as my_project. The Toolbox is one folder, sqlglot_composer: copy it whole from the download into the Project's folder. Keep your own files beside it, never inside it: to update the Toolbox, you delete the folder and copy the new one in.

Done when the Project's folder holds sqlglot_composer. Then step 3.

3. Open a notebook in the Project's folder

Goal: a notebook that finds the Toolbox. Open a notebook in the Project's folder, such as start.ipynb, for the steps here. Jupyter runs a notebook in the folder it is in, so the notebook finds sqlglot_composer beside it. The folder, the copied Toolbox and this notebook are the only things of a Project you make by hand: the Toolbox makes the rest.

Done when start.ipynb is open beside sqlglot_composer. Then step 4.

4. Import the project module

Goal: the module that makes the Project's files. The project module makes and keeps the Project's folders and files. Import it by its name:

from sqlglot_composer import project

If the import stops instead, its message says what happened, why it matters and the usual fix. Most often a file is missing, or files from different downloads were mixed in one folder.

Done when the import runs without a message. Then step 5.

5. Start the Project

Goal: the Project's layout. project.start() lays out the folder your notebook runs in:

project.start()

Python shows
Started a Project in the folder you're working in: admin/, references/, building_blocks/,
tables/ and warehouse.py.
Next: set conn, your connection, in warehouse.py.

It writes 22 files

admin/admin_log.md
admin/check_project.ipynb
admin/check_references.ipynb
admin/databases.py
admin/new_block.ipynb
admin/project_version.txt
admin/refresh_reference_sheet.ipynb
admin/registry.csv
admin/remove.ipynb
admin/run_tables.ipynb
admin/sent.csv
admin/templates/building_block.py
admin/templates/quality_checks.py
admin/templates/saved_table.py
admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py
admin/workflows/change_a_saved_table/change_a_saved_table.ipynb
admin/workflows/convert/convert.ipynb
admin/workflows/new_table/new_table.ipynb
admin/workflows/promote/promote.ipynb
admin/workflows/references/references.ipynb
admin/workflows/upgrade/upgrade.ipynb
warehouse.py

Done when it says Started a Project. Then step 6.

6. Know every file of a Project

Goal: know which call makes each file, and what you fill in. Besides the three things you made by hand, each file of a Project is made by a call of the project module, a Project action: a one-time change to the Project's files, recorded in the Admin log. Make each file with its Project action, never by hand in your file explorer. Here is every file, the call that makes it, and what you fill in:

my_project/, the Project's folder: made by you, in your file explorer.
sqlglot_composer/: copied in by you. You fill in nothing: never edit a file inside it.
start.ipynb, or any name you like, the notebook you run project.start() from: made by you, in Jupyter. Keep it for trying things out, or delete it.
warehouse.py: project.start(). You set conn, your connection, in it, in step 8.
admin/, a notebook for each job on the whole Project, such as check_project.ipynb, run_tables.ipynb and remove.ipynb: project.start(). You fill in the names some ask for in their names cell, such as the tables to remove, and run them. Calling the same Project action from any notebook of the Project does just the same.
admin/workflows/, a folder per Workflow, an ordered list of steps that takes the Project to one end goal, such as new_table/, references/ and convert/, each holding its notebook: project.start(). You name what the Workflow works on in its notebook's names cell, such as the tables, and save it; then run each step's cell in turn, or project.do_next(), which runs the steps it can for you, those that change only files, up to the first it can't. project.check() names the next step.
admin/databases.py, the names of your Dev Database, where you create and write tables of your own, and your Prod Database, where others read your finished tables: project.start(). You fill in the two names, DEV and PROD.
admin/sent.csv, a row for each change a notebook of the Project sends to the warehouse: project.start(), then each change you run on the warehouse. You fill in nothing.
admin/admin_log.md, the Admin log, the record of each Project action and each change you run: project.start(), then each Project action adds an entry. You fill in nothing.
admin/registry.csv, the registry, the list of what each Project action made: project.start(), then each Project action that makes or archives a Table folder, a Table reference, a Building block or a Conversion folder adds a row. You fill in nothing.
admin/project_version.txt, the Toolbox version that last wrote the Project: project.start(), then project.upgrade(). You fill in nothing.
admin/workflows/convert/<name>/, a Conversion folder per batch of someone else's Hive you convert, its scripts as given and what each statement became: project.convert(...), from admin/workflows/convert/convert.ipynb, which makes each table the Hive writes a Saved table in your Dev Database. You answer each of its tables' Decisions, the things only you decide, as Convert someone else's Hive shows.
an Upgrade's checklist in admin/, named for the version it upgraded to, such as upgrade_7.3.md: project.upgrade(), once you copy in a newer Toolbox. You make each change it lists, each marked # UPGRADE <version>: at its line.
admin/templates/, the Templates a new notebook or Building block is made from: project.start(). Nothing, unless you want your team's own shape.
references/<db>__<table>.py, a Table reference per table: project.add_references([...], query=query) for a table in the warehouse, or project.new_table(...) for a Saved table of your own. You fill in its TODO lines, and add your own names for its columns, as Add your tables' references shows.
references/<db>.py, a Database module per Database, the group of tables a name such as ops in ops.jobs names, such as references/ops.py: written again by each Project action that adds or archives a Table reference. Leave it as the Project actions write it.
references/reference_sheet.csv, the Reference sheet, every column of every table you have a Table reference for: project.refresh_reference_sheet(). You fill in nothing.
building_blocks/<name>.py, a Building block: project.new_block("<name>"). You fill in its <...> and TODO lines, as Share Building blocks shows.
tables/<db>__<table>/<db>__<table>.ipynb, a Table notebook per Saved table, whose cells create, preview and write it: project.new_table("<db>.<table>"). You fill in its <...> lines, as Save a table shows.
tables/<db>__<table>/lineage/, the Lineage of that table, which table columns feed each of its columns: its notebook's lineage cell. You fill in nothing.
tables/<db>__<table>/<db>__<table>.hql, the Hive that table's logic sends: its notebook's Hive cell, written again each time it runs, as Save a table shows. You fill in nothing: edit the logic instead.
lineage/, beside the notebook you run it from, such as start.ipynb: the Lineage of a Statement exported outside a Table notebook, by export_lineage(...), as Follow lineage shows. You fill in nothing.
admin/removed/, what you archive: project.remove_table(...), project.remove_reference(...) and project.remove_block(...) move a file or folder here rather than delete it, as does project.remove_conversion(...) with a Conversion folder; project.upgrade() keeps here each file as it was before it changed it, and project.convert(..., overwrite=True) each file it writes over.


references/, building_blocks/ and tables/ are empty for now. Each Project action writes over nothing of yours: most refuse a file or folder that is already there, and convert, run again, writes a fresh copy of a file you changed into its batch's folder. Each logs what it did in the Admin log. Path, from Python's own pathlib, reads a file; the log's first entry is the one after its heading:

from pathlib import Path
print(Path("admin/admin_log.md").read_text(encoding="utf-8").split("## ")[1])

Python prints
2026-09-25 09:00:00, Project action

- Call: `project.start()`
- Files: created admin/, references/, building_blocks/, tables/, warehouse.py
- Script: a notebook, at commit 1a2b3c4
- Result: worked

The Script line names the git commit your Project's folder is at, when you keep it in git, and nogit when you don't; this page shows a made-up one, 1a2b3c4.

Done when the Admin log's first entry names project.start(). Then step 7.

7. Read the warehouse.py start wrote

Goal: know how the Toolbox reaches your warehouse. The Toolbox never connects to anything itself. It writes Hive as text, and hands that text to a function in warehouse.py, which hands it to your connection to the warehouse. Your team's library most likely gives you a connection with two calls: one that gives back rows, as a SELECT does, and one that gives back nothing, as a statement that changes a table does, such as a write or a CREATE TABLE. So warehouse.py holds your connection, conn, set once, and two functions over it:

query runs Hive through conn.query, and gives back its rows as a pandas DataFrame;
execute runs Hive through conn.execute, and gives back nothing.


You pick which one sends each Statement, a query the Toolbox builds, by the Toolbox function you call, as Build and run a first statement shows. project.start() set conn to the Example database, the made-up tables that ship inside the Toolbox, so every how-to runs before you touch a real table:

print(Path("warehouse.py").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))

Python prints
"""warehouse - your connection to the warehouse, conn, and its two calls.

Why: every notebook in the Project reaches the warehouse through query and execute, so a change
to how you reach it is made here, once.

Set conn to your connection: an object whose query(sql) runs Hive that gives back rows and gives
them back as a pandas DataFrame, and whose execute(sql) runs Hive that changes something:
whatever it gives back, execute drops. Until you do, conn is the Example database, which the
how-tos run on. You
pick which one sends each Statement, by calling run_query(s, query) for what gives back rows, as
a SELECT does, or run_execute(s, execute) for what changes something, as a write does. Let the
two share one session, so a Session setting sent through execute holds for what is sent after
it; and let each raise when the warehouse reports a failure, since the Toolbox knows a step
failed only when your connection raises. Keep it importing nothing from references/,
building_blocks/ or tables/, so any notebook or script of yours can import it; a connection
kept in a file outside the Project is imported as the comment above conn shows.
"""

import pandas as pd

from sqlglot_composer import example_database

# Your connection to the warehouse. At work, replace example_database with your own, such as
# conn = your_library.connect(...): its query(sql) gives back a DataFrame, and its
# execute(sql) runs Hive that changes something. A connection kept in a file outside the
# Project, such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, in place of the line
# conn = example_database below:
#     import sys
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
conn = example_database


def query(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Run a Hive string that gives back rows, and give them back as a pandas DataFrame."""
    return conn.query(hive)


def execute(hive: str) -> None:
    """Run a Hive string that changes something, such as a write, and give back nothing."""
    conn.execute(hive)

Among the lines the ... above leaves out, warehouse.py imports the Example database from the Toolbox:

from sqlglot_composer import example_database

example_database.query runs Hive on the Example database's nine tables, and example_database.execute prints the first line of the Hive and changes nothing, since the Example database can't be written to. The Getting started how-tos keep warehouse.py just so.

Done when warehouse.py shows conn = example_database and the two functions. Then step 8.

8. Set your connection

Goal: query and execute that reach your warehouse. At work, open warehouse.py in your editor and set conn to your team's connection, once, in place of example_database. conn can be anything with the two calls: a module, as example_database is, or the object your team's library connects you with. Here my_api and its connect call are made-up names for yours:

import my_api  # your team's library

conn = my_api.connect()  # its query gives back a DataFrame; its execute runs a change

If your team keeps its connection in a file outside the Project, such as team_connection.py in a folder C:/work/connections, write these lines in warehouse.py in place of conn = example_database: they put that folder on Python's path, then import it.

import sys

sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
from team_connection import conn

That is all, when your connection's query gives back a pandas DataFrame whose columns are named as the SELECT names them, and its execute runs Hive that changes something. Whatever conn.execute gives back, execute drops it. query and execute below it pass each Hive string on. In this page, query and execute are always the two functions in warehouse.py; conn.query and conn.execute are your connection's.

If your connection gives rows some other way, as each row a dict or a tuple, change query to turn them into a DataFrame. A dict, such as {"run_id": 97, "job_id": 3}, gives its column names from its keys:

def query(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    return pd.DataFrame(conn.query(hive))

A database cursor gives each row as a tuple, and the column names apart, in cursor.description. Pass those names on, rather than typing them yourself, since each Statement has its own columns:

def query(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    cursor = conn.cursor()  # your connection's cursor, from its database library
    cursor.execute(hive)
    names = [column[0] for column in cursor.description]
    return pd.DataFrame(cursor.fetchall(), columns=names)

def execute(hive: str) -> None:
    conn.cursor().execute(hive)  # the same connection: nothing comes back to read

hive: str, -> pd.DataFrame and -> None are type hints: they say a Hive string goes in, and that a DataFrame comes out of query and nothing out of execute. Python doesn't check them.

Both functions use the one conn. Most connections keep one session, so a Session setting, a Hive SET line that holds for the rest of the session, sent through execute holds for what is sent after it, through either function; if yours opens a new session for each call, keep one open in conn yourself. And let each raise when the warehouse reports a failure. If your connection gives back an error rather than raising one, raise it yourself, such as raise RuntimeError(answer.error): the Toolbox knows a step failed only when your connection raises.

Done when warehouse.py sets conn to your connection, and query gives back a DataFrame. Then step 9.

9. Try your query

Goal: proof that query gives back rows. Every notebook of the Project imports the two from warehouse.py:

from warehouse import query, execute

Try it on Hive that reads no table. A working query gives back a pandas DataFrame, one column for each name after SELECT:

one = query("SELECT 1 AS one")  # -> DataFrame
one

Python shows

one

1




From Build and run a first statement on, the Toolbox writes the Hive for you, and run_query hands it to your query.

Done when one shows a column named one holding 1. Then step 10.

10. See how a notebook deeper in the Project finds the Toolbox

Goal: know why each notebook the Project actions make starts as it does. Python looks for what a notebook imports in the folder the notebook runs in, so a notebook in the Project's folder finds sqlglot_composer, warehouse.py and references/ there. A Table notebook sits two folders down, in tables/<db>__<table>/, so its first code cell, under a comment line, starts with three lines, two imports and one that puts the Project's folder on Python's list of places to look, before its own imports. This is the cell as the saved_table Template, the shape a new Table notebook is made from, writes it:

# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import query

pathlib.Path.cwd() is the folder the notebook runs in, and .parents[1] the folder two above it: the Project's. An admin notebook, one folder down, starts with .parent in its place, and a Workflow's, three down, with .parents[2]. project.new_table and project.start write these lines into each notebook they make.

Done when you can say which line puts the Project's folder on Python's list. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

The Project's folders are there, and project.check() finds nothing wrong with them: every Table folder, Table reference and Building block the registry lists is there, and none is there that it doesn't list.

sorted(path.name for path in Path(".").iterdir() if path.is_dir())

Python shows
['admin', 'building_blocks', 'references', 'tables']

In your own Project the list holds sqlglot_composer too, the Toolbox you copied in, and __pycache__/, where Python keeps warehouse.py ready to import once a notebook has imported it; this page's folder has neither.

project.check()

Python shows
Every Table folder, Table reference and Building block is registered, and each one registered is there.

Your query gives back a pandas DataFrame, with the column named as the Hive names it:

import pandas as pd
isinstance(one, pd.DataFrame)

Python shows
True

list(one.columns)

Python shows
['one']

Before you go on, check that:

the Project's folder holds admin, building_blocks, references and tables;
project.check() says every Table folder, Table reference and Building block is registered;
query("SELECT 1 AS one") gives back a DataFrame whose one column is named one.


Common mistakes

A query that gives back the wrong thing

Many APIs give each row as a tuple, without the column names. A query that hands those rows to pd.DataFrame without the names gives back a DataFrame whose columns are numbered 0, 1 and 2. This page has no API, so a stand-in plays yours here, giving each row as a tuple:

def query_api(hive: str) -> list[tuple[object, ...]]:
    answer = query(hive)
    return [tuple(row) for row in answer.itertuples(index=False)]
def query_without_column_names(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    return pd.DataFrame(query_api(hive))
query_without_column_names("SELECT 1 AS one, 2 AS two, 3 AS three")

Python shows

012

123




Nothing stops it, but no column has its name: reading one by name, such as "one", stops with a KeyError. Pass pd.DataFrame the column names your connection gives, as step 8, Set your connection, shows. A query that gives back the rows as a plain list, without pd.DataFrame, is refused the first time run_query gets them back.

Starting the Project again

project.start() run again in the Project's folder writes over nothing. It adds only what start() makes and the Project lacks, such as an admin notebook deleted by mistake, and says so:

project.start()

Python shows
This folder is a Project already, and lacks nothing start() makes.

Run it in a folder inside the Project, such as tables/, and it refuses, so no Project is ever started inside another. To work on the Project, open a notebook in it: there is nothing to start again.

Making a file by hand

A Table reference or Table folder you make in your file explorer isn't in the registry, so nothing logs it, and project.check() reports it. Make each with its Project action, as the file map in step 6 says; Keep the Project in order shows how to adopt one made by hand.

Next

Write the Table references of the tables you read, many at once: Add your tables' references.
Build a Statement on one of them, read its Hive and run it: Build and run a first statement.
See a Project filled in: the Starter Example project, in the folder example_projects/starter, is much like the Project the Getting started how-tos build; its README says how the two differ.


Toolbox names used: project.start, project.check




2. Add your tables' references Getting started

Goal

Get every column of the tables you read, each name and its Hive type, into your Project without typing them. project.add_references asks each table itself and writes its Table reference, a small .py file describing the table, into the Project's references/ folder. You fill in what it can't know, add Aliases, your own names for its columns and conditions, and check the file against the table. Then from references import ops gives each table of the Database ops, the group of tables whose names start with ops., by its name, and each column at a dot after it.

When you'd use it

If you read a table at work for the first time, or your notebooks start reading another, write its Table reference as the steps below do. A Statement, a query the Toolbox builds, reads a table through its Table reference, so every table you read needs one, and a table with forty columns is forty chances to mistype a name or a type. Write them many at once, and every notebook of the Project imports them from references/.
If the table doesn't exist yet, such as a Saved table (a table of your own, which your Statements write into) you are about to create, there is nothing to ask: Save a table writes its file for you to fill in, and Describe a table by hand shows the file written in full.


Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project whose references/ folder the steps below fill. Start a Project left a Project with nothing in references/ yet. This page has none, so it starts one; in your own Project, which is started already, leave project.start() out. The star import brings every Toolbox name, such as check_key and equals; the project module is imported by its name, since the star leaves it out:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
project.start()

Python shows
Started a Project in the folder you're working in: admin/, references/, building_blocks/,
tables/ and warehouse.py.
Next: set conn, your connection, in warehouse.py.

It writes 22 files

warehouse.py

from warehouse import query, execute

Every step here passes that query; execute waits for a step that changes a table. Here it runs on the Example database, the made-up tables that ship inside the Toolbox, as warehouse.py holds it; at work it is your own, and you name your own tables.

Done when from warehouse import query, execute runs without an error. Then step 2.

2. See what a table says about itself

Goal: know where a Table reference's facts come from. A table in the warehouse can describe itself. The Hive command DESCRIBE lists its columns and their types, and SHOW PARTITIONS lists the Partitions it holds. Neither reads a single row, so both are quick on the biggest table. Here is what DESCRIBE says of ops.run_alerts, sent straight through your query:

described = query("DESCRIBE ops.run_alerts")  # -> DataFrame
described

Python shows

col_namedata_typecomment

alert_idbigint

run_idbigint

severitystringlow / high

dtstring

NULLNULL

# Partition InformationNULLNULL

# col_namedata_typecomment

dtstring




The first four rows are its columns. The fifth is a blank line DESCRIBE leaves before its next section: its name is empty, and its other two cells are missing, NULL in Hive, which pandas prints as NaN. The rows under # Partition Information say which columns split the table into Partitions, the pieces the warehouse stores it in: here dt alone, one Partition for each value of dt.

Done when described lists alert_id, run_id, severity and dt. Then step 3.

3. Write your tables' Table references

Goal: a Table reference for each table you read. project.add_references takes a list of tables. For each, it sends those two commands through your query, turns what comes back into a Table reference file in references/, and adds it to the registry, admin/registry.csv, the list of what each Project action made; then it logs what it did in the Admin log, the record of each Project action. Run it from the references Workflow's notebook, admin/workflows/references/references.ipynb, which runs this call on the names in its names cell, or from any notebook in the Project's folder, as here:

project.add_references(["ops.jobs", "ops.job_runs", "ops.run_alerts"], query=query)

Python shows
Wrote references/ops__jobs.py, references/ops__job_runs.py and references/ops__run_alerts.py: fill in their TODO lines.
Wrote references/ops.py: from references import ops gives ops.job_runs, ops.jobs and ops.run_alerts.
Restart the kernel of a notebook that imported references, to see a table added since.

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

Each file is named after the database and the table joined by two underscores, ops.jobs giving ops__jobs.py, since a dot can't be in a name Python imports. The variable in it is named after the table alone, jobs.

4. Read a Table reference it wrote

Goal: know what the file holds and what it leaves to you. Open references/ops__job_runs.py in your editor. It imports Table, then makes one variable, job_runs:

columns= holds every column and its type;
partitioned_by=["dt"] comes from DESCRIBE's # Partition Information rows;
three lines say TODO: the line at the top that says what one row is, key=None and does_not_add_up=[]. The table can't tell anyone those, so the file leaves them to you;
descriptions= holds what each column holds, in words, from its comment in the warehouse, such as status's SUCCESS / FAILED / TEST, NULL while running;
under the Aliases heading at the end, commented-out examples show how to add your own names for the table's columns and conditions.


Done when you have found the file's three TODO lines. Then step 5.

5. Fill in the TODOs

Goal: a Table reference that says what only you know. Open each file in your editor and replace its three TODO lines. This is references/ops__job_runs.py with them filled in, and two Aliases of your own at the end:

The file references/ops__job_runs.py, after you fill it in

"""ops.job_runs - one row per run of a job, on the day it ran."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table, equals

job_runs = Table(
    "ops.job_runs",
    columns={
        "run_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "status": "string",
        "duration_mins": "int",
        "avg_retry_secs": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["run_id"],
    does_not_add_up=["avg_retry_secs"],
    descriptions={
        "status": "SUCCESS / FAILED / TEST, NULL while running",
    },
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = job_runs.status
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(job_runs.status, <value>)

minutes = job_runs.duration_mins
failed = equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED")

The first line says, in your own words, what one row is.
key= lists the columns that pick out one row, the table's Key. Every run has its own job_runs.run_id, so it is key=["run_id"]. Ask whoever owns the table if you aren't sure: check_key, in step 9, checks it. A join uses the Key to warn you when it would repeat rows.
does_not_add_up= lists the columns that are averages, ratios or counts of different values. job_runs.avg_retry_secs is each run's average wait between retries: adding up two runs' averages gives a number that means nothing, so the Toolbox refuses to add it up once it is listed here.
An Alias is your own name, written by hand. minutes is a shorter name for job_runs.duration_mins, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame a query gives back, still call the column duration_mins. failed names a condition on this table alone. Add equals to the import at the top, beside Table, since failed calls it.
descriptions= is yours too: add what a column holds where the warehouse has no comment, as "duration_mins": "how long the run took, in minutes".


The other two the same way. A job is one row of ops.jobs, and nothing in it is an average, so its does_not_add_up=[] stays empty; the TODO comments go:

The file references/ops__jobs.py, after you fill it in

A run can raise several alerts, so the Key of ops.run_alerts is its alert_id, not its run_id. It gets an Alias too, high, for its high alerts:

Done when no file holds a TODO line. Then step 6.

6. Reach a table and its columns

Goal: each table and column by name, in any notebook. In any notebook of the Project, one line imports the Database module, and with it every table of the Database ops:

from references import ops

Each table is at a dot after ops, and each of its columns at a dot after the table. In a notebook, tab completion lists them at each dot:

ops.job_runs  # -> Table

Python shows
Table('ops.job_runs', columns: run_id, job_id, status, duration_mins, avg_retry_secs, dt)

ops.job_runs.status  # -> Column

Python shows
job_runs.status

A Column is what the Toolbox's functions take, as you'll see in Build and run a first statement. In an f-string, a Column gives the name the Hive knows it by, and a Table what follows FROM, for the rare Hive you write by hand (Hand-written Hive with your Table references shows when):

f"WHERE {ops.job_runs.status} = 'FAILED'"  # -> str

Python shows
"WHERE job_runs.status = 'FAILED'"

f"FROM {ops.job_runs}"  # -> str

Python shows
'FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs'

Done when ops.job_runs.status shows job_runs.status. Then step 7.

7. Import an Alias from its table's file

Goal: your own names in a notebook. ops gives each table, but not your Aliases: they are names in the table's own file. Import them from it, by its name in references/, beside the table:

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs, failed, minutes
minutes  # -> Column

Python shows
job_runs.duration_mins

failed  # -> Condition

Python shows
job_runs.status = 'FAILED'

job_runs is the same Table as ops.job_runs. Import a table from its own file when you use its Aliases, as the Building blocks and Table notebooks after this how-to do; otherwise from references import ops is all a notebook needs.

Done when minutes shows job_runs.duration_mins. Then step 8.

8. Check each file against its table

Goal: a file that still says what the table holds. check_table_reference asks the table for its columns again and compares them with the file. Run it after you edit a Table reference, and whenever the table may have changed:

check_table_reference(ops.job_runs, query=query)  # -> Verdict

Python shows
ops.job_runs matches its Table reference.

It gives back a Verdict: what the check found, which prints as the sentence above, and whose .ok is True when it found nothing wrong. When something differs, it lists each problem with the line to change in the file. It leaves the file for you to edit.

Done when it says ops.job_runs matches its Table reference. Then step 9.

9. Check the Key you wrote

Goal: proof that the Key names one row. The warehouse never checks a Key: nothing stops two rows sharing one run_id. check_key counts the rows for each Key, and lists any Key that repeats. ops.job_runs is partitioned, so where= says which Partitions to check, here those whose dt is 2026-09-24; a table that isn't partitioned, such as ops.jobs, is checked whole:

check_key(ops.job_runs, query=query,
          where=equals(ops.job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"))  # -> Verdict

Python shows
ops.job_runs: the key (run_id) holds where job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'.

check_key(ops.jobs, query=query)  # -> Verdict

Python shows
ops.jobs: the key (job_id) holds.

reads_all_partitions=True in place of where= checks the whole of a partitioned table.

Done when each check says its key holds. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

The three Table reference files and the Database module are in references/, and the registry lists each Table reference:

from pathlib import Path
sorted(path.name for path in Path("references").glob("ops*.py"))

Python shows
['ops.py', 'ops__job_runs.py', 'ops__jobs.py', 'ops__run_alerts.py']

project.check()

Python shows
Every Table folder, Table reference and Building block is registered, and each one registered is there.

Each file matches its table:

for table in (ops.jobs, ops.job_runs, ops.run_alerts):
    print(check_table_reference(table, query=query))

Python prints
ops.jobs matches its Table reference.
ops.job_runs matches its Table reference.
ops.run_alerts matches its Table reference.

Before you go on, check that:

references/ holds ops.py, ops__job_runs.py, ops__jobs.py and ops__run_alerts.py;
project.check() says every Table reference is registered;
check_table_reference says each table matches its Table reference;
check_key says each Key holds.


Common mistakes

A column name the table doesn't have

A Table reference is there so you reach each column by name, never typing it as text. Use tab completion at the dot to pick the name. A misspelt column stops the line it is on, and lists the columns the table has:

ops.job_runs.stauts

Python stops with this message
AttributeError:
job_runs has no column 'stauts'. Did you mean 'status'? Its columns are: run_id, job_id, status, duration_mins, avg_retry_secs, dt.

A misspelt table stops the same way, and lists the tables of the Database:

ops.job_run

Python stops with this message
AttributeError:
ops has no table 'job_run'. Did you mean 'job_runs'? Its tables are: job_runs, jobs, run_alerts.

Reaching an Alias through ops

An Alias isn't a column of the table, so ops can't give it:

ops.job_runs.failed

Python stops with this message
AttributeError:
job_runs has no column 'failed'. Its columns are: run_id, job_id, status, duration_mins, avg_retry_secs, dt.

Import it from the table's file: from references.ops__job_runs import failed.

Adding a table whose Table reference is already there

project.add_references writes over no file: once written, the file is yours, and writing it again would lose the Key and the notes you added. A list that names a table already in references/ writes the others, skips that one, and then stops, naming it:

project.add_references(["ops.jobs"], query=query)

Python stops with this message
SkippedTables:
  What happened:  add_references skipped 1 of 1 tables: ops.jobs (references/ops__jobs.py is there already).
  Why it matters: Each table is written or skipped on its own, so one name that is wrong or already there never stops the rest.
  Usual fix:      Fix each name skipped as wrong or whose DESCRIBE failed, then run add_references again with those names alone; a table whose file is there already needs nothing. Each is in admin/admin_log.md.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

It writes admin/admin_log.md

# Admin log

Each Admin statement, Unchecked statement, Statement holding an Unchecked expression or Unchecked derived table, and Project action the Toolbox has run, oldest first. The Toolbox only ever adds to this file. Its Unchecked statements, expressions and derived tables are the list of what the Toolbox should learn to write next.

3. Build and run a first statement Getting started

Goal

Build a Statement from your Table references, read the Hive the Toolbox writes for it, run it through your query, and get the rows back as a pandas DataFrame. A Statement is one query, made of the Toolbox's Clause functions; a Table reference is the file that describes one table, its columns, its Partitions and its Key; Hive is the SQL your warehouse runs. On the way you meet the chain of types every Statement is built along, from a Table to a DataFrame, and the Conditions WHERE takes to keep only the rows you want.

When you'd use it

If you have a question for a table, build a Statement for it, as the steps below do.
If someone who doesn't use Python needs the query, print its Hive with show_hive (step 7) and paste it into another program, such as Hue, Beeline or DBeaver.
If a result looks wrong, read the Statement's Hive (step 6): it shows exactly what the warehouse was asked.
If the question counts per group or joins tables, do this how-to first, then Count, join and stay safe.


Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project whose Table references the steps below read. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout.

Add your tables' references left the Project with the Table references of ops.jobs, ops.job_runs and ops.run_alerts. This page has no Project, so it makes one with the same three; in your own Project, leave these calls out. Their TODO lines change no Statement here, so this page leaves them as project.add_references writes them:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
project.start()

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

Every notebook of the Project starts with three imports: every Toolbox name, your query and execute, and the tables of the Database ops. A Database is a named group of tables, the first part of a table's name, ops in ops.jobs:

from references import ops

Done when from references import ops runs without an error. Then step 2.

2. Name the table and a column

Goal: the first two links of the chain of types. A Statement is built along a chain of types, each made by a call on the one before:

ops.job_runs is a Table: a table, its columns, its Partitions and its Key;
ops.job_runs.status is a Column: one of its columns;
equals(...), and every test like it, gives a Condition: a test of each row;
SELECT(...), FROM(...), WHERE(...) and the other Clause functions each give a Clause: one part of a query;
statement(...) gives a Statement: its Clauses, checked together as one query;
to_hive(...) gives a str: the Hive, the query's text;
run_query(..., query) gives a DataFrame: the rows your query gives back, in pandas.


Each step below makes one, and its comment, such as # -> Table, says which. The question the steps answer: which runs failed on 2026-09-23 and 2026-09-24? Give the table a short name first, then read one of its Columns:

job_runs = ops.job_runs  # -> Table
job_runs.status  # -> Column

Python shows
job_runs.status

Done when job_runs.status shows job_runs.status. Then step 3.

3. Make a Condition

Goal: a test that keeps the failed runs. A Condition tests a Column, row by row:

is_failed = equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED")  # -> Condition
is_failed

Python shows
job_runs.status = 'FAILED'

Done when is_failed shows job_runs.status = 'FAILED'. Then step 4.

4. Make a WHERE Clause

Goal: a Clause that keeps only the rows you want. ops.job_runs is partitioned by dt: the warehouse stores it a Partition at a time, one Partition for each value of dt. So every Statement that reads it filters dt, here with between, which keeps the values from the first to the last, both included. WHERE keeps a row only if every Condition in it holds:

where = WHERE(is_failed, between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"))  # -> Clause
where

Python shows
<WHERE clause - put it in statement(...)>

Done when where shows <WHERE clause - put it in statement(...)>. Then step 5.

5. Put the Clauses into a Statement

Goal: one query, checked as a whole. A Clause on its own is only a part. statement puts the Clauses together in SQL's order, SELECT the columns you want, FROM a table, then WHERE, and checks them as one query:

failed = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id, job_runs.job_id, job_runs.dt),
    FROM(job_runs),
    where,
)
failed

Python shows
<Statement reading ops.job_runs - print(to_hive(s)) shows its Hive>

Nothing has run yet: failed holds the Statement, ready to become Hive. Most of the time you write the Clauses straight into statement(...), as every Statement after this one does; it is the same Statement.

Done when failed shows <Statement reading ops.job_runs - ...>. Then step 6.

6. Read its Hive

Goal: see exactly what the warehouse will be asked. to_hive gives the Hive as text, a str, exactly what run_query sends. print shows it as it is, one clause to a line:

hive = to_hive(failed)  # -> str
print(hive)

Python prints
SELECT
  job_runs.run_id,
  job_runs.job_id,
  job_runs.dt
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.status = 'FAILED' AND job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'

FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs reads the table and calls it job_runs, which is why every column in the Hive starts with job_runs., just as it does in your Python. The two Conditions are joined with AND.

Done when the Hive's WHERE holds both Conditions, joined with AND. Then step 7.

7. Print it ready to paste

Goal: Hive you can paste into Hue, Beeline or DBeaver. show_hive prints the same Hive with a ; at the end, which those programs need to tell one query from the next. It gives the text back too, so text keeps it to save or use again:

text = show_hive(failed)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_runs.run_id,
  job_runs.job_id,
  job_runs.dt
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.status = 'FAILED' AND job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24';

In a notebook you can also write show_hive(failed) on a line of its own: it prints the Hive once, and the text it gives back isn't shown a second time. Given several Statements, it prints them all, ready to paste as one script, each headed by a -- comment line: its number, such as -- 1 of 2, then the name of the variable it is in, when it is in one, such as -- 1 of 2: failed.

Done when the printed Hive ends with ;. Then step 8.

8. Run it

Goal: the rows, as a DataFrame. run_query turns the Statement into Hive, hands it to your query, and gives back the DataFrame query returns. Call run_query for a Statement that reads rows:

result = run_query(failed, query)  # -> DataFrame
result

Python shows

run_idjob_iddt

9732026-09-23

10222026-09-24




The column names are the Statement's. From here it is pandas, such as result["run_id"]. A Statement that writes a table goes to your execute instead, with run_execute, which gives back nothing, as Save a table shows.

Done when the result holds runs 97 and 102. Then step 9.

9. Keep only the rows you want

Goal: the Conditions for any question you ask. WHERE takes any number of Conditions. Each function below makes one:

equals and not_equals test one value;
at_least (>=), at_most (<=), more_than (>) and less_than (<) compare with a number, and between keeps a range, both ends included;
is_in and is_not_in test against a Python list;
contains and starts_with test text;
is_null and is_not_null find missing values, NULL;
any_of keeps a row where at least one of its Conditions holds, SQL's OR, and all_of groups Conditions that must all hold, for use inside any_of.


The runs on 2026-09-24 of jobs 1 and 3 that took 20 minutes or more, each Condition on one line:

long_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id, job_runs.job_id, job_runs.duration_mins),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"),
          is_in(job_runs.job_id, [1, 3]),
          at_least(job_runs.duration_mins, 20)),
)
text = show_hive(long_runs)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_runs.run_id,
  job_runs.job_id,
  job_runs.duration_mins
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
  AND job_runs.job_id IN (1, 3)
  AND job_runs.duration_mins >= 20;

run_query(long_runs, query)

Python shows

The runs that failed or took 30 minutes or more, with any_of. The Hive puts brackets around the OR, so the filter on dt still holds for every row kept:

to_check = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id, job_runs.status, job_runs.duration_mins),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"),
          any_of(equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"), at_least(job_runs.duration_mins, 30))),
)

still_running = statement(  # -> Statement

10. Name a column in the result

minutes_named = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id, AS(job_runs.duration_mins, "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"), at_least(job_runs.duration_mins, 30)),
)
run_query(minutes_named, query)

11. Select every column

Goal: every column of a table, named one by one. all_columns gives a Table's Columns as a Python list, in the table's order, and SELECT takes a list as well as single Columns. Hive's SELECT * would ask for whatever the table holds on the day it runs, so a column added later would quietly change your result; a list of names keeps your result the same:

every_column = all_columns(job_runs)  # -> list[Column]
every_column

Python shows
[job_runs.run_id, job_runs.job_id, job_runs.status, job_runs.duration_mins, job_runs.avg_retry_secs, job_runs.dt]

Done when every_column lists the table's six Columns. Then step 12.

12. Select columns by their names

Goal: Columns chosen by names you hold as text, from a list in your settings or another table's columns. Python's own getattr takes each Column by its name:

wanted = ["run_id", "status", "duration_mins"]
chosen = [getattr(job_runs, name) for name in wanted]  # -> list[Column]
chosen_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(chosen),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23")),
)
run_query(chosen_runs, query)

13. Take a first look at a table

Goal: a quick look at a big table. first_look builds a Statement that reads every column and the first 20 rows the warehouse comes to: LIMIT 20 stops the read early. Which 20 a big table gives is up to the warehouse:

text = show_hive(first_look(job_runs))

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_runs.run_id,
  job_runs.job_id,
  job_runs.status,
  job_runs.duration_mins,

Done when the Hive ends with LIMIT 20;. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

Check a Statement's rows against the same Conditions worked out in pandas, on every row of the Partitions it reads. Here, to_check: the runs that failed or took 30 minutes or more.

every_run = run_query(statement(  # -> DataFrame
    SELECT(all_columns(job_runs)),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
), query)
in_pandas = every_run[(every_run["status"] == "FAILED")
                      | (every_run["duration_mins"] >= 30)]
sorted(in_pandas["run_id"]) == sorted(run_query(to_check, query)["run_id"])

Python shows
True

Both lists are sorted first: without ORDER_BY, the warehouse may give the rows in any order. At work, run this check on a Partition small enough to look at, and look at a few of the rows left out too. Before you go on, check that:

print(to_hive(failed)) shows a WHERE that filters dt;
run_query(failed, query) gives runs 97 and 102;
the comparison with pandas above shows True.


Common mistakes

A Statement that doesn't filter a partition column

A Statement that filters none of a partitioned table's partition columns would read every Partition the table holds. The Toolbox refuses it as you build it, before anything runs:

every_failed_run = statement(
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED")),
)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  FROM(job_runs) reads ops.job_runs, but filters none of its partition columns: dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would read every Partition the table holds, which can stall the cluster for everyone.
  Usual fix:      Filter one of them in WHERE, as in WHERE(equals(job_runs.dt, ...)), or with is_in, between or last_n_days.
  Opt-out:        FROM(job_runs, reads_all_partitions=True)

This is a Load limit: a check that protects the cluster, not the answer. Filter the partition column as the message says, as failed does. The Opt-out, reads_all_partitions=True, reads every Partition: keep it for a small table you truly need whole. Count, join and stay safe shows the other Load limits.

Giving run_query the Hive text

run_query takes the Statement and makes the Hive itself, so it can check it first. Given the text, it stops:

run_query(hive, query)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  run_query was given "SELECT\n  job_runs.run_id,\n  job_runs.job_id,\n  job_runs.dt\nFROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs\nWHERE\n  job_runs.status = 'FAILED' AND job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'", which isn't a Statement.
  Why it matters: run_query writes the Hive of one Statement made by statement(...) and sends it.
  Usual fix:      Pass the Statement itself, made by statement(...): run_query writes its Hive for you.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Pass the Statement itself: run_query(failed, query). Your query works the other way round: it takes Hive text, a str.

Comparing with None

equals(job_runs.status, None) looks like the way to find run 98, but in SQL nothing equals NULL, not even NULL, so it would keep no rows at all. The Toolbox stops it with a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result:

equals(job_runs.status, None)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  equals(job_runs.status, None) compares with None.
  Why it matters: In SQL nothing is equal to NULL, not even NULL, so this would match no rows and quietly give an empty or wrong result.
  Usual fix:      Use is_null(column) or is_not_null(column) to find missing values.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write is_null(job_runs.status), as still_running does. A None in a list given to is_in stops the same way.

Expecting not_equals to keep NULL

pandas' != keeps a row whose value is missing; SQL's <>, which not_equals writes, drops it. Leaving out the TEST runs on 2026-09-23 also leaves out run 98, still running:

not_test = statement(
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id, job_runs.status),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23"), not_equals(job_runs.status, "TEST")),
)
run_query(not_test, query)

Python shows

run_idstatus

95SUCCESS

96SUCCESS

97FAILED




Nothing stops this, since the Toolbox can't know which columns may be NULL. To keep run 98, ask for the NULL rows too: any_of(not_equals(job_runs.status, "TEST"), is_null(job_runs.status)).

A value of the wrong type

job_runs.job_id holds numbers. Comparing it with text, as if "3" were 3, would leave the warehouse to convert one into the other, which can quietly match nothing. The Toolbox stops it:

equals(job_runs.job_id, "3")

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  equals(job_runs.job_id, ...) compares job_runs.job_id, a bigint column, with '3'.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would convert one side to the other's type, so the comparison could quietly match nothing or the wrong rows, or stop with an error.
  Usual fix:      Pass a value of the column's own type, such as 42.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write the value in the column's type: equals(job_runs.job_id, 3).

4. Count, join and stay safe Getting started

Goal

Answer "how many, and how much, per something", such as runs per job or minutes per team, and put two tables' columns side by side without counting any row twice. GROUP_BY makes one row per group, count_rows and sum_of work out its numbers, HAVING keeps some groups, and ORDER_BY with LIMIT keeps the top N. JOIN and LEFT_JOIN bring in another table. A Statement is one query, built from the Toolbox's Clause functions. On the way you meet the three ways the Toolbox stops a Statement, or warns about it, as you build it: a Guard, a Warning and a Load limit, and when each one's opt-out is right.

When you'd use it

If the answer is a table of totals rather than a list of rows, such as a daily count for a dashboard, the busiest jobs or each team's minutes, group and count as the steps below do. The warehouse does the counting, so only the totals come back to your notebook.
If you need another table's columns beside each row, join it, as steps 8 to 11 do.
If a Statement stops with a message, or builds with a Warning, read the message before you change anything, as steps 12 to 17 show.


Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project with the three Table references, the files that describe each table, filled in. Build and run a first statement built its Statements on the Project as Add your tables' references left it: three Table references filled in, and Aliases in two of them. This page has no Project, so it makes one with the same three files; in your own Project, leave these calls out:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
project.start()

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

Done when references/ holds ops__region_budgets.py and ops__team_notes.py, filled in as above. Then step 3.

3. Name the tables

Goal: short names that keep the Statements below easy to read. from references import ops gives every table, and the Aliases failed and minutes, your own names for a condition and a column, come from their table's own file:

from references import ops
from references.ops__job_runs import failed, minutes
jobs = ops.jobs  # -> Table
job_runs = ops.job_runs  # -> Table
run_alerts = ops.run_alerts  # -> Table

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 4.

4. Count every row

Goal: one total over every row read. count_rows() counts rows, and sum_of adds up a Column, here minutes, the Alias of job_runs.duration_mins. Each gives a Column, a calculation, and every calculation in SELECT needs a name for its column in the result, which AS gives:

all_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "runs"), AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(all_runs)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  COUNT(*) AS runs,
  SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24';

run_query(all_runs, query)

Python shows

runsminutes

9180




Without GROUP_BY, all the rows read are one group: one row comes back.

Done when the result is one row: 9 runs, 180 minutes. Then step 5.

5. Make one row per job

Goal: the numbers of each job. GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id) makes one group per job. SELECT then holds the columns you group by, and calculations over each group, such as min_of and max_of. count_rows takes where=, a Condition, to count only the rows where it holds while the group keeps every row: here the Alias failed:

per_job = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(
        job_runs.job_id,
        AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
        AS(count_rows(where=failed), "failed_runs"),
        AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes"),
        AS(max_of(minutes), "longest"),
    ),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
)
text = show_hive(per_job)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_runs.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS runs,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END) AS failed_runs,
  SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes,
  MAX(job_runs.duration_mins) AS longest
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_runs.job_id;

run_query(per_job, query)

Python shows

job_idrunsfailed_runsminuteslongest

1406740

2315320

3216030




COUNT(CASE WHEN ... THEN 1 END) counts the rows where the Condition holds. Putting failed in WHERE instead would drop the other runs before counting, so "runs" would count only failed runs too.

Done when the result has one row per job, jobs 1, 2 and 3. Then step 6.

6. Keep some groups

Goal: only the groups whose numbers pass a test. WHERE picks rows before they are counted, so it can't test a count. HAVING tests each group after GROUP_BY has counted it, and repeats the calculation itself, count_rows(): SQL works HAVING out before SELECT has named anything. Here, the jobs with 3 runs or more:

busy_jobs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
    HAVING(at_least(count_rows(), 3)),
)
run_query(busy_jobs, query)

Python shows

job_idruns

14

23




Done when the result holds jobs 1 and 2 alone. Then step 7.

7. Keep the top N

Goal: the few groups with the biggest numbers. ORDER_BY sorts the result, smallest first, or largest first with descending, and LIMIT keeps the first rows. ORDER_BY is worked out after SELECT, so it sorts by the name. Here, the two jobs with the most minutes:

top_two = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
    ORDER_BY(descending("minutes")),
    LIMIT(2),
)
run_query(top_two, query)

Python shows

job_idminutes

167

360




Done when the result holds jobs 1 and 3, most minutes first. Then step 8.

8. Join on the other table's Key

Goal: another table's columns beside each row, with no row repeated. JOIN adds another table's columns to each row, matched by its ON= Condition, a test of each row. Each run names one job, and jobs.job_id is the Key of ops.jobs, the columns that name exactly one of its rows, so each run meets exactly one row of it: nothing is repeated, and counting per team after the join is safe:

per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(jobs.team, AS(count_rows(), "runs"), AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    JOIN(jobs, ON=equals(jobs.job_id, job_runs.job_id)),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(jobs.team),
)
text = show_hive(per_team)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  COUNT(*) AS runs,
  SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = job_runs.job_id
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  jobs.team;

run_query(per_team, query)

Python shows

teamrunsminutes

data467

finance5113




Nine runs, and 180 minutes, as before the join.

Done when the teams' runs add up to 9 and their minutes to 180. Then step 9.

9. Read the repeated-rows Warning

Goal: see a join that repeats rows, and the Warning that says so. A Warning lets a Statement through, but says why a number may come out wrong. Now the runs' alerts. run_alerts.run_id isn't the Key of ops.run_alerts: a run with two alerts matches two rows, so the run comes out twice, and its minutes are added twice. The Toolbox builds the Statement, since sometimes one row per match is what you want, but warns at the JOIN. run_alerts is partitioned too, so it gets its own filter on run_alerts.dt:

careless_total = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes"), AS(count_rows(), "alerts")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    JOIN(run_alerts, ON=equals(run_alerts.run_id, job_runs.run_id)),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"),
          between(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
)

It builds, with a Warning
RepeatedRowsWarning:
  What happened:  JOIN(run_alerts) matches on run_id, but a run_alerts row is told apart only by its Key (alert_id).
  Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching run_alerts row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
  Usual fix:      Group run_alerts first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(run_alerts, ON=..., many_matches=True)

run_query(careless_total, query)

Python shows

minutesalerts

2109




The Statement builds, with the repeated-rows Warning. In a notebook it shows under the cell, at your own line, and on this page under the step that builds it; it doesn't stop anything. It says:

What happened: JOIN(run_alerts) matches on run_id, but a run_alerts row is told apart only by its Key (alert_id).
Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching run_alerts row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
Usual fix: Group run_alerts first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that.
Opt-out: JOIN(run_alerts, ON=..., many_matches=True)


And so it is: the nine runs took 180 minutes, but here they come out as 210. Runs 97 and 103 are added twice, run 101 three times, and the four runs with no alert not at all, since JOIN keeps only rows that match.

Done when you have read the Warning's four parts, and seen 210 minutes where 180 is right. Then step 10.

10. Add up first, with a Derived table

Goal: the right totals, with the alerts joined. The Warning's usual fix: give ops.run_alerts one row per run before joining it. derived gives a Statement a name, so another Statement can read it like a table: a Derived table, which is a Table too. This one groups the alerts by run, and the Toolbox takes its GROUP_BY column as its Key. LEFT_JOIN keeps every run, those with no alert too, with NULL in the joined columns, and fill_null turns that NULL into 0:

alerts_per_run = derived("alerts_per_run", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(run_alerts.run_id, AS(count_rows(), "alerts")),
    FROM(run_alerts),
    WHERE(between(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(run_alerts.run_id),
))
fixed_total = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes"),
           AS(fill_null(sum_of(alerts_per_run.alerts), 0), "alerts")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    LEFT_JOIN(alerts_per_run, ON=equals(alerts_per_run.run_id, job_runs.run_id)),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(fixed_total)

Hive, ready to paste
WITH alerts_per_run AS (
  SELECT
    run_alerts.run_id,
    COUNT(*) AS alerts
  FROM ops.run_alerts AS run_alerts
  WHERE
    run_alerts.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    run_alerts.run_id
)
SELECT
  SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes,
  COALESCE(SUM(alerts_per_run.alerts), 0) AS alerts
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
LEFT JOIN alerts_per_run
  ON alerts_per_run.run_id = job_runs.run_id
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24';

run_query(fixed_total, query)

Python shows

minutesalerts

1809




No Warning, and the totals are right. The Derived table is written at the top of the Hive, as WITH alerts_per_run AS (...). Share Building blocks keeps one in a file of its own, for every notebook that needs it.

Done when the result is 180 minutes and 9 alerts, with no Warning. Then step 11.

11. Find the rows with no match

Goal: the rows of one table that nothing in the other matches. A row LEFT_JOIN keeps for having no match has NULL in every joined column, so is_null on one of them keeps just those rows. Here, the jobs that never ran on the two days, from the runs counted per job first, so each job meets at most one row:

runs_per_job = derived("runs_per_job", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
))
never_ran = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(jobs.job_id, jobs.job_name),
    FROM(jobs),
    LEFT_JOIN(runs_per_job, ON=equals(runs_per_job.job_id, jobs.job_id)),
    WHERE(is_null(runs_per_job.job_id)),
)
run_query(never_ran, query)

Python shows

job_idjob_name

4cache_warm




A plain JOIN would have left cache_warm out, with nothing to say it was missing.

Done when the result is job 4, cache_warm. Then step 12.

12. Know the three ways the Toolbox stops or warns

Goal: tell a Guard, a Warning and a Load limit apart. You have met two already. The three kinds, each checked as you build a Statement:

a Guard refuses a Statement that would silently give a wrong answer, such as a sum that counts some rows twice. It stops with a GuardRefused;
a Warning lets the Statement through, but says why a number may come out wrong, where the risky Statement is often the one you meant, as the repeated-rows Warning above;
a Load limit refuses a Statement that would cost the cluster or your notebook too much, though its answer would have been right. It stops with a LoadRefused.


Every message has the same four parts: what happened, why it matters, the usual fix, and the opt-out, a keyword to add to one of your calls, or "none" when there is no way around it. Steps 13 to 15 each meet one, on the two tables added in step 2.

Done when you can say which of the three stops with a GuardRefused, which with a LoadRefused, and which lets the Statement through. Then step 13.

13. A Load limit: read only the Partitions you need

Goal: a Statement that reads only the Partitions, the slices a table is stored in, that it needs. ops.region_budgets is partitioned by region, so a Statement that names no region would read every Partition:

statement(
    SELECT(ops.region_budgets.job_id, ops.region_budgets.budget_cents),
    FROM(ops.region_budgets),
)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  FROM(region_budgets) reads ops.region_budgets, but filters none of its partition columns: region.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would read every Partition the table holds, which can stall the cluster for everyone.
  Usual fix:      Filter one of them in WHERE, as in WHERE(equals(region_budgets.region, ...)), or with is_in, between or last_n_days.
  Opt-out:        FROM(region_budgets, reads_all_partitions=True)

The usual fix is the right one: name the regions to read, here both, with is_in. A region isn't a day: a partition column can be any column, and a Statement filters it as it filters any other:

budgets = ops.region_budgets  # -> Table
every_budget = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(budgets.job_id, budgets.region, budgets.budget_cents),
    FROM(budgets),
    WHERE(is_in(budgets.region, ["eu", "us"])),
)
run_query(every_budget, query)

Python shows

job_idregionbudget_cents

1eu150

2eu200

3us350

4eu50




The opt-out, reads_all_partitions=True on FROM, is right only for a small table you truly need whole.

Done when every_budget gives the four budgets. Then step 14.

14. A Guard: a condition on the LEFT_JOIN's table goes in ON=

Goal: a LEFT_JOIN that keeps the rows with no match. Every job, with its budget in eu, keeping the jobs that have none there. WHERE runs after the join, and a job with no eu budget has NULL in every column LEFT_JOIN brought in, so a Condition on them in WHERE would throw that job away again, and the LEFT_JOIN would quietly act as a JOIN. A Guard stops it:

statement(
    SELECT(jobs.job_name, budgets.budget_cents),
    FROM(jobs),
    LEFT_JOIN(budgets, ON=equals(budgets.job_id, jobs.job_id)),
    WHERE(equals(budgets.region, "eu")),
)

It builds, with a Warning
RepeatedRowsWarning:
  What happened:  LEFT_JOIN(region_budgets) matches on job_id, but a region_budgets row is told apart only by its Key (job_id, region).
  Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching region_budgets row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
  Usual fix:      Group region_budgets first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that.
  Opt-out:        LEFT_JOIN(region_budgets, ON=..., many_matches=True)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  WHERE has region_budgets.region = 'eu', a condition on region_budgets, which LEFT_JOIN brought in.
  Why it matters: LEFT_JOIN keeps rows with no match in region_budgets, but those rows have NULL there, so this WHERE throws them away again. The result is an ordinary JOIN.
  Usual fix:      Move the condition into LEFT_JOIN(region_budgets, ON=...), next to the join condition, so it only decides which rows match.
  Opt-out:        LEFT_JOIN(region_budgets, ON=..., keeps_only_matches=True)

Before the Guard refused it, it also warned at the LEFT_JOIN, since a Statement can get a Warning and a refusal both: matched on budgets.job_id alone, a job meets its budget in every region, and the region is the other column of the Key. The Guard's usual fix mends both: move the Condition into ON=, beside the join's own, with all_of. It filters the partition column there too, and the join now matches on the whole Key:

eu_budgets = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(jobs.job_name, budgets.budget_cents),
    FROM(jobs),
    LEFT_JOIN(budgets, ON=all_of(equals(budgets.job_id, jobs.job_id),
                                 equals(budgets.region, "eu"))),
)
run_query(eu_budgets, query)

Python shows

job_namebudget_cents

cache_warm50.0

invoice_sync200.0

nightly_load150.0

report_buildNULL




report_build is billed in us, so it has no eu budget, and is kept, with NaN, pandas' empty value; a column of whole numbers holding a NaN is made floats, so the budgets show as 50.0. The opt-out, keeps_only_matches=True, is almost never right: if you want only the jobs that have a match, JOIN says so plainly.

Done when eu_budgets keeps report_build, with NaN. Then step 15.

15. A Warning: a table with no Key

Goal: a join onto a table with no Key, its Warning answered. ops.team_notes has no Key, so the Toolbox can't know how many notes a team has, and warns at any join onto it. Each job, beside its team's notes:

notes = ops.team_notes  # -> Table
job_notes = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(jobs.job_name, notes.note),
    FROM(jobs),
    JOIN(notes, ON=equals(notes.team, jobs.team)),
)

It builds, with a Warning
RepeatedRowsWarning:
  What happened:  JOIN(team_notes) matches on team, but team_notes declares no Key, so the Toolbox can't tell whether one row matches or several.
  Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching team_notes row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
  Usual fix:      Group team_notes first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that. If you know each row matches one team_notes row at most, many_matches=True is right.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(team_notes, ON=..., many_matches=True)

run_query(job_notes, query)

Python shows
