invoice_syncinvoice_sync's bill comes in a day late

invoice_syncreport_build came over from data today

nightly_loadask ana before changing nightly_load

nightly_loadnightly_load runs long at the end of a month

report_buildinvoice_sync's bill comes in a day late

report_buildreport_build came over from data today




This time one row per matching note is the point, and nothing is added up over it, so the Warning's opt-out is right. many_matches=True, on the call the message names, says so to the Toolbox and to the next reader, and the Warning goes:

job_notes = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(jobs.job_name, notes.note),
    FROM(jobs),
    JOIN(notes, ON=equals(notes.team, jobs.team), many_matches=True),
)

Done when job_notes builds with no Warning. Then step 16.

16. Switch on the row limit

Goal: a cap on the rows a query brings back to your notebook. Two Load limits are always on: a partitioned table must have a partition column filtered, and ORDER_BY needs a LIMIT, as Common mistakes shows. A third is off until you switch it on, since only you know how much your notebook can take. set_load_limits(rows=...) adds a LIMIT to every query that has none of its own, and refuses a result that fills it, since it was then probably cut short. At work, set_load_limits(rows=100000), in the first cell of a notebook, is a good start. The Example database is tiny, so this page sets 5:

set_load_limits(rows=5)  # -> dict[str, int | None]

Python shows
{'rows': 5}

run_query(statement(
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
), query)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  run_query(...) got back 5 rows, exactly the automatic LIMIT set by set_load_limits(rows=...).
  Why it matters: The result was probably cut short, so anything computed from it would be missing rows.
  Usual fix:      Narrow the Statement with more WHERE, or read it in parts, such as a Partition at a time.
  Opt-out:        statement(..., returns_all_rows=True)

The usual fix is to ask for fewer rows. Its opt-out, returns_all_rows=True on statement(...), is right when you know the result fits in your notebook. set_load_limits() with nothing in it switches the limit off again:

set_load_limits()  # -> dict[str, int | None]

Python shows
{'rows': None}

Done when set_load_limits() shows {'rows': None}. Then step 17.

17. Know when an opt-out is right

Goal: an opt-out only where the message's reason doesn't apply. Each opt-out says "I know, and I mean it". Add one only when the message's "why it matters" doesn't apply to what you want:

many_matches=True on a JOIN or LEFT_JOIN: when one row per match is the point, such as listing each note beside its job, and nothing over it is added up.
reads_all_partitions=True on FROM or a join: for a small partitioned table you truly need every Partition of.
returns_all_rows=True on statement(...): when you know the result fits in your notebook.
adds_up=True on a sum: when a column the Toolbox takes for one that doesn't add up really does. For a column a Table reference lists in does_not_add_up=[...], fix the list instead.
sorts_everything=True on ORDER_BY: for a result you know is small.
keeps_only_matches=True on LEFT_JOIN: almost never; write JOIN.


When the opt-out would hide a mistake, make the fix instead.

Done when you can say the case each opt-out above is right for. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

A join that doesn't repeat rows keeps the totals of the table in FROM: the runs and minutes per team add up to those of every run, and fixed_total has them too:

totals = run_query(all_runs, query)  # -> DataFrame
teams = run_query(per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame
int(teams["runs"].sum()) == int(totals["runs"][0])

Python shows
True

int(teams["minutes"].sum()) == int(run_query(fixed_total, query)["minutes"][0]) == 180

Python shows
True

And the warehouse's counts per job match the same counts made in pandas from the rows:

every_run = run_query(statement(  # -> DataFrame
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, job_runs.duration_mins),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
), query)
from_hive = run_query(per_job, query)  # -> DataFrame
every_run.groupby("job_id")["duration_mins"].sum().to_dict() == dict(
    zip(from_hive["job_id"], from_hive["minutes"]))

Python shows
True

Before you go on, check that:

the teams' runs in per_team add up to the runs in all_runs, 9;
fixed_total gives 180 minutes, the total before any join;
the minutes per job from the warehouse match those worked out in pandas: the comparison shows True.


Common mistakes

A column that isn't grouped

Each row of the result is one group, so every column in SELECT must be in GROUP_BY or inside a calculation. Here job_runs.status is neither, and one job's runs have several statuses:

statement(
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, job_runs.status, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  SELECT has job_runs.status, which GROUP_BY leaves out.
  Why it matters: Each output row is one group, so a column that isn't grouped has no single value to show. Hive would refuse the Statement.
  Usual fix:      Add job_runs.status to GROUP_BY. To keep one whole row per group instead, such as each job's latest run, number the rows with row_number(...) inside derived(...), then keep number 1 with WHERE(equals(..., 1)); help(row_number) shows how.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Add it to GROUP_BY for one row per job and status. A calculation with no AS stops the same way: name it, as in AS(count_rows(), "runs").

Adding up a column that doesn't add up

job_runs.avg_retry_secs is listed in does_not_add_up= in its Table reference, so a total of it stops as you build it:

statement(
    SELECT(AS(sum_of(job_runs.avg_retry_secs), "wait")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  sum_of(job_runs.avg_retry_secs) adds up job_runs.avg_retry_secs, which is listed in does_not_add_up in its Table reference.
  Why it matters: Averages, ratios and distinct counts don't add up: the average of daily averages is not the weekly average, and a user seen on two days would be counted twice.
  Usual fix:      It could be an average, a ratio or a distinct count, so work it out again from the table it was made from. For an average or a ratio, add up its two parts and divide after your own GROUP_BY. For a distinct count, count again with count_distinct(...).
  Opt-out:        sum_of(job_runs.avg_retry_secs, adds_up=True)

List every average, ratio and count of different values in does_not_add_up=[...]: left empty, nothing stops the total, and it means nothing.

Sorting without a LIMIT

Sorting a whole result makes the warehouse put every row in order before any comes back, which is slow on a big table. A Load limit stops a sort with no LIMIT:

statement(
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
    ORDER_BY(descending("minutes")),
)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  ORDER_BY has no LIMIT.
  Why it matters: Sorting the whole result makes the warehouse put every row in order before any comes back, which is slow on a big result; pandas can sort the rows once you have them.
  Usual fix:      Sort in pandas after run_query(...), with df.sort_values(...). For a top N, add LIMIT(n).
  Opt-out:        ORDER_BY(..., sorts_everything=True), but not in a Statement you pass to derived(...), where the order of rows may not be kept anyway, so this refusal can't be switched off there.

For a top N, add LIMIT. To sort every row, sort in pandas once the result is back, with .sort_values(...).

Silencing the Warning instead of fixing the join

many_matches=True, pasted to make the repeated-rows Warning go away, leaves the total just as wrong, and now nothing says so:

silenced = statement(
    SELECT(AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    JOIN(run_alerts, ON=equals(run_alerts.run_id, job_runs.run_id), many_matches=True),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"),
          between(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
)
run_query(silenced, query)

Python shows

minutes

210




To add up, add up before you join, as fixed_total does.

Forgetting the joined table's partition filter

Each partitioned table a Statement reads needs a filter on one of its own partition columns, the joined one too:

statement(
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id, run_alerts.alert_id),
    FROM(job_runs),
    JOIN(run_alerts, ON=equals(run_alerts.run_id, job_runs.run_id), many_matches=True),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  JOIN(run_alerts, ON=...) reads ops.run_alerts, but filters none of its partition columns: dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would read every Partition the table holds, which can stall the cluster for everyone.
  Usual fix:      Filter one of them in WHERE, as in WHERE(equals(run_alerts.dt, ...)), or with is_in, between or last_n_days.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(run_alerts, ON=..., reads_all_partitions=True)

The opt-out on the wrong call

An opt-out goes on the call the message names, such as JOIN(notes, ON=..., many_matches=True). On another call, Python itself refuses the keyword, and the Warning stays:

statement(
    SELECT(jobs.job_name, notes.note),
    FROM(jobs),
    JOIN(notes, ON=equals(notes.team, jobs.team)),
    many_matches=True,
)

It builds, with a Warning
RepeatedRowsWarning:
  What happened:  JOIN(team_notes) matches on team, but team_notes declares no Key, so the Toolbox can't tell whether one row matches or several.
  Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching team_notes row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
  Usual fix:      Group team_notes first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that. If you know each row matches one team_notes row at most, many_matches=True is right.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(team_notes, ON=..., many_matches=True)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
statement() got an unexpected keyword argument 'many_matches'

Copy the opt-out from the message as it is written.

Next

Keep a Derived table such as alerts_per_run in a file of its own, and use it in every notebook that needs it: Share Building blocks.
Save a count like per_job as a table of your own, a Partition at a time: Save a table.
More joins, such as rows of one table with no match in another: Semi-, anti- and full joins.
The gallery's Worked examples of GROUP_BY, HAVING, JOIN, LEFT_JOIN and set_load_limits, and the traps beside their fixes: repeated rows, a LEFT_JOIN then WHERE and jobs that never ran.

5. Share Building blocks Getting started

Goal

Write a piece several Statements need once, and use it in each of them. A Statement is one query, built from the Toolbox's Clause functions. The piece is a Derived table: a Statement given a name with derived, which another Statement reads like a table, and which the Hive writes at its top as WITH name AS (...). Wrapped in a function, kept in a file of its own in building_blocks/, it is a Building block, which any notebook of the Project imports. project.new_block makes the file, from the Project's building_block Template, the shape a new block's file is made from.

When you'd use it

If two Statements need the same numbers, such as each job's runs, failed runs and minutes, write them once in a Building block: they can't be worked out two different ways, and a fix reaches them all.
If a Statement grows long, write it in named steps, each a Derived table: it can be read and checked a step at a time.
If you add up before a join, as Count, join and stay safe does, write the added-up table as a Derived table.


Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project with the Table references, the files that describe each table, that the block reads. Count, join and stay safe left the Project with its Table references filled in, as Add your tables' references fills them, and two more beside them. This how-to reads two of them, ops.job_runs, with its Aliases minutes and failed, and ops.jobs. In your own Project they are there already; here, the steps make them again, their comments left out:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
project.start()

Python shows

tables/ and warehouse.py.
Next: set conn, your connection, in warehouse.py.

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

"""ops.job_runs - one row per run of a job, on the day it ran."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table, equals

job_runs = Table(
    "ops.job_runs",
    columns={
        "run_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "status": "string",
        "duration_mins": "int",
        "avg_retry_secs": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["run_id"],
    does_not_add_up=["avg_retry_secs"],
    descriptions={
        "status": "SUCCESS / FAILED / TEST, NULL while running",
    },
)

minutes = job_runs.duration_mins
failed = equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED")

2. Name one step with derived

Goal: a Statement other Statements read like a table. derived gives a Statement a name, and gives back a Table: its columns are what its SELECT makes, checked like a Table reference's. Here, each job's runs and minutes on 2026-09-24:

job_runs = ops.job_runs  # -> Table
job_totals = derived("job_totals", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_runs.job_id, AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
           AS(sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id),
))
job_totals

Python shows
derived('job_totals', columns: job_id, runs, minutes)

Done when job_totals shows its columns job_id, runs and minutes. Then step 3.

3. Read the Derived table from another Statement

Goal: a Statement built on the named step. Another Statement reads it with FROM or JOIN, like any table. Here, the minutes per team. It needs no filter on the day of its own: the day is inside job_totals, and ops.jobs isn't partitioned.

per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(ops.jobs.team, AS(sum_of(job_totals.minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_totals),
    JOIN(ops.jobs, ON=equals(ops.jobs.job_id, job_totals.job_id)),
    GROUP_BY(ops.jobs.team),
)
text = show_hive(per_team)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH job_totals AS (
  SELECT
    job_runs.job_id,
    COUNT(*) AS runs,
    SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  WHERE
    job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_runs.job_id
)
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  SUM(job_totals.minutes) AS minutes
FROM job_totals
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = job_totals.job_id
GROUP BY
  jobs.team;

run_query(per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame

finance50




The Hive starts with WITH job_totals AS (...), the step's own Hive; then the Statement reads job_totals by name. A Derived table lives only inside the Statement that reads it: nothing is saved in the warehouse.

4. Make a Building block with project.new_block

Goal: a file for the piece, which any notebook imports. A Building block is a function that gives back the Derived table, so every Statement that needs it calls the function. What changes from one Statement to the next, such as the rows it reads, becomes its argument. project.new_block makes its file, building_blocks/<name>.py, from admin/templates/building_block.py, and registers it in the registry, the Project's list of what each Project action made. Run it from the admin notebook admin/new_block.ipynb, or any notebook of the Project:

project.new_block("runs_per_job")

It writes building_blocks/runs_per_job.py

"""runs_per_job - TODO: say in one line what one row of it is.

Fill in every <...> in the code below this text, brackets and all: Python stops at one left
in. They are:
    <SOURCE_NAME> and <SOURCE_TABLE>: the table it reads, by its file in references/ and its
        name there, such as ops__job_runs and job_runs.
    <GROUP_COLUMN>: the column each row is for, such as job_id.
    <NUMBER_COLUMN>: a column of numbers to add up, such as duration_mins.
    <TOTAL_NAME>: the name of what it adds up to, in quotes, such as "minutes".

Why: TODO: which tables' notebooks read it, so they can never work it out two ways.

project.new_block("runs_per_job") wrote this file from admin/templates/building_block.py. A
Building block is a piece several notebooks share; this one is a Derived table, a Statement
given a name, which each Statement reading it writes at its top, as WITH runs_per_job AS (...). In a
notebook's logic cell, import it, and read it like a table, given the rows it reads, such
as READS, the condition at the top of a saved_table notebook's logic cell:

    from building_blocks.runs_per_job import runs_per_job


    def preview() -> Statement:
        per_group = runs_per_job(READS)
        return statement(SELECT(per_group.<GROUP_COLUMN>, per_group.row_count),
                         FROM(per_group))
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>  # the Table reference it reads


def runs_per_job(reads: Condition) -> Table:  # named as its file is
    """One row per group of the rows `reads` picks: its rows, and its total.

    `reads` is a condition on the table it reads, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24").
    On a partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns.
    """
    return derived(
        "runs_per_job",  # the name the Hive gives it, the same as its file's
        statement(
            SELECT(
                <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
                # to keep each Partition's rows apart, add its partition column here and in
                # GROUP_BY, such as job_runs.dt
                AS(count_rows(), "row_count"),
                # add a line like the next for each number to add up
                AS(sum_of(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<NUMBER_COLUMN>), <TOTAL_NAME>),
            ),
            FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>),
        ),
    )

It writes admin/registry.csv too.

Done when it says Wrote building_blocks/runs_per_job.py. Then step 5.

5. Fill in the block's file

Goal: the block's own piece of Statement. The file's docstring says what each <...> is for. Fill them in, or write over its code with your own piece, as here: each job's runs, failed runs and minutes, for each day of the rows it reads.

The file building_blocks/runs_per_job.py, after you fill it in

"""runs_per_job - each job's runs, failed runs and minutes for each dt it reads.

Why: more than one Statement needs each job's runs, failed runs and minutes, such as each
team's failed runs and a check of one day, and writing them once here means they can never
be counted two different ways.

Every run counts, TEST runs and runs still going among them, since each one takes the
cluster's time: a team that wants TEST runs left out says so in its own Statement.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.ops__job_runs import failed, job_runs, minutes  # failed, minutes: Aliases


def runs_per_job(reads: Condition) -> Table:
    """One row per job for each dt of the rows `reads` picks: its runs, failed runs and
    minutes.

    `reads` is a condition on ops.job_runs, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"):
    the table is partitioned by dt, so it must filter dt. dt is kept in GROUP_BY, so the
    counts of each dt stay apart when `reads` picks several, and in SELECT, so a Statement
    that reads this can tell its days apart, or keep them apart in its own GROUP_BY.
    """
    return derived(
        "runs_per_job",
        statement(
            SELECT(
                job_runs.job_id,
                job_runs.dt,
                AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
                AS(count_rows(where=failed), "failed_runs"),
                AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes"),
            ),
            FROM(job_runs),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id, job_runs.dt),
        ),
    )

Three things to notice:

It imports what it reads: the Toolbox's names, and the Table reference of ops.job_runs with its two Aliases, your own names for its columns and conditions, from references/. A Building block's file imports only these, never another Building block's file or a notebook: Keep the Project in order says why.
Its function is named as its file is, and so is its Derived table, "runs_per_job": the Hive and the Lineage, the record of which table columns feed each output column, of every Statement that reads it call it the same.
Its argument is a Condition, a test of rows, here the rows it reads, so each Statement that calls it picks its own. A Saved table, a table your own Statements write, built on it hands it the rows it saves, as Follow lineage shows.


Done when the file holds no <...> or TODO line. Then step 6.

6. Import it, and check it alone

Goal: proof that the block gives the rows you expect. A notebook imports it by its file's name:

from building_blocks.runs_per_job import runs_per_job
last_day = runs_per_job(equals(ops.job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"))  # -> Table

run_query runs a Statement, and a Derived table is a step inside one, so check a block alone in the smallest Statement that reads it: every column, with all_columns. Check a block like this before you build on it:

check_last_day = statement(SELECT(all_columns(last_day)), FROM(last_day))  # -> Statement
run_query(check_last_day, query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when the check gives one row per job for 2026-09-24. Then step 7.

7. Use the same block in another Statement

Goal: a second Statement built on the same numbers. Any Statement that needs each job's runs calls the same function. Here, each team's failed runs over two days: the block keeps each day apart, and the Statement adds the days up:

two_days = runs_per_job(between(ops.job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"))  # -> Table
failed_per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(ops.jobs.team, AS(sum_of(two_days.failed_runs), "failed_runs")),
    FROM(two_days),
    JOIN(ops.jobs, ON=equals(ops.jobs.job_id, two_days.job_id)),
    GROUP_BY(ops.jobs.team),
)
text = show_hive(failed_per_team)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH runs_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS runs,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END) AS failed_runs,
    SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  WHERE
    job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt
)
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  SUM(runs_per_job.failed_runs) AS failed_runs
FROM runs_per_job
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = runs_per_job.job_id
GROUP BY
  jobs.team;

run_query(failed_per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamfailed_runs

data0

finance2




Its Hive starts with the same WITH runs_per_job AS (...) as the check's, reading the days this Statement gave it. The Alias failed became job_runs.status = 'FAILED': an Alias is a name for you, and the Hive says what it stands for.

Done when the result gives finance 2 failed runs and data 0. Then step 8.

8. Keep one Building block to a file

Goal: blocks the registry lists, each in its own file. Each Building block is a file of its own in building_blocks/, named as it is, made by project.new_block, so the registry lists it and project.check() can find it. A second block that reads this one, such as the busy jobs among its rows, is made the same way, with project.new_block("busy_jobs"), and takes the Derived table runs_per_job gives back as its argument, rather than importing building_blocks/runs_per_job.py: the notebook that reads both imports both, builds runs_per_job once and hands it in.

Done when each block you need has its own file, made by project.new_block. Then check it worked, below.

Common mistakes

Running a Derived table itself

run_query takes a Statement. A Derived table is a step to read, so on its own it stops:

run_query(last_day, query)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  run_query was given derived('runs_per_job', columns: job_id, dt, runs, failed_runs, minutes), which isn't a Statement.
  Why it matters: run_query writes the Hive of one Statement made by statement(...) and sends it.
  Usual fix:      A Derived table is a step for another Statement to read, so wrap it in the smallest Statement that reads it: run_query(statement(SELECT(all_columns(last_day)), FROM(last_day)), query).
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Wrap it in the smallest Statement that reads it, as step 6, Import it, and check it alone, does.

A column the block doesn't make

A Derived table's columns are checked like a Table reference's, so a name it doesn't have stops at once, with the names it has:

last_day.failed

Python stops with this message
AttributeError:
runs_per_job has no column 'failed'. Did you mean 'failed_runs'? Its columns are: job_id, dt, runs, failed_runs, minutes.

failed is an Alias of ops.job_runs, for the block to count with; what the block gives back is the count, "failed_runs".

Making a Building block whose name is taken

project.new_block writes over no file of yours. Run again with the same name, it stops:

project.new_block("runs_per_job")

Python stops with this message
FileExistsError:
  What happened:  project.new_block("runs_per_job"): building_blocks/runs_per_job.py is already there, so nothing was made.
  Why it matters: A Building block is made once, and making it again would write over your file.
  Usual fix:      Open building_blocks/runs_per_job.py, or give the new Building block a name of its own. To start it again, archive it first with project.remove_block("runs_per_job").
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Open the file that is there, or give the new block a name of its own.

6. Save a table Getting started

Goal

Keep a Statement's rows in a Saved table: a real table in the warehouse that your Project writes, and that other Statements then read like any other table. A Statement is one query; a Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout. project.new_table makes the table's Table folder, the folder that holds everything about one Saved table, with a Table notebook in it, the notebook whose cells create and write the table, made from the saved_table Template, the empty shape of such a notebook. It also makes the table's Table reference, the file that describes the table's columns. You fill in both, try the notebook's steps, and from then on project.run_tables writes the table with the Project's other Saved tables.

When you'd use it

If a result is slow to work out, save it in a Saved table: working it out once is cheaper than working it out again every time someone asks.
If many notebooks and dashboards read a result, save it in a Saved table, so each reads the rows already worked out.
If you need each Partition's rows kept as they were when they were written, save them in a Saved table. A Partition is a slice of a table: the rows that share one value of each column the table is partitioned by, such as one day.
If you only need the rows once, run the Statement with run_query instead, as Build and run a first statement shows.


The Example database, the small made-up tables this page runs on, can be read but not written to. So on this page each write shows its Hive, ready to paste, and a write that is sent goes to warehouse.py's execute, which here calls example_database.execute: it prints the write's first line and changes nothing. At work, the same calls send it to your warehouse, through your own execute.

Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project with the Table references the Saved table reads.

Share Building blocks left the Project with its Table references filled in and one Building block. The Saved table this how-to makes reads one table, ops.run_alerts, so the calls below make the Project again with three Table references alone, as project.add_references writes them:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
project.start()

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

        "status": "SUCCESS / FAILED / TEST, NULL while running",

    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.

It writes tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb too.

It made four things, and listed two of them, the Table folder and the Table reference, in the registry, the Project's list of everything a Project action made:

tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/, the table's Table folder, named after the database and the table joined by two underscores;
mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb in it, the Table notebook, from the saved_table Template: a Saved table worked out from a table it reads, a count per group of the rows it reads;
lineage/ beside it, empty until the notebook exports the table's Lineage;
references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py, its Table reference. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook creates it, so nothing can be asked of it: this one stands in until project.reference_from_logic writes it from the logic you write.


Both hold <...> placeholders. Type over each in the notebook, brackets and all; the Table reference's are written for you, once the logic is. Until you do, the notebook isn't Python yet, and project.load, which runs a notebook's logic from anywhere in the Project, says which line stops it:

project.load("mart.alerts_per_severity")

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  project.load("mart.alerts_per_severity"): the logic cell of tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb isn't Python yet, at its line 9: from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>
  Why it matters: A placeholder such as <SOURCE_TABLE> stops Python until it is filled in.
  Usual fix:      Fill in every <...> in the logic cell, run it in its notebook to check it, then load it again.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Done when tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/ and references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py are there. Then step 3.

3. Fill in its notebook

Goal: a Table notebook that says what the table reads and how it counts it.

Open the notebook in Jupyter. This is it as project.new_table wrote it, from the saved_table Template, the table's database and name filled in, <...> everywhere else:

The file tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb, as project.new_table wrote it

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.alerts_per_severity
#
# Fill in every `<...>` in the code cells below, brackets and all: the notebook won't run with
# one left. They are:
#   - `<SOURCE_NAME>` and `<SOURCE_TABLE>`: the table it reads, by its file in references/ and
#     its name there, such as `ops__job_runs` and `job_runs`;
#   - `<READS>`: which of its rows write() reads, as a condition on it, such as
#     `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")` or `equals(region_costs.region, "eu")`. On a
#     partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns. A table that isn't
#     partitioned can be read whole: delete the line `READS = <READS>` and each line
#     `WHERE(READS),` instead;
#   - `<GROUP_COLUMN>`: the column each row is for, such as `job_id`;
#   - `<COUNT_NAME>`: the name of the count it saves, in quotes, such as `"runs"`;
#   - `<PARTITION_COLUMN>`: the column of the table it reads that fills this table's partition
#     column, such as `dt` or `region`. If this table isn't partitioned, delete the two lines
#     of rows() naming `<PARTITION_COLUMN>`, and write `PARTITIONED_BY = []`, instead.
#
# ## Terms
#
# - **Logic**: the code cell that starts `# logic`, cell 2. It only imports and defines, so
#   `project.load("mart.alerts_per_severity")` can run it from anywhere else, for lineage across your
#   tables and for `admin/run_tables.ipynb`, which sends its `write()`.
# - **Table reference**: `references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py`, the script describing this table: its
#   columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
# - **Partition**: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned
#   by.
# - **Key**: the columns whose values name exactly one row of the table.
# - **Decision**: one thing about this table that only you decide, such as its location; its
#   Table reference says whether it is made.
#
# ## Steps
#
# 1. **Fill in each `<...>`.** Goal: logic that runs. Action: type over each one listed above,
#    in the code cells. Done when no code cell holds a `<...>`; then step 2.
# 2. **Save the notebook.** Goal: the logic its Table reference is written from. Action: save
#    it. Done when it is saved; then step 3.
# 3. **Write its Table reference.** Goal: a Table reference written from the logic. Action:
#    run `project.reference_from_logic("mart.alerts_per_severity")`, a new_table Workflow step, which
#    writes `references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py` from the logic, for you to answer.
#    It lists the columns write() saves, in the order its SELECT gives them: here
#    <GROUP_COLUMN>, typed as the table read types it, then <COUNT_NAME>, a count, "bigint",
#    and last its partition column, if it has one, as PARTITIONED_BY names it. Its Key,
#    proposed from the query, is the column each row is for, and the partition column if it
#    has one, such as key=["job_id", "region"].
#    Done when `references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py` lists those columns; then step 4.
# 4. **Answer its Decisions.** Goal: each Decision made. Action: answer each open Decision its
#    Table reference leaves, such as its location, in the new_table Workflow's decisions
#    cell. Done when its Table reference leaves no Decision open; then step 5.
# 5. **Say what one row is.** Goal: a reader knows what the table holds. Action: write one
#    line saying what one row of mart.alerts_per_severity is, in place of the TODO line below. Done when
#    no TODO line is left in this cell; then step 6.
# 6. **Run the cells.** Goal: the logic tried, its lineage and Hive written, and its rows in
#    the Reference sheet. Action: run the code cells top to bottom, once its Table reference
#    is written from the logic. Done when each runs with no error, and cell 3 shows the rows
#    `preview()` gives.
#
# ## This table
#
# TODO: say in one line what one row of mart.alerts_per_severity is.
#
# A Saved table worked out from a table it reads: a count per group of the rows READS picks.
# It is partitioned as PARTITIONED_BY says, or not at all.
#
# Made by `project.new_table("mart.alerts_per_severity")` from
# `admin/templates/saved_table.py`, which filled in the database, the table and its file's name,
# mart__alerts_per_severity.
#
# ## The code cells
#
# - 1: the Project and your query.
# - 2: the logic, which starts `# logic`.
# - 3: try it.
# - 4: its lineage.
# - 5: its Hive, in mart__alerts_per_severity.hql.
# - 6: its rows in the Reference sheet.
#
# ## Self-check
#
# - No code cell holds a `<...>`.
# - `references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py` lists the columns `write()` saves, and leaves no Decision open.
# - One line above says what one row is, and no TODO line is left.
# - Each code cell runs top to bottom with no error.


# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, WHERE, Clause, Statement, count_rows,
    create_table, equals, statement,
)
from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity  # this table's own Table reference
# each table it reads, such as: from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>

# The rows write() reads: change it to write others. A condition that calls something other
# than equals, such as between or is_in, needs it added to the import above.
READS = <READS>

# This table's partition columns, in the order its SELECT gives them, last: [] for none.
PARTITIONED_BY = ["<PARTITION_COLUMN>"]


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.alerts_per_severity from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(alerts_per_severity, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> list[Clause]:
    """The clauses preview and write share, so the two can never disagree: a count per group
    of the rows READS picks.

    A partitioned table's partition columns come last in SELECT, in its Table reference's
    order, named as it names them: INSERT_OVERWRITE fills each Partition from the rows' own
    values, and replaces only the Partitions they fill. They stay in GROUP_BY too, so each
    Partition's rows stay apart.
    """
    return [
        SELECT(
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
            AS(count_rows(), <COUNT_NAME>),
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<PARTITION_COLUMN>,  # this table's partition column, last
        ),
        FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
        WHERE(READS),
        GROUP_BY(
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<PARTITION_COLUMN>,  # kept, so each Partition's rows stay apart
        ),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows())


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3: save the rows, replacing the Partitions they fill, or the whole table if it
    isn't partitioned: sent twice, it leaves the same rows, not twice as many."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(alerts_per_severity), *rows())

# %%
# 3. Try it: the Hive of each step, then the rows
show_hive([create(), write()])
run_query(preview(), query)

# %%
# 4. Lineage: a new pair in lineage/, named by the time and this table, beside the ones before
export_lineage(write())

# %%
# 5. Its Hive: mart__alerts_per_severity.hql beside this notebook, from the logic as last saved
export_hive()

# %%
# 6. The Reference sheet: this table's rows, from references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py
project.refresh_reference_sheet("mart.alerts_per_severity")

Here is the whole notebook, filled in, as it is in the Starter Example project, a Project made for the Example database to read and copy from:

The file tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb, after you fill it in

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.alerts_per_severity
#
# One row per severity and day: how many alerts of that severity the runs raised that day.
#
# A Saved table worked out from a table it reads: a count per group of the rows READS picks.
# It is partitioned as PARTITIONED_BY says, or not at all.
#
# Made by `project.new_table("mart.alerts_per_severity")` from
# `admin/templates/saved_table.py`, which filled in the database, the table and its file's name,
# mart__alerts_per_severity. Run the cells top to bottom once both are filled in.
#
# The code cells: 1 the Project and your query; 2 the logic, which starts `# logic`; 3 try it;
# 4 its lineage; 5 its Hive, in mart__alerts_per_severity.hql; 6 its rows in the Reference sheet.
# The logic only imports and defines, so `project.load("mart.alerts_per_severity")` can run it
# from anywhere else, for lineage across your tables and for `admin/run_tables.ipynb`, which
# sends its `write()`.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, WHERE, Clause, Statement, count_rows,
    create_table, equals, statement,
)
from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity  # this table's own Table reference
# each table it reads, such as: from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# The rows write() reads: change it to write others. A condition that calls something other
# than equals, such as between or is_in, needs it added to the import above.
READS = equals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24")

# This table's partition columns, in the order its SELECT gives them, last: [] for none.
PARTITIONED_BY = ["dt"]


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.alerts_per_severity from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(alerts_per_severity, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> list[Clause]:
    """The clauses preview and write share, so the two can never disagree: a count per group
    of the rows READS picks.

    A partitioned table's partition columns come last in SELECT, in its Table reference's
    order, named as it names them: INSERT_OVERWRITE fills each Partition from the rows' own
    values, and replaces only the Partitions they fill. They stay in GROUP_BY too, so each
    Partition's rows stay apart.
    """
    return [
        SELECT(
            run_alerts.severity,  # what each row is for
            AS(count_rows(), "alerts"),
            run_alerts.dt,  # this table's partition column, last
        ),
        FROM(run_alerts),
        WHERE(READS),
        GROUP_BY(
            run_alerts.severity,
            run_alerts.dt,  # kept, so each Partition's rows stay apart
        ),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows())


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3: save the rows, replacing the Partitions they fill, or the whole table if it
    isn't partitioned: sent twice, it leaves the same rows, not twice as many."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(alerts_per_severity), *rows())

# %%
# 3. Try it: the Hive of each step, then the rows
show_hive([create(), write()])
run_query(preview(), query)

# %%
# 4. Lineage: a new pair in lineage/, named by the time and this table, beside the ones before
export_lineage(write())

# %%
# 5. Its Hive: mart__alerts_per_severity.hql beside this notebook, from the logic as last saved
export_hive()

# %%
# 6. The Reference sheet: this table's rows, from references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py
project.refresh_reference_sheet("mart.alerts_per_severity")

Each <...> became:

<SOURCE_NAME> and <SOURCE_TABLE>: ops__run_alerts and run_alerts, the table it reads, by its file in references/ and its name there;
<READS>: equals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24"), the rows write() reads: one day of ops.run_alerts, which is partitioned by dt, so a read must filter dt;
<GROUP_COLUMN>: severity, the column each row is for, so <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN> became run_alerts.severity;
<COUNT_NAME>: "alerts", the name of the count, in quotes, since it is a value, where a name Python reads, such as run_alerts, goes in bare;
<PARTITION_COLUMN>: dt, the column of ops.run_alerts that fills this table's own partition column, so <SOURCE_TABLE>.<PARTITION_COLUMN> became run_alerts.dt.


The notebook has six code cells, run top to bottom:

the path lines and imports, as in Start a Project, and your query;
the logic, whose first line is # logic: imports, READS, and four functions, each giving a Statement and running nothing: create, rows, preview and write. rows() holds the clauses preview and write share, so the rows you check are the rows you save;
try it: each step's Hive, then the rows;
its Lineage, the record of which table columns feed each of its columns, as Follow lineage shows;
its Hive, in mart__alerts_per_severity.hql beside the notebook, as step 9 shows;
its rows in the Reference sheet, the one table listing every column of every table the Project has a Table reference for, as Keep the Project in order shows.


Done when no <...> is left in the notebook's code cells. Then step 4.

4. Fill in its Table reference, beside it

Goal: a Table reference that describes the table the notebook's write() fills.

references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py describes the table the notebook's write() fills. This is it as project.new_table wrote it, from the saved_table_reference Template, to stand in until the logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic("mart.alerts_per_severity"), the new_table Workflow's step, writes it from the logic, its columns, partition columns and Key, leaving its location= for you to answer. The Starter Example project's file was written by hand instead, so it is the Starter's own, and this how-to shows that way:

The file references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py, as project.new_table wrote it

"""mart.alerts_per_severity - TODO: say in one line what one row is.

project.reference_from_logic("mart.alerts_per_severity") writes this file again from the table's logic,
filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

alerts_per_severity = Table(
    "mart.alerts_per_severity",
    columns={"<COLUMNS>": "string"},  # project.reference_from_logic writes them, from the logic
    location=None,  # TODO: its own folder, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/alerts_per_severity"
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = alerts_per_severity.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(alerts_per_severity.<column>, <value>)

It has no does_not_add_up=, which the Table references project.add_references writes have: the Template's table saves counts, which add up, so it is left out and taken as empty. Add does_not_add_up=[...] yourself if your table saves an average, a ratio or a distinct count. reference_from_logic writes each column, its partition columns and Key; by hand, write those, location= and the TODO lines, as here, the file as it is in the Starter Example project:

The file references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py, after you fill it in

"""mart.alerts_per_severity - one row per severity and day: the alerts raised.

Why: its notebook saves the day's count of low and high alerts, so a dashboard of alerts
reads two rows a day rather than every alert.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, and it was
then written by hand, which keeps it yours: project.reference_from_logic would have written it
from the table's logic. Its notebook's text cell says which columns it lists, and its Key.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

alerts_per_severity = Table(
    "mart.alerts_per_severity",
    columns={
        "severity": "string",
        "alerts": "bigint",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["severity", "dt"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/alerts_per_severity",  # its own folder
    descriptions={
        "severity": "low / high, as ops.run_alerts holds it",
        "alerts": "the alerts of that severity raised that day",
        "dt": "the day the alerts were raised, as yyyy-MM-dd",
    },
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = alerts_per_severity.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(alerts_per_severity.<column>, <value>)

columns={...} lists each column write() saves, in the order its SELECT gives them, each with its Hive type as DESCRIBE prints it: "severity" is a "string", as in ops.run_alerts, the count "alerts" is a "bigint", and the partition column "dt" comes last.
partitioned_by=["dt"] makes the table kept a Partition at a time, here a day: a write replaces the Partitions it fills, and every Statement that reads the table must filter dt.
key=["severity", "dt"] is its Key, the columns whose values name one row of the table: each row is one severity on one day, so a severity alone repeats, once a day. The warehouse never checks a Key, so the Toolbox does: a write whose rows could share one is refused as it is built. Here GROUP_BY makes one row per severity and dt.
location= is the table's own folder in the warehouse, where its files go: a Decision, one thing about the table that only you decide, written in quotes and used exactly as written. Ask whoever looks after your warehouse where your team may write, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/alerts_per_severity". Give each Saved table a folder of its own, never one inside another's: the Toolbox refuses two that share one.
descriptions={...} says what each column holds, for the Reference sheet.


Done when the Table reference holds no <...> and no TODO line. Then step 5.

5. Add it to its Database module

Goal: references/mart.py, the module that gives the Database mart's tables. A Database is a named group of tables, the first part of a table's name, mart in mart.alerts_per_severity; its Database module is the one file in references/ that gives each of its tables.

A Saved table joins its Database module, references/mart.py, once its Table reference has no <...> left: the next Project action that writes the Database modules adds it, for example project.refresh_reference_sheet, which cell 6 runs, for its own table. Run here, it writes mart.py:

project.refresh_reference_sheet("mart.alerts_per_severity")

Python shows
Wrote references/reference_sheet.csv: 3 rows, 1 table.
Wrote references/mart.py: from references import mart gives mart.alerts_per_severity.
Restart the kernel of a notebook that imported references, to see a table added since.

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.alerts_per_severity.severity.

Its tables: alerts_per_severity (references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["alerts_per_severity"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes references/reference_sheet.csv too.

Done when it says it wrote references/mart.py. Then step 6.

7. Load its logic

Goal: the notebook's functions, by name. In Jupyter, run the notebook's cells top to bottom. Here, project.load runs its logic cell and gives back its functions by name, as any notebook of the Project can: an object with one attribute per function of the logic cell, such as logic.write:

logic = project.load("mart.alerts_per_severity")  # -> the notebook's functions

8. Try its steps

Goal: the Hive that creates and writes the table, and the rows the write would save. Cell 3 prints the Hive of the steps that create the table and write the rows READS picks, then runs the preview, the rows the write would save:

text = show_hive([logic.create(), logic.write()])  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
-- 1 of 2
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE IF NOT EXISTS mart.alerts_per_severity (
  severity STRING,
  alerts BIGINT,
  PRIMARY KEY (severity, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  dt STRING
)
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/alerts_per_severity';

-- 2 of 2
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.alerts_per_severity PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  run_alerts.severity,
  COUNT(*) AS alerts,
  run_alerts.dt
FROM ops.run_alerts AS run_alerts
WHERE
  run_alerts.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  run_alerts.severity,
  run_alerts.dt;

run_query(logic.preview(), query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

severityalertsdt

high22026-09-24

low52026-09-24




logic.create() gives CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE IF NOT EXISTS, from the Table reference: EXTERNAL and LOCATION make it a table whose files are yours, in its own folder, and IF NOT EXISTS, may_exist=True in the notebook, skips a table that is there already and never changes it, so the cell can run every day. PRIMARY KEY ... DISABLE NOVALIDATE writes the Key for whoever reads the table's description: Hive keeps it and never checks it.
logic.write() gives INSERT OVERWRITE: it replaces whatever the Partitions it fills held, and no other, so sending it twice leaves the same rows, not twice as many.


At work, project.run_tables, in steps 11 and 12, sends each table's write() and never its create(), so create each table once, before its first project.run_tables. From any notebook of the Project, with logic from project.load as here, that is run_execute(logic.create(), execute); in the table's own notebook, add a cell after cell 3 that runs its create() the same way, and import execute beside query in cell 1. The Example database can be read but not written, so this page creates nothing.

Done when the preview gives one row per severity for 2026-09-24. Then step 9.

9. Keep its Hive beside it

Goal: the table's Hive in a file beside its notebook, to read and to commit. Cell 3 already prints each step's Hive, but only as the notebook's output, which is gone once the cell runs again. Cell 5, export_hive(), keeps that Hive in a file beside the notebook, mart__alerts_per_severity.hql, for two things cell 3 can't do: a file to read at a glance, without opening the notebook or running anything, and a file to commit with the notebook, so each commit shows the Hive the table sent then. It holds create(), then write(), each as show_hive shows it, in the order they are sent, under lines saying the file is generated. The Python stays the table's definition, so edit the logic and leave the file as written. To change it:

edit cell 2, the logic, of mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb;
save the notebook (Ctrl+S in Jupyter): export_hive() reads the notebook as last saved, so a change you haven't saved isn't in the file;
run cell 5, export_hive(), which writes mart__alerts_per_severity.hql again, over the one before: the same text when the logic is the same.


The file is to read and to commit, not to run: project.run_tables, in steps 11 and 12, sends the writes. Jupyter runs a notebook in its own folder, so the calls below move there first, as opening the notebook would, then back:

import os
from pathlib import Path

Given a Statement instead, export_hive writes that Statement's Hive alone, as show_hive shows it, in a file named for the Statement's variable, in the folder of the notebook or script that calls it. Put each Statement in a variable of its own, such as daily and weekly, and pass each in a call of its own: export_hive(daily), then export_hive(weekly), writes daily.hql and weekly.hql. Pass each in its own call, never in a loop, whose one variable would name every file the same, each over the one before.

Done when mart__alerts_per_severity.hql holds create(), then write(). Then step 10.

10. Choose how a write fills its Partitions

Goal: know which of the two ways a write fills Partitions you want.

The Template's write selects the partition column, run_alerts.dt, and its Hive says PARTITION(dt) with no value: each row goes to the Partition its own dt names, so a write whose READS picks several days fills each of them. The Toolbox puts the partition columns last in SELECT, where Hive looks for them, wherever you list them. Filling Partitions from the rows needs Hive's dynamic partitioning switched on first: show_hive prints the two SET lines that do it, the Session setting "dynamic_partitions", a named set of Hive SET lines sent before a Statement, ahead of the write, and project.run_tables sends them first.

A write can name its Partition instead, with partition={...}. Its SELECT then leaves the partition column out, and its Hive names the day, with no SET lines:

from references import ops
alerts_per_severity = mart.alerts_per_severity  # -> Table
run_alerts = ops.run_alerts  # -> Table
one_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(alerts_per_severity, partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"}),
    SELECT(run_alerts.severity, AS(count_rows(), "alerts")),
    FROM(run_alerts),
    WHERE(equals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(run_alerts.severity),
)
text = show_hive(one_day)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.alerts_per_severity PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-24')
SELECT
  run_alerts.severity,
  COUNT(*) AS alerts
FROM ops.run_alerts AS run_alerts
WHERE
  run_alerts.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  run_alerts.severity;

partition= says where the rows go, and WHERE which rows are read, and nothing checks that the two agree: keep them the same day. The Template's way needs no such care, since each row carries its own day; Rewrite some partitions shows when to name them.

If each row carries its own Partition's value, select the partition column, as the Template does, and leave partition= out.
If you write one Partition you name, give partition={...} and leave the partition column out of SELECT.


Done when the Hive of one_day names its Partition, PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-24'). Then step 11.

Common mistakes

Selecting the partition column a write names

With partition={"dt": ...}, the day comes from partition=, so a write that also selects run_alerts.dt is refused as you build it:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(alerts_per_severity, partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"}),
    SELECT(run_alerts.severity, AS(count_rows(), "alerts"), run_alerts.dt),
    FROM(run_alerts),
    WHERE(equals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(run_alerts.severity, run_alerts.dt),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(alerts_per_severity): it also selects dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse fills a table's columns by position, not by name, so a missing or extra column would put values in the wrong columns.
  Usual fix:      SELECT every column of alerts_per_severity's Table reference, each under its own name, except a partition column the write names in partition={...}. The Toolbox puts them in the right order, its partition columns last.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Pick one of the two ways: select only run_alerts.severity and the count with partition=, or leave partition= out and let the rows fill it, as the Template does.

Leaving the partition column out of GROUP_BY

Each row of the result is one group, so a column in SELECT must be in GROUP_BY. The Template keeps the partition column in both: dropped from GROUP_BY, the write is refused by a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(alerts_per_severity),
    SELECT(run_alerts.severity, AS(count_rows(), "alerts"), run_alerts.dt),
    FROM(run_alerts),
    WHERE(equals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(run_alerts.severity),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  SELECT has run_alerts.dt, which GROUP_BY leaves out.
  Why it matters: Each output row is one group, so a column that isn't grouped has no single value to show. Hive would refuse the Statement.
  Usual fix:      Add run_alerts.dt to GROUP_BY. To keep one whole row per group instead, such as each job's latest run, number the rows with row_number(...) inside derived(...), then keep number 1 with WHERE(equals(..., 1)); help(row_number) shows how.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Keep the partition column in GROUP_BY as well as in SELECT: GROUP_BY(run_alerts.severity, run_alerts.dt), as the Template's rows() does.

Making a Table folder that is already there

project.new_table leaves a Table folder that is there as it is: it holds your notebook and its Lineage. Run again for the same table, it stops:

project.new_table("mart.alerts_per_severity")

Python stops with this message
FileExistsError:
  What happened:  project.new_table("mart.alerts_per_severity"): tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/ is already there, so nothing was made.
  Why it matters: A Table folder is made once, and making it again would write over your notebook and its lineage.
  Usual fix:      Open tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/mart__alerts_per_severity.ipynb. To start the table again, archive the folder first with project.remove_table("mart.alerts_per_severity").
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Open the notebook that is there instead. To start a table again, archive its folder first, as Keep the Project in order shows.

7. Follow lineage Getting started

Goal

See where each column of a Statement's result comes from: the table columns that feed it, the conditions that decide which rows count towards it, and the Hive that ran. A Statement is one query; its Lineage is the record of which table columns feed each of its output columns. export_lineage writes this Lineage as two files, an HTML page to explore in your browser and a Markdown file to read anywhere. You export one Statement's, then a Saved table's from its Table notebook, into its Table folder's lineage/, then a table that reads another Saved table, drawn together with it, all the way back to the tables they start from; project.run_tables writes them in that same order. A Saved table is a real table in the warehouse that your Statements write; its Table folder holds everything about it, and its Table notebook is the notebook in that folder whose cells create and write it. Last, you declare a change made in pandas with python_step, so the Lineage goes on through it.

When you'd use it

If you hand someone a number, export its Lineage first, so you can answer "where does this come from?" by pointing at the files.
If a number looks wrong, read its Markdown file: it lists every condition that decides which rows count towards it, from every step, in one place.
If a Saved table's logic changes, export its Lineage again: each export is kept, beside the ones before, to compare.
If a step of a table is done in pandas, declare it with python_step, as steps 10 to 13 do.


Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project with the Table references and the Building block the steps read. A Table reference is the file that describes one table; a Building block is a piece of your own code reused across notebooks, here a Derived table, a named Statement another Statement reads from.

Save a table left the Project with its Table references, its Building block and one Saved table. This how-to reads two of the Table references and the Building block, so the calls below make those again, shortened; mart.alerts_per_severity isn't read here, so it is left out:

import os
from pathlib import Path
from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
project.start()

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes building_blocks/runs_per_job.py

"""runs_per_job - TODO: say in one line what one row of it is.

Fill in every <...> in the code below this text, brackets and all: Python stops at one left
in. They are:
    <SOURCE_NAME> and <SOURCE_TABLE>: the table it reads, by its file in references/ and its
        name there, such as ops__job_runs and job_runs.
    <GROUP_COLUMN>: the column each row is for, such as job_id.
    <NUMBER_COLUMN>: a column of numbers to add up, such as duration_mins.
    <TOTAL_NAME>: the name of what it adds up to, in quotes, such as "minutes".

Why: TODO: which tables' notebooks read it, so they can never work it out two ways.

project.new_block("runs_per_job") wrote this file from admin/templates/building_block.py. A
Building block is a piece several notebooks share; this one is a Derived table, a Statement
given a name, which each Statement reading it writes at its top, as WITH runs_per_job AS (...). In a
notebook's logic cell, import it, and read it like a table, given the rows it reads, such
as READS, the condition at the top of a saved_table notebook's logic cell:

    from building_blocks.runs_per_job import runs_per_job


    def preview() -> Statement:
        per_group = runs_per_job(READS)
        return statement(SELECT(per_group.<GROUP_COLUMN>, per_group.row_count),
                         FROM(per_group))
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>  # the Table reference it reads


def runs_per_job(reads: Condition) -> Table:  # named as its file is
    """One row per group of the rows `reads` picks: its rows, and its total.

    `reads` is a condition on the table it reads, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24").
    On a partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns.
    """
    return derived(
        "runs_per_job",  # the name the Hive gives it, the same as its file's
        statement(
            SELECT(
                <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
                # to keep each Partition's rows apart, add its partition column here and in
                # GROUP_BY, such as job_runs.dt
                AS(count_rows(), "row_count"),
                # add a line like the next for each number to add up
                AS(sum_of(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<NUMBER_COLUMN>), <TOTAL_NAME>),
            ),
            FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>),
        ),
    )

"""ops.job_runs - one row per run of a job, on the day it ran."""

job_runs = Table(
    "ops.job_runs",
    columns={
        "run_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "status": "string",
        "duration_mins": "int",
        "avg_retry_secs": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["run_id"],
    does_not_add_up=["avg_retry_secs"],
)

minutes = job_runs.duration_mins
failed = equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED")

The file building_blocks/runs_per_job.py, after you fill it in

"""runs_per_job - each job's runs, failed runs and minutes for each dt it reads."""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.ops__job_runs import failed, job_runs, minutes  # failed, minutes: Aliases


def runs_per_job(reads: Condition) -> Table:
    """One row per job for each dt of the rows `reads` picks: its runs, failed runs and
    minutes."""
    return derived(
        "runs_per_job",
        statement(
            SELECT(
                job_runs.job_id,
                job_runs.dt,
                AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
                AS(count_rows(where=failed), "failed_runs"),
                AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes"),
            ),
            FROM(job_runs),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id, job_runs.dt),
        ),
    )

The calls run in a notebook in the Project's folder, which Jupyter puts on Python's path:

from references import ops

Done when from references import ops runs without an error. Then step 2.

2. Build a Statement to follow

Goal: a Statement whose result you want to trace. This one counts each team's runs, and their failed runs, over two days:

team_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(
        ops.jobs.team,
        AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
        AS(count_rows(where=equals(ops.job_runs.status, "FAILED")), "failed_runs"),
    ),
    FROM(ops.job_runs),
    JOIN(ops.jobs, ON=equals(ops.jobs.job_id, ops.job_runs.job_id)),
    WHERE(between(ops.job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(ops.jobs.team),
)
run_query(team_runs, query)  # -> DataFrame
