3. Export its Lineage

Goal: the Statement's Lineage, as two files. export_lineage takes the Statement itself, its Hive and its result aside. It writes the two files and gives back their paths, the HTML page first. Nothing is sent to the warehouse: the Lineage is worked out from the Statement alone.

Where the files go, and their names, take one of two forms. Each name starts with <time>, the time it ran, such as 20260925-090000 for 09:00:00 on 2026-09-25, so each export gets a name of its own and the ones before stay as they were. The HTML page and the Markdown file share the name, ending .html and .md:

a Statement exported outside a Table notebook, as here, goes in a folder named lineage beside the notebook you run it from, at the Project's top for a notebook there, named <time>_<commit>_lineage_<notebook>_<variable>.md: <commit> is the git commit of your scripts, here pinned to 1a2b3c4 ("nogit" outside a git repository), <notebook> the notebook's name ("notebook" where the steps don't run in a saved one), and <variable> the name of the variable that holds the Statement, here team_runs. So put a Statement in a variable with a meaningful name before you export it;
a write of a Saved table goes in its Table folder's lineage/, wherever you run it, named <time>-<database>__<table>.md, such as 20260925-090000-mart__daily_job_runs.md, as step 6 shows.


to=, such as to="lineage/team_runs.html", names the files instead, replacing the last.

html_file, markdown_file = export_lineage(team_runs)  # -> tuple[Path, Path]

It writes lineage/20260925-090000_1a2b3c4_lineage_notebook_team_runs.md

# Lineage: team_runs

Made by `export_lineage`. The chart is written in Mermaid, which JupyterLab draws. Arrows run from where a value comes from to where it goes. A solid arrow carries a value. A dotted arrow carries a column into a condition, or runs from a condition to the step whose rows it decides (labelled "filters").

## Graph

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph g0["ops.jobs"]
    n0["ops.jobs.team<br/><small>string</small>"]
    n8["ops.jobs.job_id<br/><small>bigint</small><br/><small>in the Key</small>"]
  end
  subgraph g1["team_runs"]
    n1["team<br/><small>jobs.team</small><br/><small>in the Key</small>"]
    n2["runs<br/><small>count_rows()</small>"]
    n4["failed_runs<br/><small>count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, #quot;F…</small>"]
  end
  subgraph g2["ops.job_runs"]
    n3["ops.job_runs.status<br/><small>string</small>"]
    n6["ops.job_runs.dt<br/><small>string</small>"]
    n9["ops.job_runs.job_id<br/><small>bigint</small>"]
  end
  subgraph g3["filters on team_runs"]
    n5{{"WHERE in team_runs<br/><small>between(job_runs.dt, #quot;2026-09-23#quot;, #quot;2026-09…</small>"}}
    n7{{"JOIN ON in team_runs<br/><small>equals(jobs.job_id, job_runs.job_id)</small>"}}
  end
  n0 --> n1
  n3 --> n4
  n6 -.-> n5
  n8 -.-> n7
  n9 -.-> n7
  n5 -.->|filters| g1
  n7 -.->|filters| g1
```

## team_runs

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `runs`

Calculated in **team_runs** as `count_rows()`, which is `COUNT(*)`.

```text
team_runs.runs = count_rows()
└─ (no columns: it counts rows)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in team_runs: `between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)
- JOIN ON in team_runs: `equals(jobs.job_id, job_runs.job_id)`, which is `jobs.job_id = job_runs.job_id` (reads ops.job_runs.job_id, ops.jobs.job_id)

One value for each different `jobs.team`.

#### `failed_runs`

Calculated in **team_runs** as `count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"))`, which is `COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END)`.

```text
team_runs.failed_runs = count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"))
└─ ops.job_runs.status  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in team_runs: `between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)
- JOIN ON in team_runs: `equals(jobs.job_id, job_runs.job_id)`, which is `jobs.job_id = job_runs.job_id` (reads ops.job_runs.job_id, ops.jobs.job_id)

One value for each different `jobs.team`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `team` | ops.jobs.team |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- team_runs: Key (team), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each team.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  COUNT(*) AS runs,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END) AS failed_runs
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = job_runs.job_id
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  jobs.team
```

filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

"""mart.daily_job_runs - one row per job and day: runs, failed runs and minutes."""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

daily_job_runs = Table(
    "mart.daily_job_runs",
    columns={
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "runs": "bigint",
        "failed_runs": "bigint",
        "minutes": "bigint",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["job_id", "dt"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/daily_job_runs",
)

The file tables/mart__daily_job_runs/mart__daily_job_runs.ipynb, after you fill it in

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.daily_job_runs
#
# Each job's runs, failed runs and minutes per day. Where the saved_table Template counts
# the rows READS picks, its rows come from the Building block runs_per_job, given READS.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    FROM, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, Clause, Statement, create_table, equals, statement,
)
from building_blocks.runs_per_job import runs_per_job
from references.mart__daily_job_runs import daily_job_runs
from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs

# The rows write() reads: change it to write others.
READS = equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.daily_job_runs from its Table reference, if it isn't there."""
    return create_table(daily_job_runs, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> list[Clause]:
    """The clauses preview and write share: each job's numbers, from the Building block."""
    per_job = runs_per_job(READS)
    return [
        SELECT(per_job.job_id, per_job.runs, per_job.failed_runs, per_job.minutes,
               per_job.dt),
        FROM(per_job),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows())


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3: save the rows, replacing the Partitions they fill."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(daily_job_runs), *rows())

## write_daily_job_runs

It writes the Saved table mart.daily_job_runs, replacing each partition its rows fill.

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `runs_per_job.runs`

Calculated in **runs_per_job** as `count_rows()`, which is `COUNT(*)`.

```text
runs_per_job.runs = count_rows()
└─ (no columns: it counts rows)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in runs_per_job: `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.job_id` and `job_runs.dt`.

#### `runs_per_job.failed_runs`

Calculated in **runs_per_job** as `count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"))`, which is `COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END)`.

```text
runs_per_job.failed_runs = count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"))
└─ ops.job_runs.status  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in runs_per_job: `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.job_id` and `job_runs.dt`.

#### `runs_per_job.minutes`

Calculated in **runs_per_job** as `sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins)`, which is `SUM(job_runs.duration_mins)`.

```text
runs_per_job.minutes = sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins)
└─ ops.job_runs.duration_mins  (int)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in runs_per_job: `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.job_id` and `job_runs.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `job_id` | runs_per_job.job_id ← ops.job_runs.job_id |
| `runs` | runs_per_job.runs (calculated) |
| `failed_runs` | runs_per_job.failed_runs (calculated) ← ops.job_runs.status |
| `minutes` | runs_per_job.minutes (calculated) ← ops.job_runs.duration_mins |
| `dt` | runs_per_job.dt ← ops.job_runs.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('runs_per_job', ...): Key (job_id), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt, with dt held to one value.
- write_daily_job_runs: Key (job_id), since runs_per_job's rows are told apart by job_id.
- mart.daily_job_runs' Key (job_id, dt) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH runs_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS runs,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END) AS failed_runs,
    SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  WHERE
    job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.daily_job_runs PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  runs_per_job.job_id,
  runs_per_job.runs,
  runs_per_job.failed_runs,
  runs_per_job.minutes,
  runs_per_job.dt
FROM runs_per_job
```

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.daily_job_runs.job_id.

Its tables: daily_job_runs (references/mart__daily_job_runs.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__daily_job_runs import daily_job_runs

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["daily_job_runs"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

project.reference_from_logic("mart.team_day") writes this file again from the table's logic,
filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

"""mart.team_day - one row per team and day: runs, failed runs and minutes."""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

team_day = Table(
    "mart.team_day",
    columns={
        "team": "string",
        "runs": "bigint",
        "failed_runs": "bigint",
        "minutes": "bigint",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],

)

The file tables/mart__team_day/mart__team_day.ipynb, after you fill it in

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.team_day
#
# Each team's runs, failed runs and minutes per day, added up from mart.daily_job_runs.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, JOIN, SELECT, WHERE, Clause, Statement,
    create_table, equals, statement, sum_of,
)
from references.mart__daily_job_runs import daily_job_runs
from references.mart__team_day import team_day
from references.ops__jobs import jobs

# The rows write() reads: change it to write others.
READS = equals(daily_job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.team_day from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(team_day, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> list[Clause]:
    """The clauses preview and write share: each team's numbers, from daily_job_runs."""
    return [
        SELECT(
            jobs.team,
            AS(sum_of(daily_job_runs.runs), "runs"),
            AS(sum_of(daily_job_runs.failed_runs), "failed_runs"),
            AS(sum_of(daily_job_runs.minutes), "minutes"),
            daily_job_runs.dt,
        ),
        FROM(daily_job_runs),
        JOIN(jobs, ON=equals(jobs.job_id, daily_job_runs.job_id)),
        WHERE(READS),
        GROUP_BY(jobs.team, daily_job_runs.dt),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows())


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3: save the rows, once mart.daily_job_runs has written them."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(team_day), *rows())

# %%
# 3. Try it: the Hive of each step. Its preview reads mart.daily_job_runs, which only
# your warehouse holds, so it runs at work: run_query(preview(), query)
show_hive([create(), write()])

# %%
# 4. Lineage: both tables together. project.load gives the other notebook's logic, so its
# write() can be drawn here too, each in a variable named for it.
daily = project.load("mart.daily_job_runs")
write_daily_job_runs = daily.write()
write_team_day = write()
export_lineage(write_daily_job_runs, write_team_day)

# %%
# 5. Its Hive: mart__team_day.hql beside this notebook, from the logic as last saved
export_hive()

# %%
# 6. The Reference sheet: this table's rows, from references/mart__team_day.py
project.refresh_reference_sheet("mart.team_day")

One write goes in its own Table folder's lineage/ wherever you run it, but with several writes the folder you run from decides where they go: the lineage/ of the Table folder you run them from, when one of them fills its table, or else beside your notebook. ## write_daily_job_runs

It writes the Saved table mart.daily_job_runs, replacing each partition its rows fill.


Rows that count:
- WHERE in runs_per_job: `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.job_id` and `job_runs.dt`.

#### `runs_per_job.failed_runs`

Calculated in **runs_per_job** as `count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"))`, which is `COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END)`.

```text
runs_per_job.failed_runs = count_rows(where=equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED"))
└─ ops.job_runs.status  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in runs_per_job: `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.job_id` and `job_runs.dt`.

#### `runs_per_job.minutes`

Calculated in **runs_per_job** as `sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins)`, which is `SUM(job_runs.duration_mins)`.

```text
runs_per_job.minutes = sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins)
└─ ops.job_runs.duration_mins  (int)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in runs_per_job: `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.job_id` and `job_runs.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `job_id` | runs_per_job.job_id ← ops.job_runs.job_id |
| `runs` | runs_per_job.runs (calculated) |
| `failed_runs` | runs_per_job.failed_runs (calculated) ← ops.job_runs.status |
| `minutes` | runs_per_job.minutes (calculated) ← ops.job_runs.duration_mins |
| `dt` | runs_per_job.dt ← ops.job_runs.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('runs_per_job', ...): Key (job_id), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt, with dt held to one value.
- write_daily_job_runs: Key (job_id), since runs_per_job's rows are told apart by job_id.
- mart.daily_job_runs' Key (job_id, dt) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH runs_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS runs,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END) AS failed_runs,
    SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  WHERE
    job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.daily_job_runs PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  runs_per_job.job_id,
  runs_per_job.runs,
  runs_per_job.failed_runs,
  runs_per_job.minutes,
  runs_per_job.dt
FROM runs_per_job
```

#### `runs`

```text
└─ mart.daily_job_runs.runs  (bigint)

```

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.team_day PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.failed_runs) AS failed_runs,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.minutes) AS minutes,
  daily_job_runs.dt
FROM mart.daily_job_runs AS daily_job_runs
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = daily_job_runs.job_id
WHERE
  daily_job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  jobs.team,
  daily_job_runs.dt
```

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.daily_job_runs.job_id.

Its tables: daily_job_runs (references/mart__daily_job_runs.py), team_day
(references/mart__team_day.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__daily_job_runs import daily_job_runs
from references.mart__team_day import team_day

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["daily_job_runs", "team_day"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

"""mart.run_speed - one row per run: its minutes, and how many over its job's usual run."""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

run_speed = Table(
    "mart.run_speed",
    columns={
        "run_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "duration_mins": "int",
        "minutes_over_median": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["run_id", "dt"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/run_speed",
)

The file tables/mart__run_speed/mart__run_speed.ipynb, after you fill it in

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.run_speed
#
# Each run of a day, with how many minutes longer it took than its job's usual run. pandas
# works that out, so cell 3 pulls the runs, changes them and saves them with your own call;
# write() gives the Python step that declares the change.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
import pandas as pd

from sqlglot_composer import (
    FROM, SELECT, WHERE, PythonStep, Statement, create_table, equals, python_step,
    statement,
)
from references.mart__run_speed import run_speed
from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs

# The rows the change reads: change it to write others.
READS = equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.run_speed from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(run_speed, may_exist=True)


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows the change reads, pulled with a Statement."""
    return statement(
        SELECT(job_runs.run_id, job_runs.job_id, job_runs.duration_mins, job_runs.dt),
        FROM(job_runs),
        WHERE(READS),
    )


def add_minutes_over_median(runs: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """The minutes each run took over its job's median run, its usual one."""
    median = runs.groupby("job_id")["duration_mins"].transform("median")
    return runs.assign(minutes_over_median=runs["duration_mins"] - median)


def write() -> PythonStep:
    """Step 3: what writes mart.run_speed: the change above, which your own call saves."""
    return python_step(
        add_minutes_over_median,
        reads=[job_runs.run_id, job_runs.job_id, job_runs.duration_mins, job_runs.dt],
        writes=run_speed,
    )

# %%
# 3. Pull the rows, change them in pandas, and save them with your own call
show_hive([create(), write()])
runs = run_query(preview(), query)
changed = add_minutes_over_median(runs)
# at work, your own call saves them, once create() has made the table:
# my_api.write_table(changed, "mart.run_speed", partition_by=["dt"])
changed

# %%
# 4. Lineage: the Python step, drawn as one box
export_lineage(write())

# %%
# 5. Its Hive: mart__run_speed.hql beside this notebook, from the logic as last saved
export_hive()

# %%
# 6. The Reference sheet: this table's rows, from references/mart__run_speed.py
project.refresh_reference_sheet("mart.run_speed")

# Lineage: write_team_day

Made by `export_lineage`. The chart is written in Mermaid, which JupyterLab draws. Arrows run from where a value comes from to where it goes. A solid arrow carries a value. A dotted arrow carries a column into a condition, or runs from a condition to the step whose rows it decides (labelled "filters").

## Graph

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph g0["ops.jobs"]
    n0["ops.jobs.team<br/><small>string</small>"]
```

## write_team_day

It writes the Saved table mart.team_day, replacing each partition its rows fill.

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `runs`

Calculated in **write_team_day** as `sum_of(daily_job_runs.runs)`, which is `SUM(daily_job_runs.runs)`.

```text
write_team_day.runs = sum_of(daily_job_runs.runs)
└─ mart.daily_job_runs.runs  (bigint)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in write_team_day: `equals(daily_job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `daily_job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads mart.daily_job_runs.dt)
- JOIN ON in write_team_day: `equals(jobs.job_id, daily_job_runs.job_id)`, which is `jobs.job_id = daily_job_runs.job_id` (reads mart.daily_job_runs.job_id, ops.jobs.job_id)

One value for each different `jobs.team` and `daily_job_runs.dt`.

#### `failed_runs`

Calculated in **write_team_day** as `sum_of(daily_job_runs.failed_runs)`, which is `SUM(daily_job_runs.failed_runs)`.

```text
write_team_day.failed_runs = sum_of(daily_job_runs.failed_runs)
└─ mart.daily_job_runs.failed_runs  (bigint)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in write_team_day: `equals(daily_job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `daily_job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads mart.daily_job_runs.dt)
- JOIN ON in write_team_day: `equals(jobs.job_id, daily_job_runs.job_id)`, which is `jobs.job_id = daily_job_runs.job_id` (reads mart.daily_job_runs.job_id, ops.jobs.job_id)

One value for each different `jobs.team` and `daily_job_runs.dt`.

#### `minutes`

Calculated in **write_team_day** as `sum_of(daily_job_runs.minutes)`, which is `SUM(daily_job_runs.minutes)`.

```text
write_team_day.minutes = sum_of(daily_job_runs.minutes)
└─ mart.daily_job_runs.minutes  (bigint)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in write_team_day: `equals(daily_job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")`, which is `daily_job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'` (reads mart.daily_job_runs.dt)
- JOIN ON in write_team_day: `equals(jobs.job_id, daily_job_runs.job_id)`, which is `jobs.job_id = daily_job_runs.job_id` (reads mart.daily_job_runs.job_id, ops.jobs.job_id)

One value for each different `jobs.team` and `daily_job_runs.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `team` | ops.jobs.team |
| `dt` | mart.daily_job_runs.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- write_team_day: Key (team), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each team, dt, with dt held to one value.
- mart.team_day's Key (team, dt) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.team_day PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.failed_runs) AS failed_runs,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.minutes) AS minutes,
  daily_job_runs.dt
FROM mart.daily_job_runs AS daily_job_runs
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = daily_job_runs.job_id
WHERE
  daily_job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  jobs.team,
  daily_job_runs.dt
```

8. Keep the Project in order Getting started

Goal

Keep your Project's files and its tables in step as they change. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout, and a Project action is a one-time change to its files, such as project.new_table. The registry is the Project's list of every Table folder, Table reference, Building block and Conversion folder a Project action made: project.check() says what it gets wrong, and project.register adopts a file made by hand. A Table reference is the file that describes one table; a Table folder holds everything about one Saved table, a table your Statements write; a Building block is a piece of your own code reused across notebooks. The Reference sheet lists every column of every table in one CSV file. admin/check_references.ipynb compares each Table reference with its table in the warehouse. The project.remove_* actions archive what you no longer need, deleting nothing. And the Levels, the three tiers of your own files, say which of your files may import which.

When you'd use it

If you copy in a file by hand, such as a colleague's Table reference, run project.check() and adopt it with project.register (steps 3 and 4).
If you move or delete a file by hand, run project.check(): it says the call that mends the registry.
If you look for a column and don't know which table has it, or someone new asks what the Project's tables hold, read the Reference sheet (steps 6 and 7).
If a table's owners say it changed, or on a schedule, say every Monday, check each Table reference against its table (steps 8 and 9): a table goes on changing after its Table reference is written, and a Table reference out of date lets through Statements the warehouse then refuses.
If a Saved table, a Table reference or a Building block is no longer needed, archive it (step 10).


Steps

1. Set up the Project the last how-to left

Goal: a Project with Table references, a Building block and a Saved table to keep in order.

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes building_blocks/runs_per_job.py

"""runs_per_job - TODO: say in one line what one row of it is.

Fill in every <...> in the code below this text, brackets and all: Python stops at one left
in. They are:
    <SOURCE_NAME> and <SOURCE_TABLE>: the table it reads, by its file in references/ and its
        name there, such as ops__job_runs and job_runs.
    <GROUP_COLUMN>: the column each row is for, such as job_id.
    <NUMBER_COLUMN>: a column of numbers to add up, such as duration_mins.
    <TOTAL_NAME>: the name of what it adds up to, in quotes, such as "minutes".

Why: TODO: which tables' notebooks read it, so they can never work it out two ways.

project.new_block("runs_per_job") wrote this file from admin/templates/building_block.py. A
Building block is a piece several notebooks share; this one is a Derived table, a Statement
given a name, which each Statement reading it writes at its top, as WITH runs_per_job AS (...). In a
notebook's logic cell, import it, and read it like a table, given the rows it reads, such
as READS, the condition at the top of a saved_table notebook's logic cell:

    from building_blocks.runs_per_job import runs_per_job


    def preview() -> Statement:
        per_group = runs_per_job(READS)
        return statement(SELECT(per_group.<GROUP_COLUMN>, per_group.row_count),
                         FROM(per_group))
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>  # the Table reference it reads


def runs_per_job(reads: Condition) -> Table:  # named as its file is
    """One row per group of the rows `reads` picks: its rows, and its total.

    `reads` is a condition on the table it reads, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24").
    On a partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns.
    """
    return derived(
        "runs_per_job",  # the name the Hive gives it, the same as its file's
        statement(
            SELECT(
                <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
                # to keep each Partition's rows apart, add its partition column here and in
                # GROUP_BY, such as job_runs.dt
                AS(count_rows(), "row_count"),
                # add a line like the next for each number to add up
                AS(sum_of(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<NUMBER_COLUMN>), <TOTAL_NAME>),
            ),
            FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>),
        ),
    )

"""mart.alerts_per_severity - TODO: say in one line what one row is.

filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

alerts_per_severity = Table(
    "mart.alerts_per_severity",
    columns={"<COLUMNS>": "string"},  # project.reference_from_logic writes them, from the logic
    location=None,  # TODO: its own folder, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/alerts_per_severity"
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = alerts_per_severity.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(alerts_per_severity.<column>, <value>)

It writes admin/registry.csv too.

"""ops.jobs - one row per job: its name, the team that owns it and its region."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

jobs = Table(
    "ops.jobs",
    columns={"job_id": "bigint", "job_name": "string", "team": "string", "region": "string"},
    key=["job_id"],
    does_not_add_up=[],
)

The file references/ops__job_runs.py, after you fill it in

"""ops.job_runs - one row per run of a job, on the day it ran."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table, equals

job_runs = Table(
    "ops.job_runs",
    columns={
        "run_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "status": "string",
        "duration_mins": "int",
        "avg_retry_secs": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["run_id"],
    does_not_add_up=["avg_retry_secs"],
    descriptions={"status": "SUCCESS / FAILED / TEST, NULL while running"},
)

minutes = job_runs.duration_mins
failed = equals(job_runs.status, "FAILED")

The file references/ops__run_alerts.py, after you fill it in

"""ops.run_alerts - one row per alert a run raised."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table, equals

run_alerts = Table(
    "ops.run_alerts",
    columns={"alert_id": "bigint", "run_id": "bigint", "severity": "string", "dt": "string"},
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["alert_id"],
    does_not_add_up=[],
    descriptions={"severity": "low / high"},
)

high = equals(run_alerts.severity, "high")

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.alerts_per_severity.severity.

Its tables: alerts_per_severity (references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["alerts_per_severity"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_runs.run_id.

Its tables: job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), run_alerts
(references/ops__run_alerts.py), scheduler_runs (references/ops__scheduler_runs.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__run_alerts import run_alerts
from references.ops__scheduler_runs import scheduler_runs

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_runs", "jobs", "run_alerts", "scheduler_runs"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes admin/registry.csv too.

project.register takes a table's name, with its database, for its Table folder or Table reference, and a Building block's name, with no dot, such as project.register("runs_per_job"), for its file in building_blocks/. It also wrote the Database modules again, so from references import ops now gives ops.scheduler_runs too. A Database module, such as references/ops.py, is the one file that gives each table of a Database, the first part of a table's name, ops in ops.jobs.

Done when it says Registered references/ops__scheduler_runs.py. Then step 5.

5. Keep each file to its Level

Goal: imports that never break a file at a lower Level. Your files sit at three Levels, and a file imports only from the Toolbox and from Levels below its own:

Level 0, references/, each Table reference and Database module, and warehouse.py: imports the Toolbox; a Database module also imports the Table references of its Database;
Level 1, building_blocks/, each Building block: imports the Toolbox and Level 0;
Level 2, each Table notebook's logic cell: imports the Toolbox, Level 0 and Level 1.


What runs many Table notebooks in order, such as admin/run_tables.ipynb, sits above the three, and may read them all. So a Table reference imports no Building block, and a Building block imports no other: a block that reads another takes the Derived table it gives back as an argument, from the notebook that imports both, as Share Building blocks says. A Derived table is a Statement given a name so another Statement can read from it. A file changed at one Level then never breaks one below it, and project.load can run any notebook's logic from anywhere in the Project. Nothing stops an import that breaks the Levels: keep to them as you write.

Done when each file you write imports only from the Toolbox and from Levels below its own. Then step 6.

6. Write the Reference sheet

Goal: references/reference_sheet.csv, written from your Table references. project.refresh_reference_sheet(), from admin/refresh_reference_sheet.ipynb, writes references/reference_sheet.csv from the Table references in references/, never from the warehouse, so it says what your files say:

project.refresh_reference_sheet()

Python shows
Wrote references/reference_sheet.csv: 20 rows, 5 tables.

It writes references/reference_sheet.csv too.

Done when it says it wrote 20 rows, 5 tables. Then step 7.

7. Read the Reference sheet

Goal: find a column, and the tables that have it. The sheet has a row per column of each table: the table and the column, its Hive type, "yes" under partition and in_key for each partition column and each column of the Key, the columns whose values name one row, its Aliases, the names you give a column or a condition in a Table reference (a condition shown as "failed (a condition)"), its description, and exported_at, when the sheet was written. Read it in a spreadsheet, or in pandas. Python's csv module keeps an empty cell as empty text, "", so sheet.aliases != "" picks the filled ones:

with open("references/reference_sheet.csv", encoding="utf-8", newline="") as file:
    sheet = pd.DataFrame(csv.DictReader(file))  # -> DataFrame

Every table with a column named job_id, to see where a join on it could go:

sheet[sheet.column == "job_id"][["table", "type", "in_key"]]

After you change one Table reference, project.refresh_reference_sheet("ops.job_runs") refreshes its rows alone, as a Table notebook's last cell does for its own table.

Done when you have the three tables with a job_id column, and the three Aliases. Then step 8.

8. Check each Table reference against its table

Goal: know whether each Table reference still matches its table in the warehouse. admin/check_references.ipynb compares every table the Reference sheet lists with the table in the warehouse, through your query: DESCRIBE FORMATTED, Hive's fuller DESCRIBE, lists the table's columns, their types and its partition columns, and never its rows. It prints what differs, with the line to change, then checks each Key it can. Run it in Jupyter, top to bottom. At its heart is check_table_reference, which gives back a Verdict, whose .ok says whether the check found nothing wrong; here, on each Table reference, with the Project's query from warehouse.py:

from references import mart, ops
for table in [ops.jobs, ops.job_runs, ops.run_alerts, mart.alerts_per_severity]:
    print(check_table_reference(table, query=query))

Python prints
ops.jobs matches its Table reference.
ops.job_runs matches its Table reference.
ops.run_alerts matches its Table reference.
mart.alerts_per_severity: DESCRIBE FORMATTED failed, so nothing was compared. Check the table's name, and that your query works. It said:
    ValueError:
    What happened:  The Example database has no table 'mart.alerts_per_severity'.
    Why it matters: It holds nine made-up tables, all in the database ops.
    Usual fix:      Use ops.jobs, ops.job_runs, ops.run_alerts, ops.scheduler_runs, ops.job_events, ops.job_owners, ops.region_costs, ops.region_budgets or ops.team_notes.
    Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

The Saved table reports that DESCRIBE FORMATTED failed: it exists only once its notebook's create() has made it in your warehouse, and the Example database, the small made-up tables this page runs on, holds none, so that is expected here, as the notebook reports for each Saved table of the Starter Example project. At work, a Saved table you have created is compared like the others.

Done when each table the warehouse holds says it matches its Table reference. Then step 9.

9. Read what the check says when a table changes

Goal: the line to add or change when a table changes under its Table reference. Say the team that owns ops.jobs adds a column, "owner". A stand-in query plays the warehouse after that change: it answers DESCRIBE FORMATTED with the new columns, and passes every other query on:

def query_after_the_change(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    if hive.startswith("DESCRIBE"):
        return pd.DataFrame(
            [("job_id", "bigint", ""), ("job_name", "string", ""), ("team", "string", ""),
             ("region", "string", ""), ("owner", "string", "")],
            columns=["col_name", "data_type", "comment"])
    return query(hive)
check_table_reference(ops.jobs, query=query_after_the_change)  # -> Verdict

Python shows
ops.jobs matches its Table reference.
Notes:
  - owner is in the table but not in the Table reference: add "owner": "string", if you want it.

It still matches, since a Note may not matter: a column the Table reference doesn't list is just not there for your Statements. A Problem, listed under Problems, such as a column gone or a type changed, makes Statements wrong or the warehouse refuse them: fix every one, writing the line it gives into the Table reference. The check leaves your file as it is, so you write the line. Then refresh the table's rows in the Reference sheet.

If the check lists a Note, add the line it gives if you want the column; your Statements stay right either way.
If the check lists a Problem, write the line it gives into the Table reference, then refresh the table's rows in the Reference sheet.


Done when the check lists the Note about owner. Then step 10.

"""mart.job_minutes - TODO: say in one line what one row is.

project.reference_from_logic("mart.job_minutes") writes this file again from the table's logic,
filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

job_minutes = Table(
    "mart.job_minutes",
    columns={"<COLUMNS>": "string"},  # project.reference_from_logic writes them, from the logic
    location=None,  # TODO: its own folder, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/job_minutes"
)

project.remove_block archives a Building block the same way, from admin/remove.ipynb. Each refuses while something still needs what it archives: project.remove_reference while the table has a Table folder, whose notebook imports it, and project.remove_block while a Table notebook or another Building block imports the block, naming each. So archive the Table folder first, then its Table reference, as here.

Done when both are moved to admin/removed/. Then step 11.

11. Drop a table you created, with an Admin statement

Goal: a table you created gone from the warehouse, once you mean it. Nothing is deleted, and nothing in the warehouse changes: a table you created stays there until you drop it with an Admin statement, a Statement that changes one of your Saved tables in the warehouse, such as dropping it. The admin module makes them; import it by name, since the star import leaves it out:

from sqlglot_composer import admin

admin.drop_table(...) gives the one that drops a table, which you send with run_execute(..., execute), as admin/workflows/change_a_saved_table/change_a_saved_table.ipynb does. help(admin) lists every Admin statement.

Done when from sqlglot_composer import admin runs; this page drops nothing, since mart.job_minutes was never created. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

The registry lists the archived table and reference as removed, and project.check() finds nothing amiss:

registry = pd.read_csv("admin/registry.csv", keep_default_na=False)  # -> DataFrame
registry[registry.removed_at != ""][["kind", "table"]]

Python shows

kindtable

project.check()

Every Table folder, Table reference and Building block is registered, and each one registered is there.

Before you go on, check that:

the registry marks mart.job_minutes' Table folder and Table reference removed;
project.check() says every one is registered and there;
references/reference_sheet.csv lists ops.scheduler_runs, the file you adopted;
admin/removed/ holds mart__job_minutes' folder and file.


Common mistakes

Deleting a Table folder by hand

A folder deleted or moved by hand is still in the registry. project.check() says so, with the call that marks it removed:

import shutil
shutil.rmtree("tables/mart__alerts_per_severity")
project.check()

Python shows
Registered, but not there. Put each back, or mark it removed with the call beside it:
  tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/: project.remove_table("mart.alerts_per_severity")

project.remove_table("mart.alerts_per_severity")

Python shows
tables/mart__alerts_per_severity/ wasn't there, so only its registry row is marked removed.

Editing the Reference sheet by hand

Each refresh writes the sheet again from the Table references, so a change typed into the CSV file is gone the next time. Write a description in the Table reference's descriptions={...}, and an Alias below its Table(...) call, then refresh: the file is where it lives, and the sheet only shows it.

9. Convert someone else's Hive Getting started

Goal

Turn scripts of Hive someone else wrote, with their temporary tables, their FROMs with several INSERTs and their incremental INSERTs, into your own code. Hive is the SQL your warehouse runs. You end with a Saved table, a real table your own Statements write, in your Dev Database for each table the scripts write; your Dev Database is the database where you may create and write tables of your own. Each Saved table's notebook builds the same query from the Toolbox's functions, is compared with the table it copies, its original, and is then promoted to your Prod Database, the database others read your finished tables from. That is a Conversion, and the convert Workflow, an ordered list of steps in a notebook of its own, takes you through it, step by step. What no Toolbox function writes yet stays Hive, at the smallest size it can, so you get the rest converted rather than none of it.

When you'd use it

If you inherit Hive that works but that you can't maintain, such as a nightly job another team wrote or a folder of scripts that has grown for years, convert it as the steps below do. Converted, the Guards check it, Lineage traces it, and you change it in one place, dev, before promoting it.
If one script writes a table another reads, convert both in one batch, as step 3 does.
If you have no Project yet, do Start a Project first.
If you have not yet written a Saved table of your own, read Save a table first.


Steps

1. Start from the Project the story leaves

Goal: a Project to convert into. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout. Work in your own Project. This page uses a copy of the Starter Example project, the one the how-tos before this one build, whose Dev Database, DEV in admin/databases.py, is mart (yours is any database you own, such as mart_dev):

import shutil
from pathlib import Path
import sqlglot_composer
download = Path(sqlglot_composer.__file__).parents[1]  # -> Path
_ = shutil.copytree(download / "example_projects" / "starter", ".", dirs_exist_ok=True)

from sqlglot_composer import example_database, project, run_execute, hive_settings

Done when from warehouse import query, execute runs without an error. Then step 2.

2. Save the scripts

Goal: the scripts where the convert Workflow reads them. Save each script as it is, a .hql file beside the convert Workflow's notebook, in admin/workflows/convert/. Here the ops team's two: the nightly one keeps the day's failed runs in a temporary table, then counts the day's alerts two ways from one FROM; the weekly one adds each failed job to a history the job reads itself:

The file admin/workflows/convert/daily.hql, after you save it

SET hive.exec.parallel=true;
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS ops_reports.failed_runs;
CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE ops_reports.failed_runs AS
SELECT r.run_id, r.job_id, r.dt
FROM ops.job_runs r
WHERE r.dt = '2026-09-24' AND r.status = 'FAILED';
FROM ops.run_alerts a
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE ops_reports.alerts_by_severity PARTITION (dt='2026-09-24')
  SELECT a.severity, COUNT(*) AS alerts WHERE a.dt = '2026-09-24' GROUP BY a.severity
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE ops_reports.alerted_runs PARTITION (dt='2026-09-24')
  SELECT a.run_id, COUNT(*) AS alerts WHERE a.dt = '2026-09-24' GROUP BY a.run_id;

The file admin/workflows/convert/weekly.hql, after you save it

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS ops_reports.fail_history (job_id BIGINT, fails BIGINT)
PARTITIONED BY (dt STRING) STORED AS ORC;
INSERT INTO TABLE ops_reports.fail_history PARTITION (dt='2026-09-24')
SELECT f.job_id, COUNT(*) AS fails
FROM ops_reports.failed_runs f
WHERE f.job_id NOT IN (SELECT h.job_id FROM ops_reports.fail_history h WHERE h.dt = '2026-09-24')
GROUP BY f.job_id;

Done when both .hql files are in admin/workflows/convert/. Then step 3.

3. Convert them

Goal: your own code for each table the scripts write. Open admin/workflows/convert/convert.ipynb and fill in its names cell: the batch's name as NAME, a plain lowercase one, the scripts as FILES, and DATABASE, the database of a table the Hive names without one. A batch is the scripts converted together by one Conversion. Save the cell, and run the first step's cell, which is this call, from the notebook's folder. rename= gives a table a name of your own in dev; left out, ops_reports.fail_history would be mart.fail_history_ops_reports, its database at the end:

import os
os.chdir("admin/workflows/convert")  # where the notebook runs: this page stands in
print(project.convert("ops_reports", files=["daily.hql", "weekly.hql"], query=query,
                      database="ops", rename={"ops_reports.fail_history": "fail_history"}))

Python prints
Converted ops_reports: 2 scripts, 7 statements, into 3 Saved tables in mart: mart.alerts_by_severity_ops_reports, mart.alerted_runs_ops_reports and mart.fail_history.
Folded ops_reports.failed_runs, each a staging table, a Derived table in the tables that read it: batch.md says where.
admin/workflows/convert/ops_reports/batch.md lists each statement, in the order they run, and what it became.
Next: answer each table's open Decisions in the decisions cell of admin/workflows/convert/convert.ipynb, then run its next steps: write the answers in, create each table in dev, seed each that reads itself, write each, and compare each with its original.

Done when project.convert prints Converted ops_reports: .... Then step 4.

4. Read what each statement became

Goal: know what the Conversion made of each statement. The scripts' order doesn't matter: the batch orders each statement by what reads what. Each table the scripts write is a Saved table in mart, with its Table folder, the folder that holds everything about one Saved table, its Table reference, the file that describes the table's columns, Partitions and Key, and its Decisions to answer. The one exception is the temporary table: a Staging table, a table a script writes only so a statement after it can read it. The weekly script reads it, so it is folded into fail_history, its query a Derived table there, a named query that lives only inside the Statement that reads it, and never made a table of its own. To keep it a table instead, give staging={"ops_reports.failed_runs": "keep"}. The FROM with two INSERTs gives each target a Saved table of its own. The DROP becomes nothing, and the SET line becomes the Session settings of the writes after it: a Session setting is a Hive SET sent before a Statement. admin/workflows/convert/ops_reports/batch.md lists what each statement became:

print(Path("admin/workflows/convert/ops_reports/batch.md").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))

Python prints
# Batch ops_reports

2 scripts, kept as given in scripts/: daily.hql and weekly.hql. Each table they write is a Saved table in the Dev Database, mart; one they only create has its Table reference alone.

## Tables

- ops_reports.alerts_by_severity: mart.alerts_by_severity_ops_reports, tables/mart__alerts_by_severity_ops_reports/, its checklist tables/mart__alerts_by_severity_ops_reports/conversion/checklist.md
- ops_reports.alerted_runs: mart.alerted_runs_ops_reports, tables/mart__alerted_runs_ops_reports/, its checklist tables/mart__alerted_runs_ops_reports/conversion/checklist.md
- ops_reports.fail_history: mart.fail_history, tables/mart__fail_history/, its checklist tables/mart__fail_history/conversion/checklist.md

## Each statement, in the order they run

1. daily.hql line 1, `SET hive.exec.parallel=true`: the Session settings of the writes after it in its script
2. daily.hql line 2, `DROP TABLE IF EXISTS ops_reports.failed_runs`: folded into ops_reports.fail_history: a Derived table there, never created, written, compared or promoted
3. daily.hql line 3, `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE ops_reports.failed_runs AS SELECT r.run_id, r.job_id, ...`: folded into ops_reports.fail_history: a Derived table there, never created, written, compared or promoted
4. daily.hql line 7, `FROM ops.run_alerts a INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE ops_reports.alerts_by_severity P...`: mart.alerts_by_severity_ops_reports, written by its notebook (was one target of a FROM with several INSERTs)
5. daily.hql line 7, `FROM ops.run_alerts a INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE ops_reports.alerts_by_severity P...`: mart.alerted_runs_ops_reports, written by its notebook (was one target of a FROM with several INSERTs)
6. weekly.hql line 1, `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS ops_reports.fail_history (job_id BIGINT, fails BIG...`: the columns, types and Partitions of mart.fail_history's Table reference
7. weekly.hql line 3, `INSERT INTO TABLE ops_reports.fail_history PARTITION (dt='2026-09-24') SELECT...`: mart.fail_history, written by its notebook

Open tables/mart__alerted_runs_ops_reports/mart__alerted_runs_ops_reports.ipynb. Its logic cell holds your code: the query's clauses, and create(), preview() and write(). mart.fail_history's stays Hive, whole, an Unchecked statement, Hive text run through the Toolbox that no Guard looks inside: its WHERE holds a query, NOT IN (SELECT ...), which no Toolbox function writes yet, as its conversion/scan.md says. Its write reads its own table, as an incremental INSERT does, so in dev it would start from nothing: its notebook has seed() too, which copies the original's rows into it once, before its first write.

Done when batch.md starts # Batch ops_reports and you have read what each statement became. Then step 5.

5. Read each table's open Decisions

Goal: know what only you can decide. What only you can decide of a table is a Decision: here each table's location=, the folder its files go in, which your platform team names, and its Key, the columns whose values name one row. Each table's conversion/checklist.md lists its open Decisions, and project.check() names each one:

checklist = Path("tables/mart__fail_history/conversion/checklist.md")
text = checklist.read_text(encoding="utf-8")  # -> str
print(text.split("Open Decisions")[1].split("## Notes")[0].strip())

Python prints
- key: the columns whose values name one row, or None when none do, in references/mart__fail_history.py
- location: the folder its files go in, in references/mart__fail_history.py

Done when you have the list of each table's open Decisions. Then step 6.

6. Answer each Decision in the decisions cell

Goal: an answer for each open Decision. Write each answer in the convert Workflow notebook's decisions cell, DECISIONS, by each table's name as project.check() writes it. You open no Table reference, and tick nothing. "key": None says no columns name one row. A Key project.convert could work out of a table's query, as GROUP BY a.severity gives one, is proposed in its Table reference already, marked # proposed, and its checklist says so: answer "key" only where one is open or wrong. A TODO line left, such as does_not_add_up=, waits on nothing. This page stands in for the decisions cell:

DECISIONS = {table: {"location": f"/data/mart.db/{table.split('.')[1]}"}
             for table in project.batch_tables("ops_reports")}

fail_history's write reads its own table, so no Key could be worked out of it: its Key is open. Here the answer is that no columns name one row of it.

DECISIONS["mart.fail_history"]["key"] = None

Two tables write the Partition dt from a query that doesn't say its type, so each one's dt is a guessed type to answer too: the day as text, "string". A Partition is a slice of a table: the rows that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.

for table in ["mart.alerts_by_severity_ops_reports", "mart.alerted_runs_ops_reports"]:
    DECISIONS[table]["types"] = {"dt": "string"}

Done when DECISIONS holds a location for each table, a key for mart.fail_history, and a dt type for the other two. Then step 7.

the batch is in admin/workflows/convert/finished/;
the promote Workflow has written each table in prod.


Common mistakes

Converting one script at a time

A table one script writes and another reads is made once, and read in dev, only when both are in one batch. Give the scripts together, with files=.

Leaving a Decision open

The Workflow waits on each table's open Decisions, each one only you can make, such as a table's folder. Answer each one project.check() names in the decisions cell.

Promoting before comparing

promote refuses a table whose compare hasn't passed, so prod never gets rows that differ from the original's. Compare each table, and fix it in dev until its compare passes, then promote.

Next

Follow a Workflow: every Workflow's steps, drawn and listed.
Hand-written Hive with your Table references: the Unchecked pieces a Conversion leaves, and how to replace them.


Toolbox names used: run_execute, hive_settings, project.check, project.load, project.run_tables, project.convert, project.batch_tables, project.compare_batch, project.promote, project.write_decisions



Intermediate


10. Describe a table by hand Intermediate

Goal

Write a Table reference yourself, one argument at a time, for a table project.add_references can't ask about, and keep it in the Project's references/ folder. A Table reference is the file that describes one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key. Here the table is mart.job_day, a table that doesn't exist yet, with one row per job per day. Along the way you see what each argument of Table is for: the columns and their types, partitioned_by=, key=, does_not_add_up= and location=, and how to describe a table partitioned by two columns, whose days are written another way.

When you'd use it

If a table doesn't exist yet, such as one your own Python will write, or one another team is about to make, write its Table reference by hand: there is nothing to DESCRIBE, so you write what it will hold.
If you fill in the Table reference project.new_table writes for a Saved table, write it by hand in the same way, as Save a table shows.
If you know a table better than the warehouse does, fill in what DESCRIBE never holds, its Key and the columns that don't add up, even in a Table reference project.add_references writes, as Add your tables' references shows.
If you have not yet done the Getting started how-tos, do Start a Project and Add your tables' references first.


Steps

1. Copy the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout, and an Example project is one made for the Example database's tables, to read and copy from. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, and runs its steps there:

Done when the folder holds the Intermediate Example project's files. Then step 2.

2. Import the Toolbox and your query

Goal: every name the steps call. Import the Toolbox, the project module, the Project's ops Database and your query. A Database is a named group of tables, the first part of a table's name, ops in ops.jobs:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
from references import ops
from warehouse import query, execute

Done when the four imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Decide what one row is

Goal: the sentence that decides the columns and the Key. Before you write anything, say in one sentence what one row of the table is. For mart.job_day it is "one job on one day": how many times the job ran that day, how many of those runs failed, their minutes in all, and the average minutes of a run. Each of those becomes a column, and the sentence decides the Key, the columns whose values name exactly one row.

Done when you have the sentence, and the columns it names. Then step 4.

4. Write the Table reference in a file

Goal: one Table reference every notebook of the Project imports. A Table reference is one call to Table, kept in the Project's references/ folder, in a file named after its database and table joined by two underscores:

The file references/mart__job_day.py, after you write it

"""mart.job_day - one row per job and day: runs, failed runs, minutes and the average."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

job_day = Table(
    "mart.job_day",
    columns={
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "runs": "bigint",
        "failed_runs": "bigint",
        "minutes": "bigint",
        "avg_minutes": "double",  # minutes / runs: the average run that day
        "dt": "string",  # the day of the runs, as 2026-09-24
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["job_id", "dt"],
    does_not_add_up=["avg_minutes"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day",
)

What each argument says:

"mart.job_day" is the table's name as the warehouse knows it: the database, a dot, then the table.
columns= maps each column's name to its Hive type, spelled as DESCRIBE prints it: "bigint" for whole numbers, "string" for text, "double" for numbers with a decimal point, "decimal(10,2)" for exact amounts such as money. The Toolbox checks the values you compare a typed column with, so comparing job_day.runs with "3" stops, since "3" is text.
partitioned_by=["dt"] lists the columns the table is split into Partitions by, in the order Hive partitions by them: here one, the day. A Partition is a slice of the table: the rows that share one value of each of those columns. Each is a column in columns= too. The warehouse stores a Partition at a time, and every Statement that reads the table must filter at least one of them. A table that isn't partitioned, such as ops.team_notes, leaves it out.
key=["job_id", "dt"] lists the columns that pick out one row of the table. A job has a row on every day, so job_day.job_id alone repeats; the job and the day together pick out one row. JOIN reads the Key to warn you when a join could repeat rows. A table with no Key, such as ops.team_notes, says key=None, and nothing is checked against one.
does_not_add_up=["avg_minutes"] lists the columns that are averages, ratios or counts of different values. Adding up two days' averages gives a number that means nothing, so the Toolbox refuses to add these up.
location="/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day" is the table's own folder, used exactly as you write it: the Toolbox adds nothing to it. Only a table you create yourself has one, and no two of your Saved tables may name one folder. A Saved table is a real table your own Statements write. A table someone else owns, such as ops.job_events, leaves it out.


Done when references/mart__job_day.py holds the call to Table above. Then step 5.

5. Register it

Goal: the Project knows the file. A file you write by hand isn't in the Project's Registry yet, the Project's list of every file a Project action made. project.check() lists it as made by hand, with the call that adopts it:

project.check()  # -> a report, printed

Python shows
Not registered, made by hand. Adopt each with the call beside it:
  references/mart__job_day.py: project.register("mart.job_day")

project.register("mart.job_day")  # -> a report, printed
