It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.budget_overruns.job_id.

Its tables: budget_overruns (references/mart__budget_overruns.py), job_day
(references/mart__job_day.py), job_day_costs (references/mart__job_day_costs.py), job_day_facts
(references/mart__job_day_facts.py), job_events_checks (references/mart__job_events_checks.py),
job_notes (references/mart__job_notes.py), team_days (references/mart__team_days.py),
team_failures (references/mart__team_failures.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.mart__job_day import job_day
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.mart__job_events_checks import job_events_checks
from references.mart__job_notes import job_notes
from references.mart__team_days import team_days
from references.mart__team_failures import team_failures

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["budget_overruns", "job_day", "job_day_costs", "job_day_facts", "job_events_checks", "job_notes", "team_days", "team_failures"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes admin/registry.csv too.

Done when project.register prints Registered references/mart__job_day.py. Then step 6.

6. Import it from its Database

Goal: the table as an attribute of mart. project.register also writes references/mart.py again, the mart Database, so the table is one of its attributes:

from references import mart
job_day = mart.job_day  # -> Table
job_day

Python shows
Table('mart.job_day', columns: job_id, runs, failed_runs, minutes, avg_minutes, dt)

Done when job_day shows its six columns. Then step 7.

7. Read it in a Statement

Goal: a Statement, one query, that reads your table. A hand-written Table reference works exactly like one project.add_references wrote. Its columns are its attributes, and a Statement reads it with FROM:

long_days = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_day.job_id, job_day.dt, job_day.minutes),
    FROM(job_day),
    WHERE(between(job_day.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"), at_least(job_day.minutes, 30)),
)
text = show_hive(long_days)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_day.job_id,
  job_day.dt,
  job_day.minutes
FROM mart.job_day AS job_day
WHERE
  job_day.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24' AND job_day.minutes >= 30;

The table mart.job_day isn't on the Example database, so this how-to shows its Hive but can't run it. At work, once the table exists and holds some days, run_query(long_days, query) runs it.

Done when the Hive reads FROM mart.job_day AS job_day. Then step 8.

8. Read a table partitioned by two columns

Goal: a Statement on a table whose days are written another way. The Project's ops.region_costs, each job's cost in cents per region per day, is partitioned by two columns: the region, then the day. Its Key is the job, the region and the day. It writes its days like 20260924 rather than 2026-09-24. project.add_references wrote its Table reference, references/ops__region_costs.py, from its DESCRIBE, both partition columns among it, and its Key was filled in by hand:

partitioned_by=["region", "dt"],
key=["job_id", "region", "dt"],  # filled in by hand

The Toolbox compares a value with a column exactly as you write it, so write each day the table's way:

region_costs = ops.region_costs  # -> Table
costs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.cost_cents),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(equals(region_costs.dt, "20260923")),
)
run_query(costs, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idcost_cents

1120

2180




Filtering either partition column is enough for the Load limit, the check that refuses a Statement that would read too much: the step above filters the day alone, and one that filters the region alone, or both, is let through too.

Done when the result holds jobs 1 and 2. Then step 9.

9. Keep a range of days written another way

Goal: several days from the same table. Days written year first, as both 20260924 and 2026-09-24 are, sort in date order as text, so between works on them as on dates:

two_days = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.cost_cents),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, "20260923", "20260924")),
)
text = show_hive(two_days)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  region_costs.job_id,
  region_costs.cost_cents
FROM ops.region_costs AS region_costs
WHERE
  region_costs.dt BETWEEN '20260923' AND '20260924';

Done when the Hive's WHERE reads BETWEEN '20260923' AND '20260924'. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

Compare a table that already exists with its Table reference:

check_table_reference(region_costs, query=query)  # -> Verdict

Python shows
ops.region_costs matches its Table reference.

It matches, both partition columns in their order included.

The table mart.job_day doesn't exist yet. create_table writes the Hive that makes it from your Table reference: read it to check the columns, their types and the partition are what you meant.

text = show_hive(create_table(job_day))  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE mart.job_day (
  job_id BIGINT,
  runs BIGINT,
  failed_runs BIGINT,
  minutes BIGINT,
  avg_minutes DOUBLE,
  PRIMARY KEY (job_id, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  dt STRING
)
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day';

The partition column, dt, goes under PARTITIONED BY rather than among the other columns: that is how Hive splits a table into Partitions. LOCATION is the folder you wrote, as you wrote it. STORED AS ORC says how Hive keeps the table's files: ORC, a compact format Hive reads quickly. EXTERNAL and LOCATION say the files are yours, in the table's own folder.

At work, once run_execute(create_table(job_day), execute) has made the table, check_table_reference(job_day, query=query) compares it with your file, as above. And the Project knows the file:

project.check()  # -> a report, printed

Python shows
Every Table folder, Table reference and Building block is registered, and each one registered is there.

Before you go on, check that:

check_table_reference says the table matches its Table reference;
the Hive of create_table(job_day) holds dt under PARTITIONED BY and your LOCATION;
project.check() says every Table reference is registered.


Common mistakes

A partition column left out of columns

A partition column is a column of the table too, so it goes in columns= as well as in partitioned_by=. One that is only in partitioned_by= stops:

job_day_no_dt = Table(
    "mart.job_day",
    columns={"job_id": "bigint", "runs": "bigint"},
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  Table('mart.job_day'): partitioned_by names 'dt', which isn't one of its columns.
  Why it matters: Every name there must be a column in columns={...}.
  Usual fix:      Use one of: job_id, runs.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Add "dt": "string" to columns=.

A Key that names a column the table doesn't have

job_day_typo = Table(
    "mart.job_day",
    columns={"job_id": "bigint", "runs": "bigint", "dt": "string"},
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["job_id", "day"],
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  Table('mart.job_day'): key names 'day', which isn't one of its columns.
  Why it matters: Every name there must be a column in columns={...}.
  Usual fix:      Use one of: job_id, runs, dt.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Name in key=, partitioned_by= and does_not_add_up= only columns from columns=, spelled the same way.

Adding up a column that doesn't add up

week = statement(
    SELECT(job_day.job_id, AS(sum_of(job_day.avg_minutes), "avg_minutes")),
    FROM(job_day),
    WHERE(between(job_day.dt, "2026-09-21", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_day.job_id),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  sum_of(job_day.avg_minutes) adds up job_day.avg_minutes, which is listed in does_not_add_up in its Table reference.
  Why it matters: Averages, ratios and distinct counts don't add up: the average of daily averages is not the weekly average, and a user seen on two days would be counted twice.
  Usual fix:      It could be an average, a ratio or a distinct count, so work it out again from the table it was made from. For an average or a ratio, add up its two parts and divide after your own GROUP_BY. For a distinct count, count again with count_distinct(...).
  Opt-out:        sum_of(job_day.avg_minutes, adds_up=True)

The Toolbox stops it with a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result. Each day's average is right for its day, but a week's average isn't the sum of the days', nor their average when the days had different numbers of runs. The table keeps job_day.minutes and job_day.runs, which add up: total those over the week, and work the average out from the totals. Keep the Opt-out for the rare call where a total really is what you mean.

A day written the other way

The Toolbox doesn't know how a table writes its days: it compares the value as you wrote it. A day written like 2026-09-23, on a table that writes 20260923, matches no row, and nothing stops it:

wrong_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(equals(region_costs.dt, "2026-09-23")),
)
run_query(wrong_day, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows
No rows.

When a result comes back empty, look at how the table writes the column: first_look shows a few of its rows. Then write the day the table's way.

date_partition= from an older Toolbox

A Table reference written for Toolbox 6 said date_partition= and, for days written another way, date_format=. Both stop, saying what to write instead:

old_reference = Table(
    "ops.region_costs",
    columns={"job_id": "bigint", "cost_cents": "bigint", "region": "string",
             "dt": "string"},
    date_partition="dt",
)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  Table('ops.region_costs') was given date_partition=, which Toolbox 7.0 replaced.
  Why it matters: Since 7.0 a table is partitioned by any columns, or none, and no column is a day to the Toolbox: partitioned_by= lists them, and a date is compared as it is written. Table takes name, columns=, partitioned_by=, key=, does_not_add_up=, location=, purge=, properties=, stored_as=, compression=, bucketed_by=, buckets=, sorted_by= and descriptions=.
  Usual fix:      Write partitioned_by=["dt"] where it said date_partition="dt", leave it out where it said date_partition=None, and remove date_format=. CHANGES.md, under 7.0, says more.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write the Table reference as the Usual fix says.

Next

Create a Saved table from a Table reference and write rows into it, from its own Table notebook: Save a table.
Join your table to another without repeating rows, which is what key= is for: Count, join and stay safe.
Rewrite some of a partitioned table's Partitions, the days or the regions it holds: Rewrite some partitions.
The gallery's Worked examples of Table and create_table, and a Saved table built and written step by step: Worked example of a Saved table.

11. The latest row of each group, and the top N Intermediate

Goal

Keep one whole row of each group, such as each job's last run, or the top N rows of each group, such as each job's three longest runs. row_number numbers the rows of each group in the order you choose, inside a Derived table: a Statement given a name so another Statement can read from it, never stored. A Statement is one query, made of the Toolbox's Clause functions. The Statement that reads the Derived table keeps number 1, or numbers 1 to N.

When you'd use it

If you want the latest row of each group, such as each job's last run, each customer's last order or each product's newest price, keep number 1, as steps 5 to 7 do.
If you want the top N of each group, such as each job's three longest runs or each team's busiest days, keep numbers 1 to N, as steps 8 and 9 do.
If you are about to take max_of of several columns to get "the newest row", number the rows instead: each max_of picks its own row, so the columns can come from different rows. Step 4 shows it happen.
If you have not yet done the Getting started how-tos, do Build and run a first statement, Count, join and stay safe and Share Building blocks, which starts with Derived tables, first.


Steps

1. Copy the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout, and an Example project is one made for the Example database's tables, to read and copy from. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, and runs its steps there:

warehouse.py

Done when the folder holds the Intermediate Example project's files. Then step 2.

2. Import the Toolbox, the table and its Alias

Goal: the table and the Condition the steps read. Import the Toolbox, the Project's ops Database and your query. ops.job_events holds what each job's runs did: a "start" row when a run starts, then "retry", "finish" or "fail", with the minutes since the run started. Its Table reference, the file that describes the table, ends with an Alias, a name you give by hand to a condition on that table alone: ends_a_run, the events that end a run, imported beside the table it is about:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from references import ops
from references.ops__job_events import ends_a_run
from warehouse import query, execute
job_events = ops.job_events  # -> Table
ends_a_run  # -> Condition

Python shows
job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail')

Done when ends_a_run shows job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail'). Then step 3.

3. Keep the week's run-ending rows

Goal: the filter every Statement below reads. A run's minutes are on the row that ends it, and event ids grow as events come in, so the last run of each job is its run-ending row with the largest job_events.event_id. This how-to reads the last week of the Example database, 2026-09-18 to 2026-09-24:

week_ends = [between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24"), ends_a_run]

Done when week_ends holds the two Conditions. Then step 4.

4. See why max_of isn't the latest row

Goal: see the wrong answer before the right one. The question: how long did each job's last run take? The first thing most people write takes the largest of each column:

careless = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, AS(max_of(job_events.event_id), "event_id"),
           AS(max_of(job_events.minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id),
)
text = show_hive(careless)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  MAX(job_events.event_id) AS event_id,
  MAX(job_events.minutes) AS minutes
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
  AND job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail')
GROUP BY
  job_events.job_id;

run_query(careless, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idevent_idminutes

15614

25422

34633




It runs, and it is wrong. Job 1's last run, event 56, took 10 minutes, but max_of(job_events.minutes) takes the largest minutes from any of job 1's runs, 14. Each max_of picks its own row.

Done when you see job 1's minutes, 14, come from a run other than event 56. Then step 5.

5. Number each job's runs, last first

Goal: a number on each row, 1 for each job's last run. row_number numbers the rows of each group from 1:

PARTITION_BY= names the column whose values make the groups, here each job. The word is SQL's; it has nothing to do with a table's Partitions.
ORDER_BY= says the order to number them in. descending makes the largest come first, so each job's last run gets 1.


The two may also go inside a window, passed as over=, the way every other calculation over the rows around each row takes them, such as lag and rank: row_number(over=window( PARTITION_BY=..., ORDER_BY=...)) writes the same Hive.

The number is a calculation, so name it with AS, and put it in a Derived table, for the next Statement to read:

numbered = derived("numbered", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.event_id, job_events.minutes,
           AS(row_number(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id,
                         ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id)), "last_first")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends),
))

Done when numbered is made without an error. Then step 6.

6. Check the numbering alone

Goal: see each row's number before you keep any. Read every column of the Derived table with a Statement of its own:

numbering = statement(SELECT(all_columns(numbered)), FROM(numbered))  # -> Statement
text = show_hive(numbering)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.event_id,
    job_events.minutes,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC) AS last_first
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
    AND job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail')
)
SELECT
  numbered.job_id,
  numbered.event_id,
  numbered.minutes,
  numbered.last_first
FROM numbered;

run_query(numbering, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

Each job's runs are numbered 1, 2, 3, ... from its last. The table lists them by job and event id, as the Example database gives rows when nothing orders them, so each job's numbers count down.

Done when each job's largest event id has last_first 1. Then step 7.

7. Keep number 1

Goal: one whole row per job. The Statement that reads numbered keeps the rows numbered 1:

latest = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(numbered.job_id, numbered.event_id, numbered.minutes),
    FROM(numbered),
    WHERE(equals(numbered.last_first, 1)),
)
text = show_hive(latest)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.event_id,
    job_events.minutes,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC) AS last_first
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
    AND job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail')
)
SELECT
  numbered.job_id,
  numbered.event_id,
  numbered.minutes
FROM numbered
WHERE
  numbered.last_first = 1;

run_query(latest, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idevent_idminutes

15610

25418

34633




Job 1's last run took 10 minutes, as it should: the minutes come from the same row as the event.

Done when job 1's row is event 56, 10 minutes. Then step 8.

8. Number each job's runs, longest first

Goal: a number on each row, 1 for each job's longest run. Number the runs from the longest. Two of job 1's runs took 12 minutes, so ORDER_BY= takes a list, and a second column breaks the tie, the later of the two first. Give every numbering a column that breaks ties: without one, which of two tied rows gets the lower number can change from one run to the next.

ranked = derived("ranked", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.event_id, job_events.minutes,
           AS(row_number(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id,
                         ORDER_BY=[descending(job_events.minutes),
                                   descending(job_events.event_id)]), "longest_first")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends),
))

Done when ranked is made without an error. Then step 9.

9. Keep the top N of each group

Goal: each job's three longest runs. Keep numbers 1 to N, with at_most:

top_three = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(ranked.job_id, ranked.event_id, ranked.minutes),
    FROM(ranked),
    WHERE(at_most(ranked.longest_first, 3)),
)
text = show_hive(top_three)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH ranked AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.event_id,
    job_events.minutes,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
      PARTITION BY job_events.job_id
      ORDER BY job_events.minutes DESC, job_events.event_id DESC
    ) AS longest_first
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
    AND job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail')
)
SELECT
  ranked.job_id,
  ranked.event_id,
  ranked.minutes
FROM ranked
WHERE
  ranked.longest_first <= 3;

run_query(top_three, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idevent_idminutes

13214

14213

15212

23422

24421

25418

33612

34633




Three rows per job, or fewer for a job with fewer runs, as job 3 has two. Of job 1's two runs of 12 minutes, the later, event 52, is kept. ORDER_BY with LIMIT keeps the top N rows of the whole result; row_number keeps the top N of each group.

Done when jobs 1 and 2 have three rows each and job 3 two. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

Check that each job comes back once: one row per job, as many rows as jobs that ran.

last_runs = run_query(latest, query)  # -> DataFrame
last_runs["job_id"].is_unique

Python shows
True

And check each job's last run against the largest event id worked out another way, with max_of, which is right for one column on its own. Each side becomes a dict, from each job to its event, so the order the rows came back in doesn't matter:

largest_ids = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, AS(max_of(job_events.event_id), "event_id")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id),
)
largest = run_query(largest_ids, query)  # -> DataFrame
dict(zip(largest["job_id"], largest["event_id"])) == dict(
    zip(last_runs["job_id"], last_runs["event_id"]))

Python shows
True

Before you go on, check that:

last_runs["job_id"].is_unique shows True;
the comparison with max_of above shows True;
each row of latest takes its minutes from its own event, as job 1's 10 minutes do.


Common mistakes

Testing the row number in the same Statement

A row's number is given last, after the rows are picked, so Hive can't test it in the WHERE of the Statement that makes it:

in_one_go = statement(
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.event_id, job_events.minutes),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends,
          equals(row_number(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id,
                            ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id)), 1)),
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  WHERE has ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC) = 1, which numbers rows.
  Why it matters: Rows are numbered last, after the rows are picked and grouped, so Hive can't use the number in WHERE of the SELECT that makes it.
  Usual fix:      Number the rows in a derived(...) table, then use the number in the Statement that reads it; help(row_number) shows how.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Number the rows in a Derived table, then keep number 1 in the Statement that reads it, as the steps do.

Numbering from the wrong end

Without descending, ORDER_BY= numbers from the smallest, so number 1 is each job's first run of the week, not its last. Nothing stops it:

first_first = derived("first_first", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.event_id, job_events.minutes,
           AS(row_number(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id, ORDER_BY=job_events.event_id),
              "position")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends),
))
first_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(first_first.job_id, first_first.event_id, first_first.minutes),
    FROM(first_first),
    WHERE(equals(first_first.position, 1)),
)
run_query(first_runs, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idevent_idminutes

13214

23422

33612




Each job's first run of the week. Its Hive numbers with ORDER BY job_events.event_id ASC, where numbering last first says DESC. To number from the last, wrap the column in descending.

A row number without a name

unnamed = derived("unnamed", statement(
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.event_id,
           row_number(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id,
                      ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id))),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_ends),
))

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  SELECT has a calculation with no name: ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC).
  Why it matters: Without a name, the warehouse makes one up, such as _c0 or count(1), and that is the name pandas would show you.
  Usual fix:      Name it with AS, as in SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "runs")).
  Opt-out:        none - a calculation always needs a name.

The Toolbox stops it with a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result. Name it with AS, so the Statement that reads it can test it by that name.

Next

Keep the numbering step as a Building block every Statement can call: Share Building blocks.
Each job's row as it was on a given day, from a table that keeps a copy of itself every day: Look things up as of a day.
A rank, the row before, or a rolling sum, each over a Window: Look across rows.
The gallery's Worked examples of row_number and descending, and the careless and fixed Statements side by side: latest and top N.


Toolbox names used: all_columns, SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, statement, derived, equals, at_most, between, max_of, descending, row_number, show_hive, run_query




12. Generate Statements and Table references in a loop Intermediate

Goal

Build many Statements from one function and a loop, in place of copying the same lines and changing a word in each: one Statement per team, one per table of a Database, and one per entry of a dict of settings. A Statement is one query, made of the Toolbox's Clause functions. Then, for tables you only check, generate their Table references from settings too, run a Statement for each, and export one Lineage for the lot. A Table reference describes one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key; Lineage is the record of which table columns feed each column a Statement gives.

When you'd use it

If you would otherwise copy a Statement and change one value, such as a team, a status or a region, write the Statement once as a function of that value, as steps 4 and 5 do.
If you ask the same question of several tables, such as every table of ops, loop over the tables, as steps 8 to 11 do.
If you keep a set of checks, make each one a line of settings, so adding a check is one more line, not one more Statement to write, as steps 12 to 14 do.
If you check many tables but never read them in a Statement, make their Table references in the notebook, from settings, as steps 15 to 18 do.
If only a value changes and one result is enough, write one Statement with GROUP_BY: it answers for every value at once, as step 3 shows.
If you have not yet done the Getting started how-tos, do Add your tables' references, Build and run a first statement and Count, join and stay safe first.


A Statement is a Python value, built by Python functions, so a loop builds Statements as it builds anything else.

Steps

1. Copy the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout, and an Example project is one made for the Example database's tables, to read and copy from. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, and runs its steps there:

Done when the folder holds the Intermediate Example project's files. Then step 2.

2. Import the Toolbox, pandas and your query

Goal: every name the steps call. Import the Toolbox, pandas, the Project's ops Database and your query. A Database is a named group of tables, the first part of a table's name, ops in ops.jobs:

from sqlglot_composer import *
import pandas as pd
from references import ops
from warehouse import query, execute

Done when the four imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Check whether one Statement does it

Goal: write a loop only where you need one. ops.team_notes holds the notes each team wrote about its jobs. It isn't partitioned, so a Statement may read all of it. How many notes has each team written? One Statement, grouped by team, answers for every team at once, in one query:

team_notes = ops.team_notes  # -> Table
notes_per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(team_notes.team, AS(count_rows(), "notes")),
    FROM(team_notes),
    GROUP_BY(team_notes.team),
)
run_query(notes_per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamnotes

data2

finance2

web1




A loop is for when each Statement must be its own: its own result to send somewhere, its own table, or its own columns and conditions.

Done when you know each Statement must be its own: here, each team wants its own file. Then step 4.

4. Write the Statement once, as a function

Goal: one function that gives any team's Statement. Each team wants its own notes, in a file of its own. Write the Statement once, as a function of the team:

def notes_of(team: str) -> Statement:
    return statement(
        SELECT(team_notes.written_on, team_notes.note),
        FROM(team_notes),
        WHERE(equals(team_notes.team, team)),
    )

Done when notes_of is defined. Then step 5.

5. Build one Statement per team in a loop

Goal: each team's Statement, kept by team. Loop over the three teams, keeping each team's Statement in a dict, by team:

per_team = {}
for team in ["data", "finance", "web"]:
    per_team[team] = notes_of(team)
run_query(per_team["finance"], query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when finance's Statement gives its two notes. Then step 6.

6. Save each team's result in a file

Goal: one CSV file per team. Run each Statement, and save each team's result in its own CSV file:

for team in per_team:
    result = run_query(per_team[team], query)
    result.to_csv(f"notes_{team}.csv", index=False)

It writes notes_data.csv too.

It writes notes_web.csv too.

A team with no notes gets a file with only the column names, so every team gets a file.

Done when notes_data.csv, notes_finance.csv and notes_web.csv are in the folder. Then step 7.

8. List the tables of a Database

Goal: the names to loop over. How many rows does each table of ops hold? dir(ops) lists its tables, the list tab completion shows, and getattr(ops, name) gives the table named name:

dir(ops)  # -> list[str]

Python shows

Done when dir(ops) lists the five tables. Then step 9.

9. Say which rows to count in each table

Goal: a filter for each table. The tables are partitioned in different ways, and a read of a partitioned table must filter one of its partition columns. A Partition is a slice of a table: the rows that share one value of each column it is partitioned by. So say for each table which rows to count: a condition on one of its partition columns, or None for ops.team_notes, which has none:

reads = {
    "job_events": equals(ops.job_events.dt, "2026-09-24"),
    "job_owners": equals(ops.job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"),
    "region_budgets": equals(ops.region_budgets.region, "eu"),
    "region_costs": equals(ops.region_costs.region, "eu"),
    "team_notes": None,
}

Done when reads holds an entry for each name dir(ops) lists. Then step 10.

10. Write the counting function

Goal: one function that counts any table's rows. The function takes the table itself, a Table reference, as an argument, and adds a WHERE only where there is a condition:

def row_count(table: Table, where: Condition | None) -> Statement:
    clauses = [SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "row_count")), FROM(table)]
    if where is not None:
        clauses.append(WHERE(where))
    return statement(*clauses)

Done when row_count is defined. Then step 11.

11. Count each table's rows in a loop

Goal: one row per table. Loop over the tables, by name, and gather one row per table:

counted = []
for name in dir(ops):
    result = run_query(row_count(getattr(ops, name), reads[name]), query)
    counted.append({"table": name, "row_count": result["row_count"][0]})
pd.DataFrame(counted)  # -> DataFrame

12. Write each check as settings

Goal: a set of daily checks, one entry each. Each check counts, on one day, the rows of a table whose column holds a given value. Each check is a few settings, so each is an entry of a dict: its name, then its table, its column and its value.

CHECKS = {
    "failed_runs": {"table": ops.job_events, "column": "event_type", "value": "fail"},
    "retries": {"table": ops.job_events, "column": "event_type", "value": "retry"},
    "web_jobs": {"table": ops.job_owners, "column": "team", "value": "web"},
}

Done when CHECKS holds the three checks. Then step 13.

13. Write the check function

Goal: one function that builds any check's Statement. The function names the column as text, so it reads it from the Table reference with Python's getattr: getattr(ops.job_events, "event_type") is ops.job_events.event_type. Both tables are partitioned by their day, dt:

def matching_rows(table: Table, column: str, value: str, day: str) -> Statement:
    return statement(
        SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "matching")),
        FROM(table),
        WHERE(equals(table.dt, day), equals(getattr(table, column), value)),
    )

Done when matching_rows is defined. Then step 14.

14. Run each check in a loop

Goal: one row per check. The loop builds and runs each check, and gathers one row per check:

found = []
for name, setting in CHECKS.items():
    check = matching_rows(setting["table"], setting["column"], setting["value"],
                          "2026-09-18")
    result = run_query(check, query)
    found.append({"check": name, "matching": result["matching"][0]})
pd.DataFrame(found)  # -> DataFrame

15. Write a setting per table to check

Goal: what each generated Table reference needs, a line each. Your Statements read the tables of ops through their Table references in references/, which project.add_references wrote from each table's DESCRIBE. A weekly check over many tables you never read in a Statement needs less: a Table reference made in the notebook, from a line of settings each. Here, three tables of the Example database this Project doesn't read: the table's name, its partition columns, its Key, the columns whose values name one row, under "add_up" the columns a daily total adds up, and under "does_not_add_up" any that must never be added up, such as avg_retry_secs, an average per run:

settings = [
    {"table": "ops.job_runs", "partitioned_by": ["dt"], "key": ["run_id"],
     "add_up": ["duration_mins"], "does_not_add_up": ["avg_retry_secs"]},
    {"table": "ops.run_alerts", "partitioned_by": ["dt"], "key": ["alert_id"],
     "add_up": []},
    {"table": "ops.scheduler_runs", "partitioned_by": ["dt"], "key": ["run_id"],
     "add_up": []},
]

Done when settings holds a line for each of the three tables. Then step 16.

16. Read a table's DESCRIBE

Goal: see what your query gives for each column. A Table reference needs each column and its type. Rather than type them, ask the table: your query runs DESCRIBE, which lists the columns, one per row, each with its name in "col_name" and its type in "data_type". After them it lists the partition columns again, under a heading row starting with #, often after an empty row, so stop at the first such row:

query("DESCRIBE ops.scheduler_runs")  # -> DataFrame

Done when you see where the columns end: the first empty row or row starting with #. Then step 17.

17. Build a Table reference from each setting

Goal: a Table reference per table, in the notebook. table_reference builds a Table reference from a setting and what DESCRIBE gives:

from collections.abc import Callable
def table_reference(setting: dict[str, str | list[str]],
                    query: Callable[[str], pd.DataFrame]) -> Table:
    described = query("DESCRIBE " + setting["table"])
    columns = {}
    for name, data_type in zip(described["col_name"], described["data_type"]):
        if not name or name.startswith("#"):
            break
        columns[name] = data_type
    return Table(setting["table"], columns=columns,
                 partitioned_by=setting["partitioned_by"], key=setting["key"],
                 does_not_add_up=setting.get("does_not_add_up", []))
tables = {setting["table"]: table_reference(setting, query)  # -> dict[str, Table]
          for setting in settings}
tables["ops.job_runs"]

Python shows
Table('ops.job_runs', columns: run_id, job_id, status, duration_mins, avg_retry_secs, dt)

Done when tables["ops.job_runs"] shows its six columns. Then step 18.

for t in tables.values():

A Table reference generated like this lasts as long as the notebook. For a table you build on, keep its Table reference in references/, with project.add_references, as Add your tables' references shows, so a change to the table shows up as a difference when you check it, rather than slipping into your Statements.

Done when each table matches its Table reference and each Key holds. Then step 19.

19. Generate a Statement for each table

Goal: each table's daily totals, from one function. daily_totals writes one Statement for a table and its setting: per day, the number of rows and the total of each column it adds up. getattr(t, "duration_mins") is t.duration_mins, for a column whose name you hold as text, so a column the table doesn't have stops here, naming the real ones:

def daily_totals(t: Table, setting: dict[str, str | list[str]], first_day: str,
                 last_day: str) -> Statement:
    totals = [AS(sum_of(getattr(t, name)), "total_" + name)
              for name in setting["add_up"]]
    return statement(
        SELECT(t.dt, AS(count_rows(), "row_count"), totals),
        FROM(t),
        WHERE(between(t.dt, first_day, last_day)),
        GROUP_BY(t.dt),
    )
daily = {setting["table"]: daily_totals(  # -> dict[str, Statement]
             tables[setting["table"]], setting, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")
         for setting in settings}
text = show_hive(*daily.values())  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
-- 1 of 3
SELECT
  job_runs.dt,
  COUNT(*) AS row_count,
  SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS total_duration_mins
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_runs.dt;

-- 2 of 3
SELECT
  run_alerts.dt,
  COUNT(*) AS row_count
FROM ops.run_alerts AS run_alerts
WHERE
  run_alerts.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  run_alerts.dt;

-- 3 of 3
SELECT
  scheduler_runs.dt,
  COUNT(*) AS row_count
FROM ops.scheduler_runs AS scheduler_runs
WHERE
  scheduler_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  scheduler_runs.dt;

21. Export one Lineage for them all

Goal: one page that traces every Statement. Pass every Statement to export_lineage at once, and it writes one HTML page and one Markdown file for them all, laid out as in Follow lineage. to= names the HTML file, and the Markdown file goes beside it.

export_lineage names each Statement after the variable that holds it. These sit in a dict, in no variable of their own, so it numbers them in the order you passed them: in the file below, statement_1 is ops.job_runs's, statement_2 ops.run_alerts's and statement_3 ops.scheduler_runs's, and each one's Hive, at the end of its part, names its table.

html_file, markdown_file = export_lineage(*daily.values(),
                                          to="daily_totals.html")  # -> tuple[Path, Path]

## statement_1

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `row_count`

Calculated in **statement_1** as `count_rows()`, which is `COUNT(*)`.

```text
statement_1.row_count = count_rows()
└─ (no columns: it counts rows)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in statement_1: `between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.dt`.

#### `total_duration_mins`

Calculated in **statement_1** as `sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins)`, which is `SUM(job_runs.duration_mins)`.

```text
statement_1.total_duration_mins = sum_of(job_runs.duration_mins)
└─ ops.job_runs.duration_mins  (int)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in statement_1: `between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_runs.dt)

One value for each different `job_runs.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `dt` | ops.job_runs.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- statement_1: Key (dt), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each dt.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
SELECT
  job_runs.dt,
  COUNT(*) AS row_count,
  SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS total_duration_mins
FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
WHERE
  job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_runs.dt
```

And the loops made one Statement per team, per table, per check and per setting:

len(per_team), len(counted), len(found), len(daily)

Python shows

markdown_file.name

Before you go on, check that:

the per-team Statements find 5 notes in all, as the grouped Statement counts;
the lengths show (3, 5, 3, 3): one Statement per team, per table, per check and per setting;
markdown_file.name shows 'daily_totals.md'.


Common mistakes

A setting that names a column the table doesn't have

A typo in a setting is caught when the Statement is built, with the column's likely name and the table's columns:

matching_rows(ops.job_events, "event_typ", "fail", "2026-09-18")

Python stops with this message
AttributeError:
job_events has no column 'event_typ'. Did you mean 'event_type'? Its columns are: event_id, job_id, event_type, minutes, dt.

Write the column's name as the message lists it.

A partitioned table with no condition in the loop

Give a partitioned table None in reads, and its Statement reads every Partition it has. The Load limit, the check that refuses a Statement that would read too much, stops it, and with it the loop, naming the partition columns to filter:

row_count(ops.region_costs, None)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  FROM(region_costs) reads ops.region_costs, but filters none of its partition columns: region, dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would read every Partition the table holds, which can stall the cluster for everyone.
  Usual fix:      Filter one of them in WHERE, as in WHERE(equals(region_costs.region, ...)), or with is_in, between or last_n_days.
  Opt-out:        FROM(region_costs, reads_all_partitions=True)

Give each partitioned table a condition on one of its partition columns, as reads does.

A setting that looks for a missing value

A check for jobs with no owner can't use equals with None: in SQL nothing equals a missing value, so it would always count 0. A Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result, stops it, and with it the loop:

matching_rows(ops.job_owners, "owner", None, "2026-09-24")

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  equals(job_owners.owner, None) compares with None.
  Why it matters: In SQL nothing is equal to NULL, not even NULL, so this would match no rows and quietly give an empty or wrong result.
  Usual fix:      Use is_null(column) or is_not_null(column) to find missing values.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Use is_null for such a check: give it a function of its own, or a setting that says which condition to build.

Adding up an average

A setting can list under "add_up" a column that must not be added up, such as "avg_retry_secs", an average per run. Because the Table reference was generated with "does_not_add_up" from the setting, the Toolbox refuses it:

averages = {"table": "ops.job_runs", "key": ["run_id"], "add_up": ["avg_retry_secs"]}
daily_totals(tables["ops.job_runs"], averages, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  sum_of(job_runs.avg_retry_secs) adds up job_runs.avg_retry_secs, which is listed in does_not_add_up in its Table reference.
  Why it matters: Averages, ratios and distinct counts don't add up: the average of daily averages is not the weekly average, and a user seen on two days would be counted twice.
  Usual fix:      It could be an average, a ratio or a distinct count, so work it out again from the table it was made from. For an average or a ratio, add up its two parts and divide after your own GROUP_BY. For a distinct count, count again with count_distinct(...).
  Opt-out:        sum_of(job_runs.avg_retry_secs, adds_up=True)

Fill in "does_not_add_up" for every table: left out, nothing would refuse it, and the total would come out, and be wrong.

Next

Checks of your data, such as no repeated Keys or no missing values, as Statements you could generate per table the same way: Check data quality.
Write a range of a Saved table's Partitions, in one write or in a loop of writes: Rewrite some partitions.
Compare a Lineage before and after you change the function that writes your Statements: Review and test a change.
The gallery's all_columns entry, which gives a Table reference's columns as a list to loop over, and Table, which says what each part of a Table reference does.


Toolbox names used: Table, check_key, check_table_reference, SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, statement, Statement, equals, between, Condition, count_rows, sum_of, show_hive, run_query, export_lineage




13. Rewrite some partitions Intermediate

Goal

Rewrite some of a Saved table's Partitions, and leave the rest as they are. A Saved table is a real table in the warehouse that your own Statements write; a Partition is a slice of a table, the rows that share one value of each column it is partitioned by. You write a range of days of a table partitioned by its day, in one write that fills each Partition from its rows, or in a loop of writes that each name their Partition with partition={...}; the last few days again on every run, for rows that come in late; a table partitioned by region, written a region at a time; and a table whose source writes its days another way. Then you write several Saved tables in order with project.run_tables.

When you'd use it

If a Saved table is new, and the Partitions it should already hold are empty, write the range in one backfill, as steps 4 to 7 do.
If you fixed a mistake in a write, rewrite every Partition it wrote, since each holds the mistake.
If rows can come in late, such as a bill a day or two after its day, rewrite the last few days of the table that counts them on each run, as step 8 shows.
If a backfill covers more days than one Statement should read at once, write it a Partition at a time, in a loop, as steps 9 to 12 do.
If one region's numbers changed, rewrite that region's Partition alone, and the other regions' Partitions stay as they are, as steps 13 and 14 do.
If several Saved tables read one another, write them in order with project.run_tables, as step 16 does.
If you have not yet done the Getting started how-tos, do Share Building blocks and Save a table first.


The Example database can't be written to, so on this page each write shows its Hive, and is sent through the Project's execute, which here prints the first line of what it would send and changes nothing. The rows a write would save are checked with a plain SELECT first, which the Example database does run.

Steps

1. Copy the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout, and an Example project is one made for the Example database's tables, to read and copy from. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. 2. Import the Toolbox, project, query and execute

Goal: every name the steps call. Import the Toolbox, the project module, and your query and execute:

from sqlglot_composer import *

Done when the three imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Load a Table notebook's logic

Goal: the table's functions, in your notebook. mart.job_day_facts holds each job's events, runs and minutes per day, beside the team that owned the job that day. It is partitioned by its day, dt. Its Table notebook, the notebook whose cells create and write the table, tables/mart__job_day_facts/mart__job_day_facts.ipynb, defines its steps as functions in its logic cell, and project.load gives them, from any notebook of the Project:

facts = project.load("mart.job_day_facts")  # -> the notebook's functions
facts

Python shows
The logic of mart.job_day_facts: create, rows, preview, write_days, write.

These are the functions of its logic cell this how-to uses, as the notebook has them:

from sqlglot_composer import FROM, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, Clause, Statement, statement
from building_blocks.days_rewritten import first_day_written
from building_blocks.events_per_job import events_per_job
from building_blocks.owner_on_day import owner_on_day
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners

DAY = "2026-09-24"  # the last day write() writes, the Example database's last day

def rows(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> list[Clause]:
    events = events_per_job(first_day, last_day)
    return [
        SELECT(events.job_id, job_owners.team, events.events, events.runs, events.minutes,
               events.dt),
        FROM(events),
        owner_on_day(events.job_id, events.dt, first_day=first_day, last_day=last_day),
    ]

def write_days(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Statement:
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts), *rows(first_day, last_day))

def write() -> Statement:
    return write_days(first_day_written(DAY), DAY)

events_per_job, a Building block, a piece of your own code reused across notebooks, counts each job's day of ops.job_events, and keeps the day, dt, in its GROUP_BY and its SELECT, so each day's numbers stay apart. owner_on_day joins each row to the team that owned the job that day.

Done when facts lists create, rows, preview, write_days and write. Then step 4.

4. Build a write over a range of days

Goal: one write that fills each day's Partition from its rows. write_days builds one write over a whole range of days. INSERT_OVERWRITE with no partition= fills the table's partition column, dt, from each row's own day, and replaces only the days the rows hold: any other day of the table keeps what it held. The partition column may go anywhere in SELECT: the Toolbox puts it last, where Hive takes it from. Here, the 14 days the Example database holds:

backfill = facts.write_days("2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")  # -> Statement
text = show_hive(backfill)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(
      SUM(
        CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END
      ),
      0
    ) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  events_per_job.job_id,
  job_owners.team,
  events_per_job.events,
  events_per_job.runs,
  events_per_job.minutes,
  events_per_job.dt
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24';

PARTITION(dt) names no day: Hive takes each row's day from its last column. Filling Partitions from the rows needs Hive's dynamic partitioning switched on, the two SET lines above the write: the Session setting "dynamic_partitions". A Session setting is a Hive SET sent before a Statement, which holds for the rest of the session.

Done when the Hive reads INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt). Then step 5.

Goal: see the rows a write would save, before it saves them. facts.preview() is the same rows as a plain SELECT, for the days write() writes, which the Example database runs:

run_query(facts.preview(), query)  # -> DataFrame

6. Send the Session setting

Goal: dynamic partitioning switched on in your session. run_execute sends a write through your execute, without its SET lines. So send the Session setting first, hive_settings("dynamic_partitions"), which holds for as long as your execute keeps one session:

run_execute(hive_settings("dynamic_partitions"), execute)

Python prints

Done when execute has been given both SET lines. Then step 7.

7. Send the write

Goal: the days written. Send the write through your execute:

run_execute(backfill, execute)

Python prints
execute: WITH events_per_job AS (

It writes admin/sent.csv too.

The write is an INSERT OVERWRITE, which replaces the days it writes. So if it fails, say on a network error, send it again: a day written twice holds the same rows, not twice as many.

Done when execute has been given the write. Then step 8.

8. Rewrite the last few days on every run, for rows that come in late

Goal: a write that picks up late rows. An event can reach the warehouse a day or two after its day: say a run finished at 23:50 on 2026-09-24, and its "finish" row arrives at 00:30 the next morning, after the run that wrote 2026-09-24. The row lands in 2026-09-24's Partition of ops.job_events, a day a write of only the newest day would never look at again.

So write() writes the last 3 days, ending on DAY, on every run. The 3 is written once, in the Building block building_blocks/days_rewritten.py, which every table of the Project that counts or adds up rows per day reads:

from building_blocks.days_rewritten import DAYS_REWRITTEN, first_day_written
DAYS_REWRITTEN  # -> int

Python shows
3

first_day_written("2026-09-24")  # -> str

Python shows
'2026-09-22'

print(to_hive(facts.write()).splitlines()[-1])

Hive
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'

Tomorrow's run, with DAY one day on, writes 2026-09-24 again, now with any event of that day that came in overnight, writes 2026-09-23 again with the same rows it already held, and writes 2026-09-25 for the first time. 2026-09-22, outside the window, keeps what it held. A day already right comes out the same, and a day that changed comes out new, so the same write can go out any number of times. A day whose rows all went away is the one exception: the write has no row for it, so it fills nothing there, and the day keeps its old rows.

Rewrite as many days as late rows can take to arrive, plus one: rows up to two days late need the last three. A row later than that needs a one-off backfill of its day, with write_days.

Done when write()'s Hive reads the days 2026-09-22 to 2026-09-24. Then step 9.

9. Build one write per day

Goal: a function that writes one named Partition. At work a backfill can cover months, more than one Statement should read at once. Then write it a Partition at a time: one_day below builds one write, which names its Partition with partition={"dt": day} and leaves dt out of SELECT. The Building blocks make the rows, as in the notebook, and WHERE keeps only the day the write names, so the Toolbox can see each row's day is that one, and each job has one row in it, as the table's Key, job_id and dt, needs. A Key is the columns whose values name exactly one row:

from building_blocks.events_per_job import events_per_job
from building_blocks.owner_on_day import owner_on_day
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners
def one_day(day: str) -> Statement:
    events = events_per_job(day, day)
    return statement(
        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts, partition={"dt": day}),
        SELECT(events.job_id, job_owners.team, events.events, events.runs,
               events.minutes),
        FROM(events),
        owner_on_day(events.job_id, events.dt, first_day=day, last_day=day),
        WHERE(equals(events.dt, day)),
    )

Done when one_day is defined. Then step 10.

import datetime
first = datetime.date(2026, 9, 1)
september = [str(first + datetime.timedelta(days=n)) for n in range(30)]  # -> list[str]
september[:3]

11. Read where each write writes

def insert_line(write: Statement) -> str:
    return next(line for line in to_hive(write).splitlines() if line.startswith("INSERT"))
for write in writes[:3]:
    print(insert_line(write))

12. Send the writes in a loop

Goal: each day written, oldest first. Each write names its Partition, so it needs no Session setting. show_hive(writes) prints them all, each headed by its place in the list; a loop sends them, oldest first:

from collections.abc import Callable
def send_each(writes: list[Statement], execute: Callable[[str], None]) -> None:
    for write in writes:
        run_execute(write, execute)
send_each(writes[:2], execute=execute)

It writes admin/sent.csv too.

A day the tables it reads hold no rows of is written with no rows. If the loop stops halfway, send it again from the day that failed, or from the start.

overruns = project.load("mart.budget_overruns")  # -> the notebook's functions

for write in by_region:
    print(to_hive(write).splitlines()[0])

15. Read a source that writes its days another way

Goal: a write whose source writes days like 20260924. mart.job_day_costs holds each job's cost per day, all regions added up, from ops.region_costs, which writes its days like 20260924, where the Project's other tables write 2026-09-24. The Toolbox compares a day exactly as it is written, so the Building block building_blocks/costs_per_job.py writes the days it is asked for the table's way, and the day each row holds back the Project's way, so mart.job_day_costs saves its days like 2026-09-24:

from building_blocks.costs_per_job import as_written_here
as_written_here("2026-09-24")  # -> str

[line for line in hive.splitlines() if "BETWEEN" in line]

16. Write several Saved tables in order

Goal: every table written after the tables it reads. mart.team_days reads both mart.job_day_facts and mart.job_day_costs, so it must be written after them, the same days. project.run_tables loads each table's write() and sends them in the order Lineage draws them, every table before the tables that read it, with the Session setting "dynamic_partitions" first, since some fill their Partitions from their rows. Lineage is the record of which table columns feed each column a Statement gives:

project.run_tables(execute=execute,  # -> a report, printed
                   tables=["mart.team_days", "mart.job_day_costs", "mart.job_day_facts"])

Python prints

execute: SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict
execute: WITH costs_per_job AS (
1 of 3: mart.job_day_costs  worked
execute: WITH events_per_job AS (
2 of 3: mart.job_day_facts  worked
execute: INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.team_days PARTITION(dt)
3 of 3: mart.team_days      worked

Python shows
Wrote 3 tables, in order.

It writes admin/sent.csv too.

Left out, tables= is every Table folder of the Project, and with no execute, project.run_tables() prints each write's Hive in that order and sends nothing. If a write fails, it stops there, and the tables after it aren't written, so a team's day is never written from facts that weren't.

Done when it prints Wrote 3 tables, in order. with mart.team_days last. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

A write that fills Partitions from its rows replaces the Partitions its rows fill, and no other, so the days write() rewrites are the days facts.preview() gives, the last three:

sorted(set(run_query(facts.preview(), query)["dt"]))  # -> list[str]

Python shows
['2026-09-22', '2026-09-23', '2026-09-24']

The day-at-a-time writes come oldest first, with none missing:

for write in writes[-2:]:
    print(insert_line(write))

Python prints
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-29')
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-30')

At work, after a rewrite, read the Saved table's days back with run_query, and compare them with facts.preview()'s rows.

Before you go on, check that:

facts.preview() gives the days 2026-09-22, 2026-09-23 and 2026-09-24;
the last two day-at-a-time writes name 2026-09-29 and 2026-09-30;
project.run_tables printed Wrote 3 tables, in order.


Common mistakes

Leaving the partition column out of a write that fills it from its rows

A write with no partition= takes each row's Partition from its own partition column, so its SELECT must give it. Leave it out, and the write is refused as you build it:

events = events_per_job("2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")  # -> Table
statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts),
    SELECT(events.job_id, job_owners.team, events.events, events.runs, events.minutes),
    FROM(events),
    owner_on_day(events.job_id, events.dt, first_day="2026-09-22",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts): it leaves out dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse fills a table's columns by position, not by name, so a missing or extra column would put values in the wrong columns.
  Usual fix:      SELECT every column of job_day_facts's Table reference, each under its own name, except a partition column the write names in partition={...}. The Toolbox puts them in the right order, its partition columns last.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Add events.dt to SELECT, as the notebook's rows does, or name the Partition with partition={"dt": ...}, as one_day does.

Naming one Partition in partition= while reading three days

partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"} names the Partition every row goes to, whatever day it was read from. With the Building block reading three days, each job would have three rows in 2026-09-24, one from each day read, so the table's Key, job_id and dt, would repeat. The Toolbox refuses it as you build it, with a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts, partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"}),
    SELECT(events.job_id, job_owners.team, events.events, events.runs, events.minutes),
    FROM(events),
    owner_on_day(events.job_id, events.dt, first_day="2026-09-22",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts) can't show that each row it writes has a Key (job_id, dt) of its own: events_per_job's rows are told apart by job_id, dt, and SELECT leaves out events_per_job.dt, so nothing makes its rows one for each job_id.
  Why it matters: Hive never checks a Key, so rows that share one would be saved without a word, and every join and count that trusts the Key would repeat them.
  Usual fix:      Write one row for each Key: GROUP_BY the Key's columns, use SELECT_DISTINCT, or keep row_number(...) at 1 for each Key in a Derived table, and join only on each joined table's whole Key. When a table it reads declares no Key, though a column or a few tell its rows apart, add one to its Table reference by hand, such as key=["job_id"], once check_key(...) confirms it. The how-to on Keys shows each.
  Opt-out:        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_facts, unproven_key=True), if each row has a Key of its own for a reason the Toolbox can't see.

partition= says where the rows go, and the reads which rows: keep them the same, with WHERE(equals(events.dt, day)) as one_day does, or leave partition= out and let each row's own day say, as write_days does. A table with no Key isn't checked, so there nothing would stop it: each day's Partition would hold the counts of three days.

Using INSERT_INTO for a window of days

No message stops you here. INSERT_INTO adds rows to a Partition and keeps what it held. Its Hive starts INSERT INTO where the steps' starts INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE:

added = statement(INSERT_INTO(job_day_facts), *facts.rows("2026-09-22", "2026-09-24"))
insert_line(added)  # -> str

Python shows
'INSERT INTO mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt)'

Nothing refuses it, since adding rows is sometimes what you want. But in a window of three days each day is written on three runs in a row, so each would hold its rows three times over, and every count read from it would come out three times too big. Use INSERT_OVERWRITE for any Partition you may write again.

A range written back to front

between takes the earlier day first. With the days swapped, no day is inside the range, and the Toolbox stops at once rather than write nothing:

facts.write_days("2026-09-24", "2026-09-11")

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  between(job_events.dt, '2026-09-24', '2026-09-11') starts after it ends.
  Why it matters: No value is both at least the first and at most the second, so this would match nothing.
  Usual fix:      Put the lower value first.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Give the earlier day first: facts.write_days("2026-09-11", "2026-09-24").

Writing a day the usual way, on a table that writes it another way

The Toolbox compares a day exactly as you write it, so a day written with dashes matches no row of ops.region_costs, and a Statement that reads it gives nothing:

from references import ops
with_dashes = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(ops.region_costs.job_id, ops.region_costs.cost_cents),
    FROM(ops.region_costs),
    WHERE(between(ops.region_costs.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")),
)
run_query(with_dashes, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows
No rows.

A write from it would write no rows, and nothing would say so. Write each day the table's way, as as_written_here does.

Giving run_execute a whole list

run_execute sends one Statement, and refuses a list, such as a write() that gives a write per region:

run_execute(by_region, execute)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  run_execute was given [<Statement reading ops.region_costs, ops.region_budgets - print(to_hive(s)) shows its Hive>, <Statement reading ops.region_costs, ops.region_budgets - print(to_hive(s)) shows its Hive>], which isn't a Statement.
  Why it matters: run_execute writes the Hive of one Statement made by statement(...) and sends it.
  Usual fix:      Pass its Statements one at a time, with a loop: for step in by_region: run_execute(step, execute).
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Send each in turn, with a loop, as send_each does, or let project.run_tables send them.

Next

Look things up as of each day in a table that keeps a copy of itself every day, as owner_on_day does: Look things up as of a day.
See where each column of mart.team_days comes from, through the tables it reads: Follow lineage.
Choose how a Saved table is stored, its Buckets and its compression: Choose how a Saved table is stored.
The gallery's Saved table Worked example, and its Worked examples of INSERT_OVERWRITE and hive_settings.

14. Look things up as of a day Intermediate

Goal

Find what was true on each day from a daily snapshot, a table that keeps a full copy of itself every day: here, the team each job belonged to on the day it ran. You join on the day as well as the job, count each team's runs as they were owned at the time, keep that join in a Building block, a piece of your own code reused across notebooks, and pick each job's newest row with row_number.

When you'd use it

If a table holds one row per thing per day, such as each job's owner, each product's price or each customer's plan, and another table's rows need the value from their own day, join on the day as the steps below do.
If a job changed teams last week, count it for its old team before the change and its new team after it, with the same join.
If you want each thing's value as it is now, pick its newest row (step 7).
If you haven't done the Getting started how-tos Count, join and stay safe and Share Building blocks, do them first.


Steps

1. Copy the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in, with the snapshot table's Table reference, the file that describes the table, already written. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout.

Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, and runs its steps there:

import shutil

2. Import the Toolbox and the tables

Goal: the two tables the steps read. Import the Toolbox, the Project's ops Database, the named group of tables whose names start ops., and your query. ops.job_events holds what each job's runs did over 14 days, 2026-09-11 to 2026-09-24: a "start" row when a run starts, then "finish", "retry" or "fail". ops.job_owners is the snapshot: every job's team and owner, once per day.

from sqlglot_composer import *
from references import ops
from warehouse import query, execute
job_events = ops.job_events  # -> Table
job_owners = ops.job_owners  # -> Table

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Look at the snapshot

Goal: see how the snapshot's rows are told apart. Each day holds one row per job, so its Key, the columns whose values name exactly one row, is the job and the day together: job_id and dt, which its Table reference, references/ops__job_owners.py, says. Job 3, report_build, moved from the data team to finance on 2026-09-18:

report_build_owners = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.dt, job_owners.team, job_owners.owner),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.job_id, 3),
          between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-16", "2026-09-19")),
)
run_query(report_build_owners, query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when the result shows data on 2026-09-17 and finance on 2026-09-18. Then step 4.

4. Join each run to its own day's row

Goal: each run beside its team on the day it ran. A run is a "start" row of job_events. Join the snapshot on both parts of its Key: the same job, and the same day. Each run then meets exactly one row, its job's row on the day it ran.

The snapshot is partitioned by dt too, so give it its own filter on dt, in WHERE or in ON=: the Toolbox checks each table's partition columns separately, and equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt) in ON= matches the two tables' days without saying which days to read.

report_build_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.dt, job_owners.team),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt))),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.job_id, 3),
          equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"),
          between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(report_build_runs)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.dt,
  job_owners.team
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = job_events.job_id AND job_owners.dt = job_events.dt
WHERE
  job_events.job_id = 3
  AND job_events.event_type = 'start'
  AND job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24';

run_query(report_build_runs, query)  # -> DataFrame

5. Count each team's runs as of their day

Goal: each team's runs, counted for the team that owned the job that day. The same join, for every job, grouped by team:

runs_per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt))),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"),
          between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)
run_query(runs_per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame

6. Keep the lookup in a Building block

Goal: the join written once, for every Statement that needs a job's team on a day. The Project keeps it in the Building block building_blocks/owner_on_day.py:

from sqlglot_composer import LEFT_JOIN, Clause, Column, all_of, between, equals
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners

def owner_on_day(job_id_column: Column, day_column: Column, first_day: str,
                 last_day: str) -> Clause:
    return LEFT_JOIN(
        job_owners,
        ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_id_column),
                  equals(job_owners.dt, day_column),
                  between(job_owners.dt, first_day, last_day)),
    )

It filters the snapshot's days in ON=, and it is a LEFT_JOIN, so a run whose day has no snapshot yet keeps its row, with a NULL team. A Statement calls it where a JOIN goes:

from building_blocks.owner_on_day import owner_on_day
runs_per_team_then = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    owner_on_day(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, first_day="2026-09-11",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)
run_query(runs_per_team_then, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamruns

data16

finance12




The same counts: every run of the 14 days has a snapshot. mart.job_day_facts, a Saved table of the Project, a real table your own Statements write, saves each job's day beside its team through this Building block, as Rewrite some partitions shows.

Done when the counts match step 5's: data 16, finance 12. Then step 7.

7. Pick each job's newest row

Goal: who owns each job now. Take each job's newest row of the snapshot. row_number numbers each job's rows from the newest day down; the Statement that reads them keeps number 1. Its PARTITION_BY= is SQL's word for the groups to number within, here each job, not the table's Partitions. Hive can't keep a row by its number in the same SELECT that numbers it, so the numbering goes in a Derived table, a Statement given a name so another can read from it, which the Hive writes at its top as WITH numbered AS (...). The latest row of each group starts with it:

numbered = derived("numbered", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.team, job_owners.owner, job_owners.dt,
           AS(row_number(PARTITION_BY=job_owners.job_id,
                         ORDER_BY=descending(job_owners.dt)), "newest_first")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
))
newest_owner = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(numbered.job_id, numbered.team, numbered.owner, numbered.dt),
    FROM(numbered),
    WHERE(equals(numbered.newest_first, 1)),
)
text = show_hive(newest_owner)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT
    job_owners.job_id,
    job_owners.team,
    job_owners.owner,
    job_owners.dt,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_owners.job_id ORDER BY job_owners.dt DESC) AS newest_first
  FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  WHERE
    job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'
)
SELECT
  numbered.job_id,
  numbered.team,
  numbered.owner,
  numbered.dt
FROM numbered
WHERE
  numbered.newest_first = 1;

run_query(newest_owner, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idteamownerdt

1dataana2026-09-24

2financeben2026-09-24

3financechloe2026-09-24

4webNULL2026-09-24




Here every job is in every day's copy, so each newest row is from 2026-09-24, and reading that one day would give the same. At work a row can drop out of a snapshot, such as a job that was deleted, and then row_number still finds its last row, from whichever day that was. Nobody owns cache_warm (job 4), so its owner is NULL (pandas shows it as NaN).

Done when the result holds one row per job, report_build's team finance. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

Each run should meet exactly one row of the snapshot, so the teams' runs add up to every run over the 14 days:

every_run = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
)
run_query(every_run, query)  # -> DataFrame
