report_build_runs gives report_build's runs before 2026-09-18 to data and after it to finance;
newest_owner gives one row per job.


Common mistakes

Joining the snapshot on the job alone

Without the day in ON=, each run meets its job's row on every day of the snapshot. The Toolbox warns at the JOIN, since a job_owners row is told apart only by the job and the day:

runs_per_team_repeated = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id)),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"),
          between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)

It builds, with a Warning
RepeatedRowsWarning:
  What happened:  JOIN(job_owners) matches on job_id, but a job_owners row is told apart only by its Key (job_id, dt).
  Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching job_owners row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
  Usual fix:      Group job_owners first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(job_owners, ON=..., many_matches=True)

run_query(runs_per_team_repeated, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamruns

data224

finance168




Each run counted 14 times, once per day of the snapshot. The Warning's fix names the part of the Key left out, the day: add equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt) to ON=, as report_build_runs does.

Taking every run's team from the newest day

No message stops you here. Joining every run to the snapshot of one day, the newest, gives every run the team its job has now:

runs_per_team_now = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"))),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)
run_query(runs_per_team_now, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamruns

data14

finance14




The total is still 28, but report_build's two runs before 2026-09-18 count for finance, a team that didn't own it then. Join on each row's own day, as runs_per_team does, and keep one day's snapshot for what you want as it is on that day.

Leaving the snapshot's days unfiltered

The day in ON= matches the two tables' days, but it doesn't say which of job_owners' days to read, so the Toolbox refuses as you build the Statement:

statement(
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt))),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  JOIN(job_owners, ON=...) reads ops.job_owners, but filters none of its partition columns: dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would read every Partition the table holds, which can stall the cluster for everyone.
  Usual fix:      Filter one of them in WHERE, as in WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, ...)), or with is_in, between or last_n_days.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(job_owners, ON=..., reads_all_partitions=True)

This is a Load limit, a check that protects the cluster. Filter the snapshot with the same days as the table it is joined to, in WHERE as the steps do, or in ON=, as owner_on_day does.

Next

Roll the days up into weeks and months: Rollups and subtotals.
Save each job's day beside its team then, and rewrite the last few days as rows come in late: Rewrite some partitions.
Each job's last row of a table, and the top N: The latest row of each group.
The gallery's latest run per job, which shows why max_of on each column can mix values from different rows, and its Worked example of row_number.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, JOIN, WHERE, GROUP_BY, statement, derived, equals, between, all_of, count_rows, descending, row_number, show_hive, run_query




15. Rollups and subtotals Intermediate

Goal

Turn days of rows into one row per week, or per month, with week_start and month_start; count the different jobs that ran each week without adding up each day's count; and count runs for each team and job with, in the same result, each team's subtotal and the grand total, with GROUP_BY(rollup(...)), labelling the subtotal rows with is_total. A Subtotal row is a row the grouping adds, totalling the rows of several groups, such as one per team over all its jobs, or the grand total, one over every row.

When you'd use it

If a daily table is too fine to read and the question is about weeks or months, such as runs per week, the different jobs seen each month, or a week's total against the week before, group by week_start or month_start (steps 3 to 6).
If a report shows the parts and their totals together, such as runs per job with each team's total, or any table a spreadsheet would end with a "Total" row, group with rollup (steps 7 to 11).
If you haven't done the Getting started how-tos Count, join and stay safe and Share Building blocks, do them first.


Steps

1. Copy the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in, its Table references and Building blocks written. A Project is your folder of scripts at work, in the Toolbox's fixed layout.

Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, and runs its steps there:

2. Import the Toolbox and the table

Goal: the table the steps count. Import the Toolbox, the project module, the Project's ops Database, the named group of tables whose names start ops., its Alias ends_a_run, a condition its Table reference names, and your query. ops.job_events holds what each job's runs did over 14 days, 2026-09-11 to 2026-09-24. Each run has one "start" row, so counting the "start" rows counts the runs.

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
from references import ops
from references.ops__job_events import ends_a_run
from warehouse import query, execute
job_events = ops.job_events  # -> Table

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Count each job's runs per week

Goal: one row per week and job. week_start(job_events.dt) gives the Monday that starts each day's week, as text like 2026-09-14. Name it in SELECT with AS, then group by that name in GROUP_BY, with any other column you group by:

starts_per_week = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(week_start(job_events.dt), "week"), job_events.job_id,
           AS(count_rows(), "starts")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY("week", job_events.job_id),
)
text = show_hive(starts_per_week)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  CAST(NEXT_DAY(DATE_ADD(job_events.dt, 7 * -1), 'MO') AS STRING) AS week,
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS starts
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.event_type = 'start'
  AND job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  CAST(NEXT_DAY(DATE_ADD(job_events.dt, 7 * -1), 'MO') AS STRING),
  job_events.job_id;

run_query(starts_per_week, query)  # -> DataFrame

Result, computed in pandas, not by running this Hive

weekjob_idstarts

2026-09-0713

2026-09-0721

2026-09-0731

2026-09-1417

2026-09-1425

2026-09-1432

2026-09-2114

2026-09-2124

2026-09-2131




Pasted into your own notebook, this run_query, and each run_query of a week or a month below, stops with a RuntimeError saying the Example database can't run this Hive: the Example database runs Hive with sqlglot, which has no NEXT_DAY or TRUNC. The week and month results were worked out in pandas from the same rows, to show what the Hive gives. The subtotals below run on the Example database as they are. Your warehouse runs the Hive as it is, and Check it worked below runs on the Example database too.

The Hive works the Monday out from the day: DATE_ADD(job_events.dt, 7 * -1) adds -7 days, so it is the day a week earlier, and NEXT_DAY(..., 'MO') is the first Monday after that. For Thursday 2026-09-24, a week earlier is 2026-09-17, and the first Monday after it is 2026-09-21; for Monday 2026-09-14, a week earlier is 2026-09-07, and the first Monday after it is 2026-09-14 itself. In GROUP_BY, Hive needs the calculation written out again, so the Toolbox writes it for you; you give only the name.

The 14 days start on a Friday, so they fall in three weeks: Friday to Sunday of the week of 2026-09-07, all of the week of 2026-09-14, and Monday to Thursday of the week of 2026-09-21.

Done when the result holds the weeks of 2026-09-07, 2026-09-14 and 2026-09-21. Then step 4.

4. Count each job's runs per month

Goal: one row per month and job. month_start gives the first day of each day's month, and works the same way. All 14 days are in September, so there is one month:

starts_per_month = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(month_start(job_events.dt), "month"), job_events.job_id,
           AS(count_rows(), "starts")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY("month", job_events.job_id),
)
text = show_hive(starts_per_month)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  CAST(TRUNC(job_events.dt, 'MM') AS STRING) AS month,
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS starts
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.event_type = 'start'
  AND job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  CAST(TRUNC(job_events.dt, 'MM') AS STRING),
  job_events.job_id;

run_query(starts_per_month, query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when every row's month is 2026-09-01. Then step 5.

5. Count the different jobs that ran each week

Goal: each job counted once per week. A count of different values, count_distinct, counts each value once in its group. Count from the rows themselves, grouped by week, so a job that ran on five days of a week counts once in that week:

jobs_per_week = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(week_start(job_events.dt), "week"),
           AS(count_distinct(job_events.job_id), "jobs_that_ran")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY("week"),
)
run_query(jobs_per_week, query)  # -> DataFrame

Result, computed in pandas, not by running this Hive

2026-09-213




Three different jobs ran in each week. The first mistake below shows the count that adds up each day's count instead, and what the Toolbox says.

Done when each week shows 3 jobs that ran. Then step 6.

6. Roll a Saved table's days up into weeks

Goal: weeks read from days already saved. A Saved table is a real table in the warehouse that your own Statements write. One that holds a row per day can be rolled up the same way, reading its saved days rather than counting them again. mart.team_days, a Saved table of the Project, holds each team's day, and its notebook's logic cell defines week_totals, which adds its days up into weeks; project.load gives it:

team_days = project.load("mart.team_days")  # -> the notebook's functions
week_totals = team_days.week_totals("2026-09-14", "2026-09-20")  # -> Statement
text = show_hive(week_totals)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  CAST(NEXT_DAY(DATE_ADD(team_days.dt, 7 * -1), 'MO') AS STRING) AS week,
  team_days.team,
  SUM(team_days.events) AS events,
  SUM(team_days.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(team_days.minutes) AS minutes,
  SUM(team_days.cost_cents) AS cost_cents
FROM mart.team_days AS team_days
WHERE
  team_days.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-14' AND '2026-09-20'
GROUP BY
  CAST(NEXT_DAY(DATE_ADD(team_days.dt, 7 * -1), 'MO') AS STRING),
  team_days.team;

Events, runs, minutes and costs add up, so a week's are its days' added together. A count of different values doesn't, as the first mistake shows.

Done when the Hive reads mart.team_days and groups by its week and team. Then step 7.

7. Count per team and job, with subtotals

Goal: each team's jobs, each team's subtotal and the grand total, in one result. Which team owned a job changes from day to day, so the team comes from the Project's Building block owner_on_day, a piece of the Project's code reused across notebooks, which joins each event to its job's team that day, as Look things up as of a day shows. rollup(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id) in GROUP_BY groups by both, as GROUP_BY(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id) does, then adds a subtotal row for each team, over all its jobs, and one for every row, the grand total:

from building_blocks.owner_on_day import owner_on_day
job_owners = ops.job_owners  # -> Table
runs_per_job = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id,
           AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start")), "runs"),
           AS(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=ends_a_run), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    owner_on_day(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, first_day="2026-09-18",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(rollup(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id)),
)
text = show_hive(runs_per_job)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_owners.team,
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
  SUM(
    CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END
  ) AS minutes
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = job_events.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = job_events.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_owners.team,
  job_events.job_id
  GROUPING SETS (
    (job_owners.team, job_events.job_id),
    job_owners.team,
    ()
  );

run_query(runs_per_job, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamjob_idrunsminutes

NULLNULL14206

data1.0782

financeNULL7124

finance2.0579

finance3.0245




The Hive spells out each set of rows in GROUPING SETS: each team and job, each team, and (), nothing, for one row over all of them. In a subtotal row the columns it totals over are empty, NULL, which pandas shows as NaN: the job in a team's subtotal, and the team too in the grand total. report_build, job 3, counts for finance: its team from 2026-09-18 on.

pandas keeps NaN only among fractions, so a column of whole numbers with a NaN in it shows its numbers as 1.0, 2.0 and so on: job_id here. A column that is NULL in every row has no numbers to go by, so pandas shows each NULL as None, as in step 10's job_id.

Done when the result holds one row per team and job, one per team, and the grand total, 14 runs. Then step 8.

8. Tell a subtotal row from a real NULL

Goal: know which empty value is a subtotal. A column can be empty in a real row too. Nobody owns cache_warm, job 4, so its owner is NULL, and a rollup of job_owners.owner over 2026-09-24's snapshot has two rows with NaN:

jobs_per_owner = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.owner, AS(if_else(is_total(job_owners.owner), 1, 0), "subtotal"),
           AS(count_rows(), "jobs")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(rollup(job_owners.owner)),
)
run_query(jobs_per_owner, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

ownersubtotaljobs

NULL01

NULL14

ana01

ben01

chloe01




is_total(job_owners.owner) holds on a subtotal row that totals over every owner, and nowhere else, so the first row is cache_warm, which nobody owns, and the second the grand total of all four jobs.

Done when the subtotal column is 1 on the grand total's row alone. Then step 9.

9. Label the subtotal rows

Goal: a word of your own on each subtotal row. if_else(is_total(...), ...) puts a word in place of the empty column. Its two answers should be of one type, so the job's number is written as text first, with Hive's string function: hive_function("string", job_events.job_id), which the Hive writes as CAST(job_events.job_id AS STRING):

labelled = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(if_else(is_total(job_owners.team), "every team", job_owners.team), "team"),
           AS(if_else(is_total(job_events.job_id), "every job",
                      hive_function("string", job_events.job_id)), "job"),
           AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start")), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    owner_on_day(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, first_day="2026-09-18",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(rollup(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id)),
)
run_query(labelled, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamjobruns

data17

dataevery job7

every teamevery job14

finance25

finance32

financeevery job7




The rows come sorted by their first column, as the Example database sorts them, which puts "every team" between "data" and "finance". At work rows come in no fixed order: sort them with ORDER_BY, or in pandas.

Done when the subtotal rows say "every job" and "every team". Then step 10.

10. Keep only the subtotals

Goal: each team's total and the grand total, and no other row. is_total in HAVING keeps or leaves out subtotal rows, since HAVING tests the rows GROUP_BY made, subtotals included. Put the column it tests, job_events.job_id, in SELECT too: without it, a subtotal row looks like the rows it totals, so the Toolbox refuses it.

team_totals = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id,
           AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start")), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    owner_on_day(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, first_day="2026-09-18",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(rollup(job_owners.team, job_events.job_id)),
    HAVING(is_total(job_events.job_id)),
)
run_query(team_totals, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamjob_idruns

NULLNULL14

dataNULL7

financeNULL7




Done when the result holds three rows: data's, finance's and the grand total. Then step 11.

11. Choose other sets of subtotals

Goal: the subtotals your report needs, when a rollup's aren't them. rollup drops its columns from the right, first the job, then the team: each team over its jobs, never each job over its teams.

If you want every mix, write cube(job_owners.team, job_owners.owner): each team over every owner, each owner over every team, and the grand total.
If you want to pick the sets yourself, list them with grouping_sets([job_owners.team], [job_owners.owner], []).
If you want a total within each value of a column, put that plain column beside the rollup: it is in every set, so GROUP_BY(job_events.dt, rollup(job_owners.team)) totals each day's teams, and never adds days together.


Done when your GROUP_BY names the sets of subtotal rows you want. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

Each week's runs are its days' runs added up, and the weeks' runs add up to the 28 runs over the 14 days. Check both from a count per day, which the Example database runs, with each day's Monday worked out in pandas: days.dt.weekday is 0 on a Monday and 6 on a Sunday, so taking that many days off a day gives its Monday.

import pandas as pd
starts_per_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.dt, AS(count_rows(), "starts")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.dt),
)
per_day = run_query(starts_per_day, query)  # -> DataFrame
days = pd.to_datetime(per_day["dt"])
per_day["week"] = (days - pd.to_timedelta(days.dt.weekday, unit="D")).dt.strftime(
    "%Y-%m-%d")
per_day.groupby("week")["starts"].sum()

Python shows
week
2026-09-07     5
2026-09-14    14
2026-09-21     9
Name: starts, dtype: int64

int(per_day["starts"].sum())

Python shows

These are starts_per_week's rows added up per week: 3 + 1 + 1, 7 + 5 + 2 and 4 + 4 + 1.

And each team's subtotal is its jobs' runs added up. A plain GROUP_BY, which the Example database runs, counts the same:

runs_per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team,
           AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start")), "runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    owner_on_day(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, first_day="2026-09-18",
                 last_day="2026-09-24"),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)
run_query(runs_per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

finance7




7 and 7 are the team subtotals above, and they add up to the grand total, 14.

Before you go on, check that:

the weeks' starts worked out from the days, 5, 14 and 9, are starts_per_week's rows added up per week, and add up to 28;
runs_per_team gives data 7 and finance 7, the team subtotals of runs_per_job;
those subtotals add up to the grand total, 14.


Common mistakes

Adding up each day's count of different jobs

Say a count of the different jobs on each day is already there. Adding up its days looks like the quick way to a week, but a job that ran on five days is in five days' counts, so the week would count it five times. The Toolbox refuses it as you build the Statement, with a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result:

jobs_per_day = derived("jobs_per_day", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_events.dt, AS(count_distinct(job_events.job_id), "jobs_that_ran")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.dt),
))
statement(
    SELECT(AS(week_start(jobs_per_day.dt), "week"),
           AS(sum_of(jobs_per_day.jobs_that_ran), "jobs_that_ran")),
    FROM(jobs_per_day),
    GROUP_BY("week"),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  sum_of(jobs_per_day.jobs_that_ran) adds up jobs_per_day.jobs_that_ran, which is a distinct count.
  Why it matters: Averages, ratios and distinct counts don't add up: the average of daily averages is not the weekly average, and a user seen on two days would be counted twice.
  Usual fix:      For a count over the whole span, count again with count_distinct(...) from the rows it was counted from, under your own GROUP_BY: a week's count comes from the week's rows, not from each day's count.
  Opt-out:        sum_of(jobs_per_day.jobs_that_ran, adds_up=True)

Count again from the rows, as jobs_per_week does. A plain count, such as runs, does add up: a week's runs are its days' runs added together. The gallery's re-grouping Worked example shows the wrong number the opt-out gives.

Leaving the week out of GROUP_BY

Without GROUP_BY, the Statement has no groups for the count to count in:

statement(
    SELECT(AS(week_start(job_events.dt), "week"), AS(count_rows(), "starts")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.event_type, "start"),
          between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  SELECT has the calculation "week" (from job_events.dt), but the Statement counts or adds up rows and has no GROUP_BY.
  Why it matters: Each output row is one group, so a column that isn't grouped has no single value to show. Hive would refuse the Statement.
  Usual fix:      Add GROUP_BY("week"). A calculation is grouped by the name you gave it with AS.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Its fix groups by the name you gave the week, GROUP_BY("week"): one row per week. GROUP_BY(job_events.dt), the column inside week_start, would give one row per day instead.

Reading weeks your days only partly cover

The days you read decide which days each week holds. Counting the days in each week shows it:

days_per_week = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(week_start(job_events.dt), "week"),
           AS(count_distinct(job_events.dt), "days")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY("week"),
)
run_query(days_per_week, query)  # -> DataFrame

Result, computed in pandas, not by running this Hive

weekdays

2026-09-073

2026-09-147

2026-09-214




The first and last weeks hold 3 and 4 days, so their runs look low next to the middle week's, though nothing ran less. To compare weeks, read whole weeks, from a Monday to a Sunday, as week_totals above does; for months, from the 1st to the month's last day.

Adding up a column of subtotals

Each run is counted in its job's row, its team's subtotal and the grand total, so adding up the whole "runs" column in pandas counts every run three times:

int(run_query(runs_per_job, query)["runs"].sum())

Python shows
42

Add up only the rows is_total says are real rows, or read the grand total's own row.

Writing subtotals into a Saved table

A Saved table holds one row for each Key, and subtotal rows repeat the rows they total: a sum over the table would count each row twice. mart.job_events_checks, a Saved table of the Project, holds the row count of each day of ops.job_events; a write that adds a grand total row to it is refused:

from references import mart
statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(mart.job_events_checks),
    SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "row_count"), job_events.dt),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(rollup(job_events.dt)),
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_events_checks) writes rows made by GROUP_BY(rollup(job_events.dt)).
  Why it matters: Each subtotal row totals over rows written with it, with NULL in the columns it totals over: it can repeat the Key of a real row, and a sum over the Saved table would count each row more than once.
  Usual fix:      Write the plain GROUP_BY into the Saved table, and add the subtotals in the Statement that reads it.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write the plain GROUP_BY(job_events.dt), as the table's notebook does, and add the subtotals in the Statement that reads the Saved table.

is_total in WHERE

WHERE picks the rows before GROUP_BY makes any subtotal, so there is none to test there:

statement(
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "jobs")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"), is_total(job_owners.team)),
    GROUP_BY(rollup(job_owners.team)),
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  WHERE has is_total(job_owners.team).
  Why it matters: GROUP_BY makes the subtotal rows after WHERE has picked the rows, so there is none to test in WHERE.
  Usual fix:      Test it in HAVING, or show it in SELECT, such as AS(if_else(is_total(job_owners.team), 1, 0), "is_subtotal").
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Test it in HAVING, as team_totals does.

Next

Save each day once, and rewrite the last few as rows come in late, so the weeks read from saved days: Rewrite some partitions.
Count, join and add up per group, without subtotals: Count, join and stay safe.
The gallery's Worked examples of week_start, month_start, count_distinct, rollup, cube, grouping_sets and is_total.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, HAVING, INSERT_OVERWRITE, statement, derived, rollup, is_total, equals, between, count_rows, count_distinct, sum_of, if_else, week_start, month_start, hive_function, show_hive, run_query, project.load




16. Check data quality with Statements Intermediate

Goal

Check a table's data before you trust a number built on it, with Statements you keep and run again: Keys that repeat, NULLs in each column, the rows in each Partition, and two tables that should agree. A Statement is one query, built with the Toolbox; a Partition is a slice of a table, the rows that share one value of each column it is partitioned by. Then run every check at once and gather what they find in one pandas DataFrame, and see where the Project keeps such checks: a Table notebook, the notebook that creates and writes one Saved table, made from the quality_checks Template, the empty shape of such a notebook.

When you'd use it

If a table is new to you, check it before you build on it.
If a load looked odd, check the Partitions it wrote.
If the Project's Saved tables are written every morning, check their sources first, from a Table notebook of checks (step 11).
If you want the warehouse to do the reading, write each check as a Statement: it reads every row of the Partitions you give it, and your notebook gets back only the few rows that matter.
If you haven't done the Getting started how-tos Count, join and stay safe and Save a table, do them first.


Steps

Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. 2. Import the Toolbox and the tables

Goal: the three tables the checks read. Import the Toolbox, pandas, the project module, the Project's ops Database, the named group of tables whose names start ops., and your query. This how-to reads three of its tables, each holding 14 days, 2026-09-11 to 2026-09-24: ops.job_owners, which says each day who owns each job; ops.job_events, each run's start, finish, retry or fail; and ops.region_costs, what each job cost per region and day, in cents, which writes its days like 20260911, not 2026-09-11, so its days are written that way below.

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
import pandas as pd
from references import ops
from warehouse import query, execute
job_owners = ops.job_owners  # -> Table
job_events = ops.job_events  # -> Table
region_costs = ops.region_costs  # -> Table

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Find Keys that repeat

Goal: a check that gives back each Key that names more than one row. A Table reference, the file that describes one table, lists its Key: the columns that pick out one row of the table. For ops.job_owners it is job_owners.job_id and job_owners.dt: one row per job per day. The warehouse never enforces a Key, and JOIN relies on it to warn you about repeated rows, so check it holds.

Write a check so that each row it gives back is a problem. Here, group the rows by the Key and keep, with HAVING, only the groups holding more than one row:

repeated_keys = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.dt, AS(count_rows(), "copies")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.dt),
    HAVING(more_than(count_rows(), 1)),
)
run_query(repeated_keys, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows
No rows.

No rows: no Key repeats on any of the 14 days.

Done when the result is empty. Then step 4.

4. Check the Key in a sentence

Goal: the same check, said in words. The Toolbox's own check_key does the same for the rows its where= picks, and says so in a sentence:

check_key(job_owners, query=query,  # -> Verdict
          where=between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"))

Python shows
ops.job_owners: the key (job_id, dt) holds where job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'.

Done when it says the key (job_id, dt) holds. Then step 5.

5. Count the NULLs in every column

Goal: a NULL count for each column of any table. all_columns gives every column of a Table reference, in a list, so one short function writes a NULL count for each column of any table. A column's text is the Hive that names it, such as job_owners.owner, and the part after the dot is its own name, which names each count. Which rows to count is a condition on one of the table's partition columns, since each table is partitioned its own way:

def null_counts(t: Table, where: Condition) -> Statement:
    counts = [AS(count_rows(where=is_null(column)), "null_" + str(column).split(".")[1])
              for column in all_columns(t)]
    return statement(
        SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "total_rows"), counts),
        FROM(t),
        WHERE(where),
    )

Here is the Hive it writes for ops.job_owners, one count per column:

owner_nulls = null_counts(  # -> Statement
    job_owners, between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"))
text = show_hive(owner_nulls)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  COUNT(*) AS total_rows,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_owners.job_id IS NULL THEN 1 END) AS null_job_id,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_owners.team IS NULL THEN 1 END) AS null_team,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_owners.owner IS NULL THEN 1 END) AS null_owner,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_owners.dt IS NULL THEN 1 END) AS null_dt
FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
WHERE
  job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24';

run_query(owner_nulls, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

total_rowsnull_job_idnull_teamnull_ownernull_dt

14 of the 56 rows have no owner: cache_warm has had none on any of the 14 days. The same function counts the NULLs in ops.region_costs, here its eu Partitions:

run_query(null_counts(region_costs, equals(region_costs.region, "eu")), query)

Python shows

Done when each table's result holds "total_rows" and one "null_" count per column. Then step 6.

6. Count the rows in each Partition

Goal: a row count per day, to spot a short load. A Partition holding far fewer rows than the others often means a load that broke halfway. Count the rows per day with GROUP_BY:

events_per_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.dt, AS(count_rows(), "events")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.dt),
)
run_query(events_per_day, query)  # -> DataFrame

7. Count what two tables should agree on

Goal: the two counts that should match, per day. Every run that starts should get a bill. ops.job_events says how many runs started each day, and ops.region_costs how many bills came in. Count each, per day, with one Statement each:

starts_per_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.dt,
           AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start")), "starts")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.dt),
)
bills_per_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.dt,
           AS(count_rows(where=is_not_null(region_costs.cost_cents)), "bills")),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, "20260911", "20260924")),
    GROUP_BY(region_costs.dt),
)

Done when both Statements build without an error. Then step 8.

8. Compare the two counts

Goal: each day the two tables disagree. The two tables write their days differently, so compare the two small results in pandas: write region_costs's days like job_events's, then put the two side by side with starts.merge. how="outer" keeps a day that only one of them has, with NaN, pandas' empty value, for the other count, so that day shows up as a difference too:

starts = run_query(starts_per_day, query)  # -> DataFrame
bills = run_query(bills_per_day, query)  # -> DataFrame
bills["dt"] = pd.to_datetime(bills["dt"], format="%Y%m%d").dt.strftime("%Y-%m-%d")
both = starts.merge(bills, on="dt", how="outer")  # -> DataFrame
both[both["starts"] != both["bills"]]

On 2026-09-24 two runs started but only one was billed: invoice_sync is still running, and its bill comes in when it finishes.

Done when the comparison shows 2026-09-24 alone. Then step 9.

9. Write two more checks

Goal: two more checks, each giving the rows that are problems: jobs with no owner on the newest day, and costs not billed yet:

no_owner = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.dt),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"), is_null(job_owners.owner)),
)
not_billed = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.region, region_costs.dt),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, "20260911", "20260924"),
          is_null(region_costs.cost_cents)),
)

Done when both Statements build without an error. Then step 10.

checks = {"repeated_keys": repeated_keys, "no_owner": no_owner,
          "not_billed": not_billed}
found = {name: run_query(check_statement, query)  # -> dict[str, DataFrame]
         for name, check_statement in checks.items()}
found["starts_not_billed"] = both[both["starts"] != both["bills"]]
summary = pd.DataFrame({"check": list(found),  # -> DataFrame

Common mistakes

Looking for NULLs with equals

In SQL nothing equals NULL, not even NULL, so a condition owner = NULL matches no row, and the count comes out 0 however many owners are missing. The Toolbox refuses it as you build it, with a Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result:

count_rows(where=equals(job_owners.owner, None))

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  equals(job_owners.owner, None) compares with None.
  Why it matters: In SQL nothing is equal to NULL, not even NULL, so this would match no rows and quietly give an empty or wrong result.
  Usual fix:      Use is_null(column) or is_not_null(column) to find missing values.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write is_null(column), as null_counts does, or is_not_null(column).

Expecting a day with no rows to show 0

GROUP_BY makes one row per value it finds, so a day with no rows at all is left out of the result, not shown with 0. invoice_sync, job 2, runs on weekdays only, and its count per day skips the weekends without a word:

invoice_sync_per_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.dt, AS(count_rows(), "events")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"),
          equals(job_events.job_id, 2)),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.dt),
)
per_day = run_query(invoice_sync_per_day, query)  # -> DataFrame
len(per_day)

Python shows
10

To find the days that are missing, list every day you expected, with pd.date_range, and keep those the result doesn't hold:

every_day = pd.date_range("2026-09-11", "2026-09-24").strftime("%Y-%m-%d")
[day for day in every_day if day not in set(per_day["dt"])]

Python shows
['2026-09-12', '2026-09-13', '2026-09-19', '2026-09-20']

Here the four are weekends, as expected. A missing weekday would be a load to look into.

A check that reads every Partition

A check of a partitioned table must filter one of its partition columns, as any read must. A NULL count over all of ops.region_costs is refused by a Load limit, a check that protects the cluster, naming its partition columns:

statement(SELECT(AS(count_rows(where=is_null(region_costs.cost_cents)), "nulls")),
          FROM(region_costs))

Python stops with this message
LoadRefused:
  What happened:  FROM(region_costs) reads ops.region_costs, but filters none of its partition columns: region, dt.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would read every Partition the table holds, which can stall the cluster for everyone.
  Usual fix:      Filter one of them in WHERE, as in WHERE(equals(region_costs.region, ...)), or with is_in, between or last_n_days.
  Opt-out:        FROM(region_costs, reads_all_partitions=True)

Give each check the Partitions it checks, as null_counts takes them in where.

Next

Check that a Statement gives the numbers you meant, against pandas, and review a change before you make it: Review and test a change.
Check a Saved table's Key before every write: Keys.
Put two tables' rows side by side, and find the ones that differ: Reconcile two tables.
The gallery's check_table_reference checks that a Table reference still matches its table, and check_key checks its Key on the rows you pick.


Toolbox names used: Table, check_key, all_columns, SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, HAVING, statement, Statement, equals, is_null, is_not_null, more_than, between, Condition, count_rows, show_hive, run_query, project.load




17. Call other Hive functions Intermediate

Goal

Call a Hive function the Toolbox has no function of its own for, such as upper or concat, with hive_function. Hive is the SQL your warehouse runs. Then see three things worth knowing about the Hive the Toolbox writes: what a division by 0 gives, how a Python float is written, and a function written by another name.

When you'd use it

If the Hive you need has a function the Toolbox doesn't wrap, such as the text functions upper, concat or substr, or the date functions datediff and date_format, call it with hive_function (step 3).
If the Hive you see isn't quite what you typed, read steps 4 to 6.
If you haven't done the Getting started how-tos Build and run a first statement and Count, join and stay safe, do them first.

2. Import the Toolbox and the tables

Goal: the two tables the steps read. Import the Toolbox, the Project's ops Database, the named group of tables whose names start ops., and your query:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from references import ops
from warehouse import query, execute
job_owners = ops.job_owners  # -> Table

3. Call a function the Toolbox doesn't wrap

Goal: a Hive function in a Statement, one query built with the Toolbox. hive_function takes the function's name as text, then its arguments: columns, or values, which it quotes for you. It gives back a Column, a calculation to name with AS. Here upper writes each team in capitals, and concat joins each job's owner and team into one contact, such as ana@data:

contacts = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id,
           AS(hive_function("upper", job_owners.team), "team"),
           AS(hive_function("concat", fill_null(job_owners.owner, "nobody"), "@",
                            job_owners.team), "contact")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(contacts)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_owners.job_id,
  UPPER(job_owners.team) AS team,
  CONCAT(COALESCE(job_owners.owner, 'nobody'), '@', job_owners.team) AS contact
FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
WHERE
  job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24';

run_query(contacts, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idteamcontact

1DATAana@data

2FINANCEben@finance

3FINANCEchloe@finance

4WEBnobody@web




The function concat gives NULL as soon as one of its arguments is NULL, so fill_null puts "nobody" in where a job has no owner, as job 4, cache_warm, has none.

Before writing the call, hive_function checks it: for the functions it knows, such as upper, substr or date_add, it refuses the wrong number of arguments, and it refuses a function that works only over a window of rows, such as lag or rank. The Common mistakes below show both.

Done when the result shows every team in capitals and a contact for every job. Then step 4.

4. Divide by something that could be 0

Goal: see what a division by 0 gives. Steps 4 to 6 each build a Statement, show the Hive the Toolbox writes for it, and run it. Here, how many retries each job's finished runs needed on 2026-09-24: the retries, divided by the finishes.

retries_per_finish = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id,
           AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "retry"))
              / count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")),
              "retries_per_finish")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id),
)
text = show_hive(retries_per_finish)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'retry' THEN 1 END) / COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'finish' THEN 1 END) AS retries_per_finish
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_events.job_id;

run_query(retries_per_finish, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idretries_per_finish

10.0

2NULL




invoice_sync, job 2, is still running on 2026-09-24, so it has no finish, and its row divides by 0. Hive gives that row NULL, which pandas shows as NaN, rather than stop.

Done when job 2's row shows NaN. Then step 5.

5. Compare with a Python float

Goal: see how a Python float is written. The jobs whose finished runs took more than 15.5 minutes on average. job_events.minutes holds the minutes since the run started, so on a "finish" row it is how long the run took. 15.5 is a Python float:

long_runs = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, AS(count_rows(), "finished_runs")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24"),
          equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id),
    HAVING(more_than(average_of(job_events.minutes), 15.5)),
)
text = show_hive(long_runs)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS finished_runs
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-11' AND '2026-09-24'
  AND job_events.event_type = 'finish'
GROUP BY
  job_events.job_id
HAVING
  AVG(job_events.minutes) > 15.5;

run_query(long_runs, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idfinished_runs

29

33




A Python float is written as it is, 15.5, which Hive reads as a DOUBLE, SQL's float.

Done when the Hive's HAVING ends > 15.5. Then step 6.

6. Read a hive_function call written by another name

Goal: see a function the Hive writes by another name. The function nvl gives its first argument, or its second where the first is NULL, as fill_null does. Here it puts "none" in for cache_warm, job 4, which has no owner:

owners = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, AS(hive_function("nvl", job_owners.owner, "none"), "owner")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(owners)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_owners.job_id,
  COALESCE(job_owners.owner, 'none') AS owner
FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
WHERE
  job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24';

run_query(owners, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idowner

1ana

2ben

3chloe

4none




The Hive says COALESCE, not nvl: the Toolbox writes a hive_function call as sqlglot reads it back, sometimes by another name that does the same, as COALESCE for nvl, and sometimes with an argument changed, as the Common mistakes below show for date_format. The result is the same.

Done when the Hive says COALESCE and job 4's owner is none. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

The function's name, in capitals, and each argument are in the Hive, and the result holds what the function gives: every team in capitals, and a contact for every job.

result = run_query(contacts, query)  # -> DataFrame
list(result["team"])

Python shows
['DATA', 'FINANCE', 'FINANCE', 'WEB']

int(result["contact"].isna().sum())

Python shows
0

int(...) makes the count a plain number, which numpy would otherwise print as np.int64(0).

Before you go on, check that:

the Hive of contacts names UPPER and CONCAT, each with its arguments;
every team in the result is in capitals;
no job's contact is NULL: the count above is 0.


Common mistakes

A column's name as text

hive_function quotes text for you, so "team" is the word team, not the column. Every row gets the same value, and nothing stops it:

shouting = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, AS(hive_function("upper", "team"), "team")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
run_query(shouting, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idteam

1TEAM

2TEAM

3TEAM

4TEAM




Pass the column itself, job_owners.team, as the steps do.

The whole call written as text

Only the function's name is text; its arguments go after it, one by one:

hive_function("upper(job_owners.team)")

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  hive_function('upper(job_owners.team)', ...) isn't a function name.
  Why it matters: Only the function's name is written as it is, so it must be a plain name: letters, digits and _.
  Usual fix:      Use the Hive function's name, such as hive_function("upper", jobs.team).
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write hive_function("upper", job_owners.team).

The wrong number of arguments

The function substr needs the text and where to start, and takes how many characters as a third, if you want it. Given one, the warehouse would stop, so the Toolbox refuses it first:

hive_function("substr", job_owners.owner)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  hive_function('substr', ...) gives substr 1 argument, and substr takes 2 to 3 arguments.
  Why it matters: The warehouse would stop the whole Statement with an error when it runs.
  Usual fix:      Give hive_function 2 to 3 arguments after "substr".
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Give it the number of arguments the message names, here the text and where to start.

A NULL in concat

Without fill_null, a job with no owner gets no contact at all, not "@web":

no_fill = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id,
           AS(hive_function("concat", job_owners.owner, "@", job_owners.team),
              "contact")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
run_query(no_fill, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idcontact

1ana@data

2ben@finance

3chloe@finance

4NULL




Wrap a column that may be NULL in fill_null, as contacts does.

YYYY for the year in date_format

In a date_format pattern, write yyyy for the year. YYYY is the year a week belongs to, which differs from the year in the last days of December and the first of January.

by_month = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.event_id,
           AS(hive_function("date_format", job_events.dt, "YYYY-MM"), "month")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)

No message stops you: the Toolbox writes the pattern as sqlglot reads it back, here as yyyy-MM, which is what you meant. Write yyyy anyway, so your Python says what its Hive does.

text = show_hive(by_month)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.event_id,
  DATE_FORMAT(job_events.dt, 'yyyy-MM') AS month
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt = '2026-09-24';

A function that works over a window of rows

lag, lead, rank and the other window functions need OVER (...) after them, which hive_function can't write, so it refuses them. For lag, lead, rank, dense_rank, ntile and row_number, its message names the Toolbox's own function that writes it, such as lag, which takes a window; for the others, such as percent_rank, it says to work it out in pandas. Use the function the message names; Look across rows shows the ones the Toolbox writes.

Next

lag, rank and a rolling sum, each over a Window: Look across rows.
Hive written by hand, beside your Table references, where no Toolbox function fits: Hand-written Hive with your Table references.
The gallery's hive_function entry, and fill_null for putting a value in where a column is NULL.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, HAVING, statement, equals, more_than, between, count_rows, average_of, fill_null, hive_function, show_hive, run_query




18. Look across rows, then finish in pandas Intermediate

Goal

Work out on the warehouse what looks across the rows around each row: each row's value from the row before it (lag), a rank within each group (rank), a rolling sum, and a group's total beside each row, each over a window. A Window is the rows a calculation looks across for each row: the rows of that row's group, in an order, and sometimes only those near it. Keep only some of those rows through a Derived table, a Statement given a name so another Statement can read from it, and write a Partition of a Saved table, a real table your own Statements write, that looks back on earlier Partitions. Then finish in pandas: put the results of several Statements side by side with a merge.

When you'd use it

If a step needs the rows around each row, written OVER (...) in SQL, such as how a run compares with the one before it, a running or moving total, or a rank, write it over a window (steps 3 to 7). The warehouse does that work, reading every row of the Partitions you give it, and gives back the result.
If you want only the rows a Window's result picks out, test it through a Derived table (step 8).
If a Saved table's Partition needs rows from earlier days, write it as steps 9 to 11 do.
If the numbers you need come from Statements on different tables, small enough to combine in your notebook, merge them in pandas (steps 12 and 13).
If you haven't done the Getting started how-tos Count, join and stay safe, Share Building blocks, which starts with Derived tables, and Save a table, do them first.

2. Import the Toolbox and the tables

Goal: the two tables the steps read. Import the Toolbox, pandas, the project module, the Project's ops Database, the named group of tables whose names start ops., and your query and execute:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
import pandas as pd
from references import ops
from warehouse import query, execute
job_events = ops.job_events  # -> Table
region_costs = ops.region_costs  # -> Table

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Name the Window

Goal: the rows each row looks across, named once. PARTITION_BY= makes the groups, here each job: the word is SQL's, and has nothing to do with a table's Partitions. ORDER_BY= puts each group's rows in order, here by day. Each job finishes at most once a day in the Example database, so ordering by day gives each job's runs one clear order.

each_jobs_runs = window(  # -> Window
    PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id, ORDER_BY=job_events.dt)

Done when each_jobs_runs is made without an error. Then step 4.

4. Name the rows to read

Goal: the rows to read, the same in every step: the finished runs of the last week of the Example database.

week_finished = [  # -> list[Condition]
    between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24"),
    equals(job_events.event_type, "finish"),
]

Done when week_finished holds its two Conditions. Then step 5.

5. Read the run before, with lag

Goal: how much faster or slower each run was than the job's run before it. lag gives each row the value of the row before it in its Window's order, and NULL to the first row of each job. pandas holds a missing number as NaN, and so shows that column with decimals; the tables on this page show NaN as NULL:

with_before = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, job_events.minutes,
           AS(lag(job_events.minutes, over=each_jobs_runs), "minutes_before"),
           AS(job_events.minutes - lag(job_events.minutes, over=each_jobs_runs), "change")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_finished),
)
text = show_hive(with_before)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  job_events.dt,
  job_events.minutes,
  LAG(job_events.minutes, 1) OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.dt ASC) AS minutes_before,
  job_events.minutes - LAG(job_events.minutes, 1) OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.dt ASC) AS change
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
  AND job_events.event_type = 'finish';

run_query(with_before, query)  # -> DataFrame

The Window sees only the rows the Statement reads: job 1 ran on 2026-09-17 too, but WHERE starts on 2026-09-18, so its run on 2026-09-18 has no run before it.

Done when each job's first run of the week shows NaN before it. Then step 6.

6. Rank within each group, with rank

Goal: which of each job's runs took longest. rank numbers each job's runs in its Window's order, here the longest first, with descending. Two runs that tie get the same rank, and the next run the rank after both, as job 1's two runs of 12 minutes do: both 3, then 5 for its run of 11 minutes:

longest_first = window(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id,  # -> Window
                       ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.minutes))
ranked = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, job_events.minutes,
           AS(rank(over=longest_first), "longest_first")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_finished),
)
run_query(ranked, query)  # -> DataFrame

dense_rank gives a tie the same rank too, but the next rank follows straight on: 3, 3, then 4. To keep each job's one newest run, or its top N, use row_number, which never ties: see The latest row of each group, and the top N.

Done when job 1's two runs of 12 minutes both rank 3. Then step 7.

7. Add a rolling sum, and a group's total beside each row

Goal: the minutes of each job's last three runs, added up, beside the job's minutes for the week. sum_of(..., over=...) adds up each row's Window, and gives each row its own total. rows=(-2, 0) keeps only the two rows before each row and the row itself; with no ORDER_BY and no rows=, the Window is the whole job:

last_three = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, job_events.minutes,
           AS(sum_of(job_events.minutes, over=window(
               PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id, ORDER_BY=job_events.dt, rows=(-2, 0))),
              "last_3_runs"),
           AS(sum_of(job_events.minutes, over=window(PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id)),
              "week_minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_finished),
)
text = show_hive(last_three)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  job_events.dt,
  job_events.minutes,
  SUM(job_events.minutes) OVER (
    PARTITION BY job_events.job_id
    ORDER BY job_events.dt ASC
    ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW
  ) AS last_3_runs,
  SUM(job_events.minutes) OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id) AS week_minutes
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
  AND job_events.event_type = 'finish';

run_query(last_three, query)  # -> DataFrame

8. Keep only some rows, through a Derived table

Goal: the runs that were slower than the job's run before. Hive works a Window out after it picks the rows, so a test on "change" can't go in the WHERE of the Statement that works it out (see Common mistakes). Name that Statement with derived, and test it in the Statement that reads it:

changes = derived("changes", with_before)  # -> Table
slower = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(changes.job_id, changes.dt, changes.change),
    FROM(changes),
    WHERE(more_than(changes.change, 0)),
)
run_query(slower, query)  # -> DataFrame

9. Describe the Saved table

Goal: a Table reference for the Saved table that keeps each run's change. The Saved table, mart.run_changes, has a Table reference of its own, written by hand as Describe a table by hand shows, and registered:

The file references/mart__run_changes.py, after you write it

"""mart.run_changes - each finished run beside the job's run before."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

run_changes = Table(
    "mart.run_changes",
    columns={
        "event_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "minutes": "int",
        "minutes_before": "int",  # the job's run before; NULL for its first
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["event_id", "dt"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/run_changes",
    purge=True,
)

_ = project.register("mart.run_changes")

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.budget_overruns.job_id.

Its tables: budget_overruns (references/mart__budget_overruns.py), job_day_costs
(references/mart__job_day_costs.py), job_day_facts (references/mart__job_day_facts.py),
job_events_checks (references/mart__job_events_checks.py), job_notes
(references/mart__job_notes.py), run_changes (references/mart__run_changes.py), team_days
(references/mart__team_days.py), team_failures (references/mart__team_failures.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.mart__job_events_checks import job_events_checks
from references.mart__job_notes import job_notes
from references.mart__run_changes import run_changes
from references.mart__team_days import team_days
from references.mart__team_failures import team_failures

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["budget_overruns", "job_day_costs", "job_day_facts", "job_events_checks", "job_notes", "run_changes", "team_days", "team_failures"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

10. Write a Partition that looks back on earlier ones

Goal: each day's write, its Partition at a time, with the runs before it. Each day's write needs the runs before it, which may be on earlier days. So read the earlier days too in a Derived table that keeps the day column, and keep only the day written in the Statement that writes, with WHERE(equals(week.dt, day)), naming its Partition with partition={"dt": day}. Read only the day itself, and each job's first run that day would have no run before it.

Here a job's run before may be up to 6 days back, so each write reads its day and the 6 before it:

import datetime
def changes_on(day: str) -> Statement:
    week_before = (datetime.date.fromisoformat(day)
                   - datetime.timedelta(days=6)).isoformat()
    week = derived("week", statement(
        SELECT(job_events.event_id, job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, job_events.minutes,
               AS(lag(job_events.minutes, over=each_jobs_runs), "minutes_before")),
        FROM(job_events),
        WHERE(between(job_events.dt, week_before, day),
              equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")),
    ))
    return statement(
        INSERT_OVERWRITE(run_changes, partition={"dt": day}),
        SELECT(week.event_id, week.job_id, week.minutes, week.minutes_before),
        FROM(week),
        WHERE(equals(week.dt, day)),
    )
text = show_hive(changes_on("2026-09-24"))  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
WITH week AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.event_id,
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    job_events.minutes,
    LAG(job_events.minutes, 1) OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.dt ASC) AS minutes_before
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-24'
    AND job_events.event_type = 'finish'
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.run_changes PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-24')
SELECT
  week.event_id,
  week.job_id,
  week.minutes,
  week.minutes_before
FROM week
WHERE
  week.dt = '2026-09-24';

for day in ["2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"]:

12. Add up each table per job

Goal: two small results, one row per job each. The cost of each minute a job ran comes from two tables: its minutes from ops.job_events, and its cost from ops.region_costs. Add up each per job, with a Statement each:

minutes_per_job = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
           AS(sum_of(job_events.minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_finished),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id),
)
cost_per_job = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, AS(sum_of(region_costs.cost_cents), "cost_cents")),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, "20260918", "20260924")),
    GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id),
)
minutes = run_query(minutes_per_job, query)  # -> DataFrame
costs = run_query(cost_per_job, query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when minutes and costs each hold one row per job. Then step 13.

13. Merge the results

Goal: the two results side by side, one row per job. .merge puts the two side by side, matching rows on "job_id". how="outer" keeps a job that only one of them has, and validate="one_to_one" stops the merge if "job_id" repeats on either side, which would repeat rows:

per_job = minutes.merge(  # -> DataFrame
    costs, on="job_id", how="outer", validate="one_to_one")
per_job["cents_per_minute"] = (per_job["cost_cents"] / per_job["minutes"]).round(2)
per_job

The Toolbox could join the two as Derived tables in one Statement, and on big results that is the way: the warehouse does the work. For two small results you already have, a merge in pandas is quicker to write and to change.

Common mistakes

Testing a Window's result in the same Statement

A Window is worked out after the rows are picked, so Hive can't test it in the WHERE of the Statement that works it out:

in_one_go = statement(
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(*week_finished,
          more_than(job_events.minutes - lag(job_events.minutes, over=each_jobs_runs), 0)),
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  WHERE has job_events.minutes - LAG(job_events.minutes, 1) OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id ORDER BY job_events.dt ASC) > 0, which looks across rows through a Window.
  Why it matters: Windows are worked out last, after the rows are picked and grouped, so Hive can't use it in WHERE of the SELECT that makes it.
  Usual fix:      Work it out in a derived(...) table, then use it in the Statement that reads it; help(row_number) shows how.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Test it in the Statement that reads it as a Derived table, as step 8 does.

A running total with ORDER_BY and no rows=

sum_of(job_events.minutes, over=each_jobs_runs)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  sum_of(job_events.minutes) is given a Window with ORDER_BY and no rows=.
  Why it matters: Hive would work it out over the rows up to each row and every row tied with it in ORDER_BY's order, which is seldom what is meant, so say which rows.
  Usual fix:      Give rows=(None, 0) for a running total of every row up to each one, or rows=(-6, 0) for each row and the 6 before it; leave ORDER_BY out for the whole group's.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

With ORDER_BY and no rows=, Hive would add up the rows up to each row and every row tied with it, which is seldom what's meant. Say which rows: rows=(None, 0) for every row up to each one, rows=(-2, 0) for the last three, or leave ORDER_BY out for the whole group.

Merging a result per day into one per job

Merge each job's week of minutes with its cost on each day, and each job's minutes repeat once per day, so adding them up gives far too much:

daily_cost = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.dt, region_costs.cost_cents),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, "20260918", "20260924")),
)
daily_costs = run_query(daily_cost, query)  # -> DataFrame
repeated = minutes.merge(daily_costs, on="job_id")
int(repeated["minutes"].sum()), int(minutes["minutes"].sum())

Python shows
(1035, 194)

validate="one_to_one" stops such a merge rather than let it through:

minutes.merge(daily_costs, on="job_id", validate="one_to_one")

Python stops with this message
MergeError:
Merge keys are not unique in right dataset; not a one-to-one merge
Duplicates in right:
  job_id
      1
      1
      1
      1
      1 ...

Add up per job first, as cost_per_job does, then merge.

Next

Check that a Statement, and what you do in pandas after it, give the numbers you meant: Review and test a change.
Each job's last row, or its top N, kept with row_number: The latest row of each group.
Keys: a Window keeps every row, so a Statement's Key is its FROM table's; a row_number kept at 1 gives one row per group.
The gallery's window, lag and row_number entries.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, statement, Statement, derived, equals, more_than, between, count_rows, sum_of, descending, window, rank, lag, show_hive, run_query, run_execute, project.register




19. Review and test a change Intermediate

Goal

Before you change a Building block that several Statements read, see what the change does to each of them, and check that they give the numbers you meant. A Building block is a piece of your own code that several notebooks reuse, such as a filter or a Derived table; a Statement is one query, built from the Toolbox's Clause functions. Export the Statements' Lineage, the record of which table columns feed each output column, before the change and after it, and compare the two Markdown files line by line. Then work the same answer out a second way, in pandas, from the rows themselves, and compare the two with plain asserts.

When you'd use it

If you are about to change a Building block or a Table reference that other Statements rely on, follow these steps before and after the change.
If you review someone else's change, compare the Lineage before and after it: the lines that differ are what the change does, Statement by Statement, whatever the code looked like.
If other people will read a Statement's numbers, test it against a second answer first. The Toolbox's Guards and Warnings catch mistakes of a known shape, such as a join that repeats rows; only a test catches a Statement that counts the wrong thing.
If you haven't yet, read Share Building blocks, Save a table and Follow lineage first: this how-to needs all three.


Steps

1. Start from the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in, with the Building block and the notebooks that read it. An Example project is a Project made for the Example database's tables, filled in, to read and copy from.

Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, into the folder it runs in, which Python's path holds already, as Jupyter's does for a notebook's own folder:

import shutil
from pathlib import Path
import sqlglot_composer
download = Path(sqlglot_composer.__file__).parents[1]  # -> Path
_ = shutil.copytree(download / "example_projects" / "intermediate", ".", dirs_exist_ok=True)

warehouse.py

Done when the folder you work in holds the Project's admin/, references/, building_blocks/ and tables/. Then step 2.

2. Import what the steps call

Goal: the names every step below reads. Import Python's difflib and pandas, every Toolbox name, the Toolbox's project module, your query and execute from the Project's warehouse.py, and the tables of the Database ops, a named group of tables:

import difflib
import pandas as pd
from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
from warehouse import query, execute
from references import ops
job_events, job_owners = ops.job_events, ops.job_owners  # -> Table, Table

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Build the Statements that read the Building block

Goal: the Statements the change will reach, built by one call you can make again. building_blocks/events_per_job.py is a Building block, as Share Building blocks shows: a Derived table, a Statement named so another can read from it, of each job's events, runs and minutes per day. A run's minutes are on the event that ends it, finish or fail. Two notebooks read it: mart.job_day_facts saves its numbers, and mart.team_days checks a team's day from them.

project.load gives a Table notebook's logic, its functions, as project.run_tables reads them; a Table notebook is the notebook that creates and writes one Saved table. A function of yours builds the two Statements from them, so after you change the Building block, one call builds them again; project.load reads the files as they are at that moment:

def build_statements(day: str) -> tuple[Statement, Statement]:
    facts = project.load("mart.job_day_facts")
    team_days = project.load("mart.team_days")
    return facts.write(), team_days.team_day_from_job_events(day)
facts_write, team_day = build_statements("2026-09-18")  # -> Statement, Statement
run_query(team_day, query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when run_query(team_day, query) gives the data and finance rows above. Then step 4.

4. Work out a second answer, in pandas

Goal: an answer to check team_day against, before you change anything. Fetch the rows themselves, every column of every event of the day, and each job's team that day, and work the answer out in pandas, which counts in a way of its own. This is an oracle, an answer you trust because you can read each step of it:

def rows_of(day: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    events = run_query(statement(
        SELECT(all_columns(job_events)),
        FROM(job_events),
        WHERE(equals(job_events.dt, day)),
    ), query)
    teams = run_query(statement(
        SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.team),
        FROM(job_owners),
        WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, day)),
    ), query)
    return events.merge(teams, on="job_id", how="left")
def oracle(rows: pd.DataFrame, ending: list[str]) -> pd.DataFrame:
    counted = rows.assign(
        started=rows["event_type"] == "start",
        ended=rows["minutes"].where(rows["event_type"].isin(ending), 0))
    return counted.groupby("team", as_index=False).agg(
        events=("event_id", "count"), runs=("started", "sum"), minutes=("ended", "sum"))
oracle(rows_of("2026-09-18"), ending=["finish", "fail"])  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teameventsrunsminutes

data2114

finance4234




Fetching every row is fine for a test over a day or two; it is what the Statement saves you from on the whole table.

Done when the oracle gives the same rows as team_day did in step 3. Then step 5.

5. Compare the two with assert

Goal: a test that stops when the Statement and the oracle disagree. assert stops with an AssertionError when what follows it is False, and does nothing when it is True. same_rows puts each DataFrame's rows in one order, by "team", and turns them into a list of plain dicts, which compare with == as Python lists do:

def same_rows(result: pd.DataFrame, expected: pd.DataFrame) -> bool:
    return (result.sort_values("team").to_dict("records")
            == expected.sort_values("team").to_dict("records"))
def test_team_day(s: Statement, day: str, ending: list[str]) -> None:
    result = run_query(s, query)
    expected = oracle(rows_of(day), ending)
    assert same_rows(result, expected), f"{day}:\n{result}\n{expected}"
test_team_day(team_day, "2026-09-18", ending=["finish", "fail"])

It printed nothing, so the assert held. Choose the days to hold the awkward cases: here 2026-09-18, when report_build failed, so a run ended without finishing.

Done when test_team_day prints nothing. Then step 6.

6. Export the Lineage before the change

Goal: a record of what the Statements do now, to compare with later. Pass both Statements to export_lineage, so one Lineage covers everything the Building block feeds. to= gives the files a name you can find again; without it, each export gets a new name, from the time it ran:

html_file, markdown_file = export_lineage(facts_write, team_day,
                                          to="before.html")  # -> tuple[Path, Path]
