## facts_write

It writes the Saved table mart.job_day_facts, replacing each partition its rows fill.

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `events_per_job in facts_write.events`

Calculated in **events_per_job in facts_write** as `count_rows()`, which is `COUNT(*)`.

```text
events_per_job in facts_write.events = count_rows()
└─ (no columns: it counts rows)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in events_per_job in facts_write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

#### `events_per_job in facts_write.runs`

Calculated in **events_per_job in facts_write** as `count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start"))`, which is `COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END)`.

```text
events_per_job in facts_write.runs = count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start"))
└─ ops.job_events.event_type  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in events_per_job in facts_write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

#### `events_per_job in facts_write.minutes`

Calculated in **events_per_job in facts_write** as `fill_null(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=is_in(job_events.event_type, ["finish", "fail"])), 0)`, which is `COALESCE(SUM(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END), 0)`.

```text
events_per_job in facts_write.minutes = fill_null(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=is_in(job_events.event_type, ["finish", "fail"])), 0)
├─ ops.job_events.minutes  (int)
└─ ops.job_events.event_type  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in events_per_job in facts_write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `job_id` | events_per_job in facts_write.job_id ← ops.job_events.job_id |
| `team` | ops.job_owners.team |
| `events` | events_per_job in facts_write.events (calculated) |
| `runs` | events_per_job in facts_write.runs (calculated) ← ops.job_events.event_type |
| `minutes` | events_per_job in facts_write.minutes (calculated) ← ops.job_events.minutes |
| `dt` | events_per_job in facts_write.dt ← ops.job_events.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('events_per_job', ...): Key (job_id, dt), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt.
- facts_write: Key (job_id, dt), since events_per_job's rows are told apart by job_id, dt.
- mart.job_day_facts' Key (job_id, dt) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(
      SUM(
        CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END
      ),
      0
    ) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  events_per_job.job_id,
  job_owners.team,
  events_per_job.events,
  events_per_job.runs,
  events_per_job.minutes,
  events_per_job.dt
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
```

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('events_per_job', ...): Key (job_id, dt), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt.
- team_day: Key (team), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each team.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(
      SUM(
        CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END
      ),
      0
    ) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-18'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
SELECT
  job_owners.team,
  SUM(events_per_job.events) AS events,
  SUM(events_per_job.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(events_per_job.minutes) AS minutes
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-18'
GROUP BY
  job_owners.team
```

7. Change the Building block

Goal: the new meaning, in the one file that holds it. Say only a finished run's minutes count now, and a failed run's count none: the minutes line of events_per_job changes, from where=ends_a_run, the Alias of a finish or a fail, to where=equals(job_events.event_type, "finish"), and its docstring and imports with it. An Alias is a name you give by hand, in a Table reference, to a column or a condition:

The file building_blocks/events_per_job.py, after you change it

"""events_per_job - each job's events, runs and minutes for each dt it reads.

Why: mart.job_day_facts saves these numbers, and mart.team_days' notebook checks a day from
them too, and writing them once here means the two always count them the same way.

A run's minutes are the minutes since it started, on its finish event: a run that failed
counts no minutes. A day with no run finishing has 0 minutes, not NULL.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Table, between, count_rows, derived, equals,
    fill_null, statement, sum_of,
)
from references.ops__job_events import job_events


def events_per_job(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Table:
    """One row per job and dt from first_day to last_day: its events, runs and minutes.

    The day, dt, ops.job_events' partition column, is kept in GROUP_BY, so each day's numbers
    stay apart when first_day and last_day are days apart, and in SELECT, so a Statement that
    reads this can join each row to another table on its day, and a write that reads it fills
    each day's Partition from it.
    """
    return derived(
        "events_per_job",
        statement(
            SELECT(
                job_events.job_id,
                job_events.dt,
                AS(count_rows(), "events"),
                AS(count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start")), "runs"),
                AS(fill_null(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")), 0), "minutes"),
            ),
            FROM(job_events),
            WHERE(between(job_events.dt, first_day, last_day)),
            GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt),
        ),
    )

Done when building_blocks/events_per_job.py holds the file above. Then step 8.

## facts_write

It writes the Saved table mart.job_day_facts, replacing each partition its rows fill.

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `events_per_job in facts_write.events`

Calculated in **events_per_job in facts_write** as `count_rows()`, which is `COUNT(*)`.

```text
events_per_job in facts_write.events = count_rows()
└─ (no columns: it counts rows)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in events_per_job in facts_write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

#### `events_per_job in facts_write.runs`

Calculated in **events_per_job in facts_write** as `count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start"))`, which is `COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END)`.

```text
events_per_job in facts_write.runs = count_rows(where=equals(job_events.event_type, "start"))
└─ ops.job_events.event_type  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in events_per_job in facts_write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

#### `events_per_job in facts_write.minutes`

Calculated in **events_per_job in facts_write** as `fill_null(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")), 0)`, which is `COALESCE(SUM(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'finish' THEN job_events.minutes END), 0)`.

```text
events_per_job in facts_write.minutes = fill_null(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")), 0)
├─ ops.job_events.minutes  (int)
└─ ops.job_events.event_type  (string)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in events_per_job in facts_write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `job_id` | events_per_job in facts_write.job_id ← ops.job_events.job_id |
| `team` | ops.job_owners.team |
| `events` | events_per_job in facts_write.events (calculated) |
| `runs` | events_per_job in facts_write.runs (calculated) ← ops.job_events.event_type |
| `minutes` | events_per_job in facts_write.minutes (calculated) ← ops.job_events.minutes |
| `dt` | events_per_job in facts_write.dt ← ops.job_events.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('events_per_job', ...): Key (job_id, dt), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt.
- facts_write: Key (job_id, dt), since events_per_job's rows are told apart by job_id, dt.
- mart.job_day_facts' Key (job_id, dt) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(SUM(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'finish' THEN job_events.minutes END), 0) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  events_per_job.job_id,
  job_owners.team,
  events_per_job.events,
  events_per_job.runs,
  events_per_job.minutes,
  events_per_job.dt
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
```

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('events_per_job', ...): Key (job_id, dt), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt.
- team_day: Key (team), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each team.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(SUM(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'finish' THEN job_events.minutes END), 0) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-18'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
SELECT
  job_owners.team,
  SUM(events_per_job.events) AS events,
  SUM(events_per_job.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(events_per_job.minutes) AS minutes
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-18' AND '2026-09-18'
GROUP BY
  job_owners.team
```

Made by export_lineage on 2026-09-25 09:00, from your scripts at commit 1a2b3c4, with sqlglot Composer 8.2.


It writes after.html too, a page to open in your browser.

Done when the folder holds after.html and after.md beside the before files. Then step 10.

10. Compare the two Markdown files

Goal: the lines the change made differ, Statement by Statement. Python's difflib.ndiff compares two lists of lines and marks each line that only the first holds with "- ", and each that only the second holds with "+ ". changed_lines prints those lines, each under the part of the Markdown file it is in: the Statement, from its ## heading, then the column or the Hive, from the heading under that. It leaves out the last line of each file, which says when the file was made, and so differs every time:

def changed_lines(before_file: str, after_file: str) -> None:
    before = Path(before_file).read_text(encoding="utf-8").splitlines()
    after = Path(after_file).read_text(encoding="utf-8").splitlines()
    part = heading = shown = None
    for line in difflib.ndiff(before, after):
        text = line[2:]
        if text.startswith("## "):
            part, heading = text[3:], None
        elif text.startswith("###"):
            heading = text.lstrip("# ")
        if line.startswith(("- ", "+ ")) and not text.startswith("Made by export_lineage"):
            where = part if heading is None else f"{part}, {heading}"
            if where != shown:
                print(f"In {where}:")
                shown = where
            print(line[:100])
changed_lines("before.md", "after.md")

Under the minutes column of each Statement's events_per_job, the calculation changed, so every number added up from it may change, in both Statements; in each Hive, the minutes' CASE WHEN changed. Nothing else did: no column came or went, and no column takes its value from anywhere new.

11. Run the test against the old oracle

Goal: see the change on the numbers. Run the test's comparison again. It fails on 2026-09-18, the day report_build failed: the oracle still counts a failed run's minutes, and the changed Building block doesn't. The finance team's minutes fell by 12, the minutes report_build ran before it failed:

result = run_query(team_day, query)
expected = oracle(rows_of("2026-09-18"), ending=["finish", "fail"])
assert same_rows(result, expected), f"2026-09-18:\n{result}\n{expected}"

Python stops with this message
AssertionError:
2026-09-18:
      team  events  runs  minutes
0     data       2     1       14
1  finance       4     2       22
      team  events  runs  minutes
0     data       2     1       14
1  finance       4     2       34

That is the change doing what it was meant to, on exactly the day and team it was meant to.

Done when the AssertionError shows only finance's minutes differing, 22 against 34. Then step 12.

12. Test the new meaning on several days

Goal: a test that holds for the new meaning. Change the oracle to finished runs only, ending=["finish"], and test it on several days, the awkward ones among them. test_team_day prints nothing when every assert holds:

for day in ["2026-09-14", "2026-09-18", "2026-09-24"]:
    facts_write, team_day = build_statements(day)
    test_team_day(team_day, day, ending=["finish"])

Python shows
False

Before you go on, check that:

changed_lines("before.md", "after.md") lists changes only under minutes and the Hive;
the after file names where=equals(job_events.event_type, "finish"), and the before file doesn't, as above;
test_team_day with ending=["finish"] prints nothing on each day you test.


Common mistakes

Exporting the Statements built before the change

A Statement holds its Building block as it was when it was built. Change the file, but export the Statements you already had, and the Lineage after looks just like the one before. Here team_day_before was built before the change, and still counts a failed run's minutes:

"IN ('finish', 'fail')" in to_hive(team_day_before)

Python shows
True

"IN ('finish', 'fail')" in to_hive(team_day)

Python shows
False

Build the Statements again after each change, with build_statements, then export. A function you imported yourself from the Building block also stays as it was until you restart the notebook's kernel: project.load reads the files again each time, an import of your own reads them once.

Comparing two DataFrames with ==

== between two DataFrames compares them cell by cell and gives back a third DataFrame, of True and False. assert can't say whether a whole DataFrame is true, so it stops:

facts_write, team_day = build_statements("2026-09-18")
result = run_query(team_day, query)
expected = oracle(rows_of("2026-09-18"), ending=["finish"])
assert result == expected

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
The truth value of a DataFrame is ambiguous. Use a.empty, a.bool(), a.item(), a.any() or a.all().

Compare lists of rows, as same_rows does, or use pandas' own pd.testing.assert_frame_equal.

Comparing rows in the order they came back

The Example database gives rows in a fixed order, but your warehouse gives them in no fixed order. Compare without sorting, and a right answer fails whenever the order differs, as it does here, with the most minutes first:

most_first = expected.sort_values("minutes", ascending=False)
result.to_dict("records") == most_first.to_dict("records")

Python shows
False

same_rows(result, most_first)

Python shows
True

Put both in one order before comparing, as same_rows does with "team".

An oracle that asks the Statement

An oracle built from the Statement's own result always agrees with it, right or wrong. No message stops you:

same_rows(result, result)

Python shows
True

Work the oracle out from the rows themselves, as oracle does from rows_of, and count in a way of its own, apart from the Statement's.

Next

Check data quality: checks on the data itself, so a Statement you've tested gets rows you can trust.
Declare a change made in Python: a step whose Lineage is one box, naming the function to review.
The gallery's export_lineage and derived entries.


Toolbox names used: all_columns, SELECT, FROM, WHERE, statement, Statement, equals, to_hive, run_query, export_lineage, project.load




20. Choose how a Saved table is stored Intermediate

Goal

Say in a Saved table's Table reference how the warehouse keeps it: the folder of its own its files go in, whether dropping it deletes them, their format, its Partitions, and Buckets. A Saved table is a real table in the warehouse that your own Statements write; its Table reference is the file that describes it, its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key. create_table writes all of it, and check_table_reference compares it with the table. Along the way, see why the Toolbox has no indexes, and what makes reads fast instead.

When you'd use it

If you fill in a Saved table's Table reference, give it at least its folder: every Saved table needs one.
If you check a Table reference you already have, compare it with the table, as Check it worked does.
If a table grows big, read a region at a time or joined on the same column every run, keep it so that reads touch fewer files, with Partitions and Buckets.
If you haven't made a Saved table yet, read Save a table first, which makes one with project.new_table.


Steps

"""mart.job_region_costs - TODO: say in one line what one row is.

project.reference_from_logic("mart.job_region_costs") writes this file again from the table's logic,
filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

job_region_costs = Table(
    "mart.job_region_costs",
    columns={"<COLUMNS>": "string"},  # project.reference_from_logic writes them, from the logic
    location=None,  # TODO: its own folder, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs"
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.mart__job_region_costs import job_region_costs, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = job_region_costs.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or

3. Fill in how it is stored

Goal: a Table reference that says how the table is stored. Fill it in as below; steps 5 to 8 explain its lines on storage:

The file references/mart__job_region_costs.py, after you fill it in

"""mart.job_region_costs - one row per job, region and day: its cost there, in cents."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

job_region_costs = Table(
    "mart.job_region_costs",
    columns={
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "cost_cents": "bigint",
        "region": "string",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["region", "dt"],
    key=["job_id", "region", "dt"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs",  # its own folder
    purge=True,  # dropping the table deletes its files
    compression="ZLIB",
    properties={"made_by": "mart__job_region_costs.ipynb"},
)

Done when references/mart__job_region_costs.py holds the file above. Then step 4.

4. Refresh the Database module and import the tables

project.refresh_reference_sheet()

Restart the kernel of a notebook that imported references, to see a table added since.

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.budget_overruns.job_id.

Its tables: budget_overruns (references/mart__budget_overruns.py), job_day_costs
(references/mart__job_day_costs.py), job_day_facts (references/mart__job_day_facts.py),
job_events_checks (references/mart__job_events_checks.py), job_notes
(references/mart__job_notes.py), job_region_costs (references/mart__job_region_costs.py),
team_days (references/mart__team_days.py), team_failures (references/mart__team_failures.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.mart__job_events_checks import job_events_checks
from references.mart__job_notes import job_notes
from references.mart__job_region_costs import job_region_costs
from references.mart__team_days import team_days
from references.mart__team_failures import team_failures

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["budget_overruns", "job_day_costs", "job_day_facts", "job_events_checks", "job_notes", "job_region_costs", "team_days", "team_failures"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

It writes references/reference_sheet.csv too.

from references import mart, ops
job_region_costs, region_costs = mart.job_region_costs, ops.region_costs  # -> Table, Table

Done when references/mart.py lists mart.job_region_costs. Then step 5.

5. Give it a folder of its own

Goal: files no other table reads. Every Saved table is an external table: its files are yours, in a folder you name. You give location= the table's own folder, and the Toolbox writes it exactly as you give it, adding nothing. A folder named after the database and the table, such as /data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs, keeps each table's folder apart.

A folder of its own matters because the warehouse reads every file in a table's folder. Two tables in one folder read each other's rows, and an INSERT OVERWRITE of a Partition in one can delete the other's files for that Partition. The Toolbox refuses a Table reference whose folder is another Saved table's, or sits inside it.

A path you would rather not write out in every Table reference can start with a Hive variable, such as location="${hivevar:root}/mart.db/job_region_costs": the Toolbox writes it as it is, and Hive fills it in from a SET hivevar:root=/data/your_team sent first, with hive_settings(..., sets=...) as Keep reads and joins fast sends its settings.

Done when location= names a folder no other Saved table uses. Then step 6.

6. Say what dropping it does

Goal: a drop that does what you want with the files. purge=True makes dropping the table delete its files too. Left out, or purge=False, the warehouse only forgets the table: its files stay in its folder, and a table created there again gets the old rows back once repair_partitions lists their Partitions. Write purge=True for a table you rebuild from its sources; leave it out for one whose files you would want back.

Done when the Table reference says purge=True, or leaves it out on purpose. Then step 7.

7. Choose its file format

Goal: files Hive reads fast. stored_as= is the file format: "ORC", the usual one and what you get when you leave it out, which Hive reads fastest, or "PARQUET", which more tools outside Hive read. compression= is how its files are packed: ORC's "ZLIB" makes smaller files, "SNAPPY" faster ones. properties= takes any other table property. create_table writes them all:

text = show_hive(create_table(job_region_costs, may_exist=True))  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE IF NOT EXISTS mart.job_region_costs (
  job_id BIGINT,
  cost_cents BIGINT,
  PRIMARY KEY (job_id, region, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  region STRING,
  dt STRING
)
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs'
TBLPROPERTIES (
  'external.table.purge'='true',
  'orc.compress'='ZLIB',
  'made_by'='mart__job_region_costs.ipynb'
);

Done when the Hive shows STORED AS ORC and the TBLPROPERTIES you gave. Then step 8.

8. Choose its Partitions

Goal: reads that open only the folders they need. A Partition is the rows that share one value of each column a table is partitioned by. partitioned_by=["region", "dt"] keeps each region's rows in a folder of their own, and each of its days in a folder inside that, as ops.region_costs does. A Statement that filters region reads only that region's folders, and one that filters dt too reads fewer still. A table's Partitions are whichever columns its reads filter most: a region, a day, a load, or none at all, as the Example project's mart.job_notes has none.

Done when partitioned_by= lists the columns your reads filter most. Then step 9.

9. Write each Partition from its rows

Goal: a write that fills each Partition from its rows' own values. Its SELECT names every partition column, anywhere: the Toolbox puts them last, in the order partitioned_by= gives, where Hive reads them from. Here ops.region_costs writes its days like 20260924, and mart.job_region_costs keeps them that way, since the Toolbox writes each value exactly as it is given:

def write_days(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Statement:
    return statement(
        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_region_costs),
        SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.region, region_costs.dt,
               AS(sum_of(region_costs.cost_cents), "cost_cents")),
        FROM(region_costs),
        WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, first_day, last_day)),
        GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.region, region_costs.dt),
    )
text = show_hive(write_days("20260922", "20260924"))

Hive, ready to paste
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills region, dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_region_costs PARTITION(region, dt)
SELECT
  region_costs.job_id,
  SUM(region_costs.cost_cents) AS cost_cents,
  region_costs.region,
  region_costs.dt
FROM ops.region_costs AS region_costs
WHERE
  region_costs.dt BETWEEN '20260922' AND '20260924'
GROUP BY
  region_costs.job_id,
  region_costs.region,
  region_costs.dt;

The columns in PARTITION(...) have no value: the warehouse takes each row's region and dt from the SELECT's last columns. Doing so is Hive's dynamic partitioning, which a Session setting switches on, so show_hive prints its two SET lines first. A Session setting is a Hive SET sent before a Statement, which holds for the rest of the session.

Done when the Hive ends with region and dt as the SELECT's last columns. Then step 10.

10. Send the Session setting first

Goal: dynamic partitioning switched on before the write. run_execute sends the write alone: send the Session setting first, with hive_settings, before the first such write. The warehouse keeps it for as long as your execute's connection lasts, and sending it again does no harm. project.run_tables() sends it for you. Here it goes to the Example database's execute, which prints each SET line:

run_execute(hive_settings("dynamic_partitions"), execute)

Python prints
execute: SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true
execute: SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict

INSERT OVERWRITE replaces only the Partitions its rows hold: a region and day with no rows keeps whatever it held before. So if a region drops out of the table you read, its old rows stay in the Saved table until you drop them with the admin module's drop_partitions.

Done when execute shows both SET lines. Then step 11.

11. Name some Partitions in the write itself

Goal: a write into chosen Partitions only. A write can name some Partitions itself instead, with partition={...}: here the eu region, whose rows then leave region out of SELECT. The columns it names come first in partitioned_by=, and the rest are filled from the rows:

text = show_hive(statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_region_costs, partition={"region": "eu"}),
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.dt,
           AS(sum_of(region_costs.cost_cents), "cost_cents")),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(equals(region_costs.region, "eu"),
          between(region_costs.dt, "20260922", "20260924")),
    GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.dt),
))

Hive, ready to paste
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_region_costs PARTITION(region = 'eu', dt)
SELECT
  region_costs.job_id,
  SUM(region_costs.cost_cents) AS cost_cents,
  region_costs.dt
FROM ops.region_costs AS region_costs
WHERE
  region_costs.region = 'eu' AND region_costs.dt BETWEEN '20260922' AND '20260924'
GROUP BY
  region_costs.job_id,
  region_costs.dt;

Done when the Hive's PARTITION(...) reads region = 'eu', dt. Then step 12.

12. Make a big table's Table folder

Goal: a table big enough for Buckets. A Bucket is one of a fixed number of files a table's rows are spread into by hashing some of its columns. bucketed_by= with buckets= spreads each Partition's rows into that many files by the values of those columns, and sorted_by= sorts the rows in each. A join on those columns can then pair file with file instead of reading everything. It pays off only on big tables, tens of millions of rows a Partition, joined on those columns; and changing it means writing the whole table again. Most Saved tables need none: add Buckets only to a big table. Say mart.event_facts, every event with its minutes, is such a table, joined on job_id every run:

project.new_table("mart.event_facts")

Python shows
Made tables/mart__event_facts/: mart__event_facts.ipynb and lineage/, from
admin/templates/saved_table.py.
Wrote references/mart__event_facts.py: project.reference_from_logic writes it.

"""mart.event_facts - TODO: say in one line what one row is.

project.reference_from_logic("mart.event_facts") writes this file again from the table's logic,
filling in what is left. They are:
    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py, to stand in
until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

event_facts = Table(
    "mart.event_facts",
    columns={"<COLUMNS>": "string"},  # project.reference_from_logic writes them, from the logic
    location=None,  # TODO: its own folder, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/event_facts"
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.mart__event_facts import event_facts, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = event_facts.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(event_facts.<column>, <value>)

13. Add its Buckets

Goal: a big table kept in Buckets on the column it is joined on. Fill in its Table reference with bucketed_by=, buckets= and sorted_by=:

The file references/mart__event_facts.py, after you fill it in

"""mart.event_facts - one row per event of a run, with its minutes."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

event_facts = Table(
    "mart.event_facts",
    columns={"event_id": "bigint", "job_id": "bigint", "minutes": "int", "dt": "string"},
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["event_id"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/event_facts",
    bucketed_by=["job_id"],
    buckets=16,
    sorted_by=["job_id"],
)

from references import mart ran before this file was filled in, so mart doesn't have it until the kernel restarts; its own file gives it now:

from references.mart__event_facts import event_facts
print(to_hive(create_table(event_facts)))

Hive
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE mart.event_facts (
  event_id BIGINT,
  job_id BIGINT,
  minutes INT,
  PRIMARY KEY (event_id) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  dt STRING
)
CLUSTERED BY (job_id) SORTED BY (job_id) INTO 16 BUCKETS
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/event_facts'

Done when the Hive shows CLUSTERED BY (job_id) SORTED BY (job_id) INTO 16 BUCKETS. Then check it worked, below.

Why there are no indexes

Hive removed indexes in its version 3, so the Toolbox writes none. What makes a read fast instead:

Partitions: a Statement that filters a partition column, as every Load limit insists, opens only those Partitions' folders, and one that filters a second partition column too opens fewer still.
Fewer, larger files: ORC and Parquet files keep each column's smallest and largest values, so the warehouse skips the parts of a file a condition rules out, and a few large files open faster than many small ones.
Buckets, for big tables joined on the same columns, as above.
Statistics, what the warehouse has counted about a table: once it has counted a table's rows and values, it picks how to join it, such as sending a small table to every machine instead of sorting both.


Keep reads and joins fast shows the last three at work.

Check it worked

The write's rows come from its SELECT, which the Example database can run on its own: on 2026-09-24, one job billed in eu has its cost in, and one has its bill still to come.

costs_on_the_day = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.region, region_costs.dt,
           AS(sum_of(region_costs.cost_cents), "cost_cents")),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(equals(region_costs.dt, "20260924")),
    GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.region, region_costs.dt),
)
run_query(costs_on_the_day, query)  # -> DataFrame

At work, once create_table has made the table, check_table_reference sends DESCRIBE FORMATTED, which lists how the table is kept, and compares that too. A stand-in query plays a table made by hand at a folder it shares with other tables, /data/your_team/tables, as the warehouse would describe it:

import pandas as pd
def query_at_work(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    return pd.DataFrame([
        ("job_id", "bigint", ""), ("cost_cents", "bigint", ""),
        ("# Partition Information", None, None), ("# col_name", "data_type", "comment"),
        ("region", "string", ""), ("dt", "string", ""),
        ("# Detailed Table Information", None, None),
        ("Location:", "hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/tables", None),
        ("Table Type:", "EXTERNAL_TABLE", None),
        ("Table Parameters:", None, None),
        ("", "external.table.purge", "true"), ("", "orc.compress", "ZLIB"),
        ("", "made_by", "mart__job_region_costs.ipynb"),
        ("# Storage Information", None, None),
        ("InputFormat:", "org.apache.hadoop.hive.ql.io.orc.OrcInputFormat", None),
    ], columns=["col_name", "data_type", "comment"])
check_table_reference(job_region_costs, query=query_at_work)

Python shows
mart.job_region_costs differs from its Table reference.
Problems:
  - it is at hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/tables, not at /data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs, the folder location= names: change the line to location="hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/tables", if that is the own folder of the table. A folder that isn't a table's own may hold other tables' files: two tables in one folder read each other's files, and an INSERT OVERWRITE of a Partition in one can delete the other's files for that Partition. Move it with admin.move_to_own_folder(t, query=...).

Everything else matches. The warehouse writes a folder in full, with hdfs://namenode:8020 before it, and the Toolbox compares the folder alone. Move a Saved table to its own folder moves a table that shares a folder.

Before you go on, check that:

costs_on_the_day gives job 1's cost in eu and job 2's still to come, as above;
show_hive(create_table(...)) shows the LOCATION, PARTITIONED BY and TBLPROPERTIES your Table reference gives;
at work, check_table_reference names no problem once the table is made.


Common mistakes

Giving two Saved tables one folder

location= is the table's own folder, and no other Saved table may use it, nor a folder inside it. A second Table reference copied from the first, with its name changed and its folder not, is refused:

Table("mart.job_region_costs_eu", columns={"job_id": "bigint", "dt": "string"},
      partitioned_by=["dt"], location="/data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs")

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  Table('mart.job_region_costs_eu') would be at /data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs, the same folder as mart.job_region_costs (/data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs).
  Why it matters: Every Saved table needs a folder of its own: two tables in one folder read each other's files, and an INSERT OVERWRITE of a Partition in one can delete the other's files for that Partition.
  Usual fix:      Give each its own folder in location=, such as "/data/your_team/mart.db/daily_runs" for mart.daily_runs.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Give it a folder of its own, location="/data/your_team/mart.db/job_region_costs_eu".

Leaving a partition column out of the write

A write fills each partition column partition= doesn't name from its rows, so its SELECT must list it. Left out, it is refused as you build it:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_region_costs),
    SELECT(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.dt,
           AS(sum_of(region_costs.cost_cents), "cost_cents")),
    FROM(region_costs),
    WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, "20260922", "20260924")),
    GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id, region_costs.dt),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_region_costs): it leaves out region.
  Why it matters: The warehouse fills a table's columns by position, not by name, so a missing or extra column would put values in the wrong columns.
  Usual fix:      SELECT every column of job_region_costs's Table reference, each under its own name, except a partition column the write names in partition={...}. The Toolbox puts them in the right order, its partition columns last.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

List region in SELECT, as write_days does.

Naming a later Partition but not an earlier one

Hive takes the partition columns in order: the ones a write names first, then the ones it fills from the rows. So partition={"dt": "20260924"} alone, with region left to the rows, is refused:

INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_region_costs, partition={"dt": "20260924"})

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_region_costs, partition=...) names dt, but not region, which comes before it in mart.job_region_costs's partitioned_by (region, dt).
  Why it matters: Hive takes the partition columns in order: first the ones partition= gives a value, then the others by name alone, so a column after one with no value can't have one either.
  Usual fix:      Give region a value too, or leave dt out as well: then the write fills each partition column given no value from the rows' own values.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Name region too, or leave dt to the rows.

Storage on a table you only read

Only your own Saved tables are stored as you say. A Table reference with no location= is a table someone else owns, and giving it storage is refused:

Table("ops.other_costs", columns={"job_id": "bigint", "dt": "string"},
      partitioned_by=["dt"], purge=True)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  Table('ops.other_costs') has purge= but no location=.
  Why it matters: purge= is part of how one of your own Saved tables is stored, and each of those is in a folder of its own, its location=.
  Usual fix:      Add location=..., such as location="/data/your_team/mart.db/daily_runs", or remove purge=: a table someone else owns needs none of it.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Give storage only in the Table reference of one of your own Saved tables, beside its location=.

Next

Move a table that shares a folder: Move a Saved table to its own folder.
Statistics, small files and map joins: Keep reads and joins fast.
Write some of a table's Partitions again: Rewrite some partitions.
Every option in one place: the gallery's create_table entry, and help(Table).


Toolbox names used: Table, check_table_reference, create_table, SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, statement, Statement, equals, between, sum_of, to_hive, show_hive, run_query, run_execute, hive_settings, project.new_table, project.refresh_reference_sheet




21. Keys, checked before each write Intermediate

Goal

Give a table a Key, the columns whose values name exactly one row of it, and let the Toolbox check it: a write into a Saved table, a real table your own Statements write, whose rows the Toolbox can't see are one per Key is refused before anything is sent, check_key counts the rows themselves, and Lineage, the record of which table columns feed each output column, shows each step's Key. A table with no Key, such as ops.team_notes, is written unchecked, and a join onto it warns.

When you'd use it

If you fill in a Table reference, the file that describes one table, write its Key in it.
If you make a Saved table, give it a Key and let each write be checked against it. Hive never checks a Key: a table declared with one takes two rows with the same Key without a word. Then every join on that Key repeats rows, and every sum over the join comes out too big, far from the write that caused it. The Toolbox checks the Key while the write is built, where the mistake is.
If a table already holds rows, count them against its Key with check_key, as Check it worked does.
If you haven't yet, read Add your tables' references, Count, join and stay safe and Save a table first: this how-to needs all three.


Steps

1. Start from the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project to work in. An Example project is a Project made for the Example database's tables, filled in, to read and copy from.

Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, into the folder it runs in, which Python's path holds already, as Jupyter's does for a notebook's own folder:

import shutil
from pathlib import Path
import sqlglot_composer
download = Path(sqlglot_composer.__file__).parents[1]  # -> Path
_ = shutil.copytree(download / "example_projects" / "intermediate", ".", dirs_exist_ok=True)

2. Import what the steps call

Goal: the names every step below reads. Import the Toolbox, its project module, the Project's query and execute from warehouse.py, which here call the Example database, and the Project's ops Database, a named group of tables:

from sqlglot_composer import *

Done when the imports run without an error. Then step 3.

3. Read the Keys of the tables you read

Goal: know what names one row of each table. key= lists the columns that, together, pick out one row: no two rows have the same values in all of them. Which columns those are depends on what a row is, never on how the table is partitioned into Partitions, the slices of rows that share a value of each partition column.

Each Table reference in the Project's references/ says its Key, such as references/ops__job_events.py:

ops.job_events has a row per event, and every event has its own event_id: key=["event_id"]. It is partitioned by dt, but an event_id names a row on its own, so dt isn't in the Key.
ops.job_owners has a row per job per day, a snapshot of each job each day: one job_id comes back on every day, so naming a row takes the job and the day, key=["job_id", "dt"]. dt is its partition column too; that it is in the Key is because a row needs it.
ops.team_notes has no columns that name a row, since a team can write the same note twice: key=None. A table with no Key is fine; what the Toolbox can check of it is less.


Done when you can say each table's Key from what one of its rows is. Then step 4.

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.budget_overruns.job_id.

Its tables: budget_overruns (references/mart__budget_overruns.py), job_day_costs
(references/mart__job_day_costs.py), job_day_events (references/mart__job_day_events.py),
job_day_facts (references/mart__job_day_facts.py), job_events_checks
(references/mart__job_events_checks.py), job_notes (references/mart__job_notes.py), team_days
(references/mart__team_days.py), team_failures (references/mart__team_failures.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs
from references.mart__job_day_events import job_day_events
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.mart__job_events_checks import job_events_checks
from references.mart__job_notes import job_notes
from references.mart__team_days import team_days
from references.mart__team_failures import team_failures

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["budget_overruns", "job_day_costs", "job_day_events", "job_day_facts", "job_events_checks", "job_notes", "team_days", "team_failures"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

from references import mart
job_day_events = mart.job_day_events  # -> Table

Done when references/mart.py lists mart.job_day_events. Then step 7.

7. Write a Statement that shows its Key

Goal: a write the Toolbox can see is one row per Key. GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt) makes one row for each job and day, so the Toolbox can see the Key holds, and builds the write. It fills each day's Partition from the rows' own dt, which SELECT may name anywhere: the Toolbox puts it last, where Hive reads it from, and show_hive adds the Session setting, a Hive SET sent before it, that such a write needs:

def write_job_day_events(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Statement:
    return statement(
        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events),
        SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, AS(count_rows(), "events"),
               AS(sum_of(job_events.minutes), "minutes")),
        FROM(job_events),
        WHERE(between(job_events.dt, first_day, last_day)),
        GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt),
    )
write = write_job_day_events("2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")  # -> Statement
text = show_hive(write)

Hive, ready to paste
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_events PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS events,
  SUM(job_events.minutes) AS minutes,
  job_events.dt
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_events.job_id,
  job_events.dt;

Done when show_hive(write) prints the INSERT OVERWRITE with its GROUP BY. Then step 8.

8. See a write that can't show its Key refused

Goal: know what the check stops. Leave GROUP_BY out, and the write would save one row for each event: a job with four events that day would have four rows with the same Key. A Guard, a check that refuses a Statement that would give a wrong result, refuses it as it is built:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events),
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, AS(job_events.event_id, "events"),
           AS(job_events.minutes, "minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events) can't show that each row it writes has a Key (job_id, dt) of its own: job_events' rows are told apart by event_id, not by job_id, dt.
  Why it matters: Hive never checks a Key, so rows that share one would be saved without a word, and every join and count that trusts the Key would repeat them.
  Usual fix:      Write one row for each Key: GROUP_BY the Key's columns, use SELECT_DISTINCT, or keep row_number(...) at 1 for each Key in a Derived table, and join only on each joined table's whole Key. When a table it reads declares no Key, though a column or a few tell its rows apart, add one to its Table reference by hand, such as key=["job_id"], once check_key(...) confirms it. The how-to on Keys shows each.
  Opt-out:        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events, unproven_key=True), if each row has a Key of its own for a reason the Toolbox can't see.

ops.job_events' own Key is event_id: without GROUP_BY, the rows written are its rows, one for each event, and nothing makes them one for each job and day.

Done when the GuardRefused names the Key (job_id, dt). Then step 9.

9. Show a Key in another way

Goal: a Key the Toolbox sees without GROUP_BY. The Toolbox looks at how a write makes its rows. Each of these shows a Key:

GROUP_BY over the Key's columns, as above: one row for each group.
SELECT_DISTINCT: one row for each different row. A column whose value another column already fixes doesn't count: one job_events.event_id always comes with the same job_events.job_id, so SELECT_DISTINCT of both makes one row for each event.
row_number(...) kept at 1 for each Key, in a Derived table, a Statement named so another can read from it, as The latest row of each group does: one row for each PARTITION_BY= value.
The rows of the table in FROM, by its own Key, when SELECT returns that Key's columns, and each join matches its joined table's whole Key, so each row from FROM meets at most one row of the joined table. A SEMI_JOIN or ANTI_JOIN keeps the Key whatever its ON= matches, since it never repeats a row; a FULL_JOIN loses it, since either side's columns may be NULL. Semi-, anti- and full joins shows each.


Here the last two together: each job's last event of each day, numbered in a Derived table, then joined to ops.job_owners on its whole Key, the job and the day, which keeps one row for each job and day:

numbered = derived("numbered", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, job_events.event_type,
           AS(row_number(PARTITION_BY=[job_events.job_id, job_events.dt],
                         ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id)), "last_first")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
))
last_events = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(numbered.job_id, numbered.dt, numbered.event_type, job_owners.team),
    FROM(numbered),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, numbered.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, numbered.dt),
                               between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"))),
    WHERE(equals(numbered.last_first, 1)),
)

Done when last_events builds without a Warning. Then step 10.

## last_events

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `numbered.last_first`

Calculated in **numbered** as `row_number(PARTITION_BY=[job_events.job_id, job_events.dt], ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id))`, which is `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id, job_events.dt ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC)`.

```text
numbered.last_first = row_number(PARTITION_BY=[job_events.job_id, job_events.dt], ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id))
├─ ops.job_events.job_id  (bigint)
├─ ops.job_events.dt  (string)
└─ ops.job_events.event_id  (bigint)
```

Rows that count:
- Window of row_number(...), its groups: `PARTITION_BY=job_events.job_id, job_events.dt`, which is `PARTITION BY job_events.job_id, job_events.dt`
- Window of row_number(...), its order: `ORDER_BY=descending(job_events.event_id)`, which is `ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC`
- WHERE in numbered: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `job_id` | numbered.job_id ← ops.job_events.job_id |
| `dt` | numbered.dt ← ops.job_events.dt |
| `event_type` | numbered.event_type ← ops.job_events.event_type |
| `team` | ops.job_owners.team |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- derived('numbered', ...): Key (job_id, dt, last_first), since row_number(...) numbers the rows of each job_id, dt.
- last_events: Key (job_id, dt), since numbered's rows are told apart by job_id, dt, last_first, with last_first held to one value.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    job_events.event_type,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY job_events.job_id, job_events.dt ORDER BY job_events.event_id DESC) AS last_first
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
)
SELECT
  numbered.job_id,
  numbered.dt,
  numbered.event_type,
  job_owners.team
FROM numbered
JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = numbered.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = numbered.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  numbered.last_first = 1
```

## write

It writes the Saved table mart.job_day_events, replacing each partition its rows fill.

### Calculated columns

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

#### `events`

Calculated in **write** as `count_rows()`, which is `COUNT(*)`.

```text
write.events = count_rows()
└─ (no columns: it counts rows)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

#### `minutes`

Calculated in **write** as `sum_of(job_events.minutes)`, which is `SUM(job_events.minutes)`.

```text
write.minutes = sum_of(job_events.minutes)
└─ ops.job_events.minutes  (int)
```

Rows that count:
- WHERE in write: `between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")`, which is `job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'` (reads ops.job_events.dt)

One value for each different `job_events.job_id` and `job_events.dt`.

### Copied columns

| Output | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| `job_id` | ops.job_events.job_id |
| `dt` | ops.job_events.dt |

### Keys

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- write: Key (job_id, dt), since GROUP_BY makes one row for each job_id, dt.
- mart.job_day_events' Key (job_id, dt) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_events PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  job_events.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS events,
  SUM(job_events.minutes) AS minutes,
  job_events.dt
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_events.job_id,
  job_events.dt
```

11. Let through rows you know are one per Key

Goal: a write whose rows are one per Key for a reason the Toolbox can't see. Sometimes you know more than the Toolbox can see: here, that a job has at most one finish event a day, a habit of how its runs are scheduled that no Table reference says. Then unproven_key=True on the write lets it through:

finishes = statement(  # -> Statement
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events, unproven_key=True),
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, AS(job_events.event_id, "events"),
           AS(job_events.minutes, "minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24"),
          equals(job_events.event_type, "finish")),
)

Write it only when you are sure, and check the days with check_key once they are written. INSERT_INTO is checked the same way, on the rows it adds; the rows already there may share a Key with them, which only check_key can find.

Done when finishes builds without a refusal. Then step 12.

12. Create the table with its Key

Goal: the Key in the table's CREATE TABLE. create_table makes the table with its Key:

text = show_hive(create_table(job_day_events))

Hive, ready to paste
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE mart.job_day_events (
  job_id BIGINT,
  events BIGINT,
  minutes BIGINT,
  PRIMARY KEY (job_id, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  dt STRING
)
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_events';

DISABLE NOVALIDATE tells Hive to keep the Key as information only: Hive never checks it, which is why the Toolbox does.

Done when the Hive shows PRIMARY KEY (job_id, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE. Then step 13.

13. Write a table with no Key

Goal: a write into a table that declares no Key. Nothing is checked against a Key a table doesn't have. The Example project's mart.job_notes says key=None, so its notebook's write is built without a Key check, and needs no unproven_key=True. The table has no partition column, so the write replaces the whole table:

notes_write = statement(  # -> Statement
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(mart.job_notes),
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, team_notes.team, team_notes.note, team_notes.written_on),
    FROM(team_notes),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.team, team_notes.team),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, team_notes.written_on),
                               between(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-11", "2026-09-24")),
         many_matches=True),
)

Done when notes_write builds without a refusal. Then step 14.

14. Join onto a table with no Key

Goal: a join you have said may match several rows. A join onto a table with no Key is another matter: the Toolbox can't tell whether one row matches or several, so it gives a Warning, a check that lets the Statement through but says why a number may come out wrong, as it does for a join that doesn't match a Key whole:

notes_per_job = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, team_notes.note),
    FROM(job_owners),
    JOIN(team_notes, ON=equals(team_notes.team, job_owners.team)),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)

It builds, with a Warning
RepeatedRowsWarning:
  What happened:  JOIN(team_notes) matches on team, but team_notes declares no Key, so the Toolbox can't tell whether one row matches or several.
  Why it matters: Each row before the join is repeated once per matching team_notes row, so sums and counts over it may come out too big.
  Usual fix:      Group team_notes first so it has one row per value you join on, and join that. If you know each row matches one team_notes row at most, many_matches=True is right.
  Opt-out:        JOIN(team_notes, ON=..., many_matches=True)

In a notebook, the Warning shows under the cell, at your own line, and doesn't stop anything: it says that team_notes declares no Key, so the Toolbox can't tell whether one row matches or several. Here several rows are what you want, a row for each note of each job's team, so say so with many_matches=True, as notes_write does. A count or a sum over such a join counts each job once per note: to count each job once, group the notes first.

Done when the join says many_matches=True, or groups the notes first. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

The Toolbox's check sees how the rows are made; check_key counts the rows themselves. Run it on a Saved table after its first writes, and on any table you join to by its Key. The Example database holds no mart.job_day_events, but the write's rows come from its SELECT, which it can run: one row for each job and day.

per_job_day = statement(
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, AS(count_rows(), "events")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt),
)
rows = run_query(per_job_day, query)  # -> DataFrame
bool(rows.duplicated(["job_id", "dt"]).any())

Python shows
False

On a table that holds rows already, check_key counts them, on the rows where= picks: a partitioned table is read only where you say, as any read is.

check_key(job_owners, query=query,
          where=equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"))  # -> Verdict

Python shows
ops.job_owners: the key (job_id, dt) holds where job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24'.

Before you go on, check that:

the write's rows show no repeated job_id and dt: the check above gives False;
the Lineage's Markdown file says mart.job_day_events' Key (job_id, dt) holds;
check_key says the key holds on each table you join to by its Key.


Common mistakes

Grouping by more than the Key

A GROUP_BY with one more column makes one row for each event type, not for each job and day:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events),
    SELECT(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, AS(count_rows(), "events"),
           AS(sum_of(job_events.minutes), "minutes")),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-22", "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt, job_events.event_type),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events) can't show that each row it writes has a Key (job_id, dt) of its own: GROUP_BY groups by job_events.event_type, which SELECT leaves out, so nothing makes its rows one for each job_id, dt.
  Why it matters: Hive never checks a Key, so rows that share one would be saved without a word, and every join and count that trusts the Key would repeat them.
  Usual fix:      Write one row for each Key: GROUP_BY the Key's columns, use SELECT_DISTINCT, or keep row_number(...) at 1 for each Key in a Derived table, and join only on each joined table's whole Key. When a table it reads declares no Key, though a column or a few tell its rows apart, add one to its Table reference by hand, such as key=["job_id"], once check_key(...) confirms it. The how-to on Keys shows each.
  Opt-out:        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events, unproven_key=True), if each row has a Key of its own for a reason the Toolbox can't see.

Decide which is meant: if a row is one per event type, add event_type to the Key; if a row is one per job and day, group by the job and the day alone.

A join that can repeat rows

A join onto a table with no Key may add several rows to one, and many_matches=True silences only the join's Warning. The write's Key check stays: mart.job_day_events' Key still says one row for each job and day, and these rows can repeat one:

statement(
    INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events),
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.dt, AS(job_owners.owner, "events"),
           AS(job_owners.owner, "minutes")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    JOIN(team_notes, ON=equals(team_notes.team, job_owners.team), many_matches=True),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events) can't show that each row it writes has a Key (job_id, dt) of its own: JOIN(team_notes, ON=...) can match several team_notes rows to one row, and team_notes declares no Key, so nothing makes its rows one for each job_id, dt.
  Why it matters: Hive never checks a Key, so rows that share one would be saved without a word, and every join and count that trusts the Key would repeat them.
  Usual fix:      Write one row for each Key: GROUP_BY the Key's columns, use SELECT_DISTINCT, or keep row_number(...) at 1 for each Key in a Derived table, and join only on each joined table's whole Key. When a table it reads declares no Key, though a column or a few tell its rows apart, add one to its Table reference by hand, such as key=["job_id"], once check_key(...) confirms it. The how-to on Keys shows each.
  Opt-out:        INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_events, unproven_key=True), if each row has a Key of its own for a reason the Toolbox can't see.

Group the joined rows by the Key, one row for each job and day, as Count, join and stay safe shows.

Checking every Partition by leaving where= out

check_key reads the rows it checks, so on a partitioned table it needs where=, as any read does:

check_key(job_events, query=query)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  check_key(job_events): ops.job_events is partitioned, and nothing says which Partitions to check.
  Why it matters: Checking every Partition reads the whole table, which may be more than the cluster should read at once.
  Usual fix:      Pass where=..., such as where=equals(job_events.dt, <value>), or reads_all_partitions=True to check them all.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Pass where=equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24"), or reads_all_partitions=True for a table small enough to read whole. A table with no Key, such as ops.team_notes, has nothing to check, and check_key says so.

Silencing the check with unproven_key=True

unproven_key=True doesn't make the rows one per Key: it only stops the Toolbox saying they might not be. Make the rows one per Key with GROUP_BY, SELECT_DISTINCT or row_number first, and keep unproven_key=True for rows you know are one per Key.

The latest row of each group: row_number, kept at 1 for each Key.

22. Semi-, anti- and full joins Intermediate

Goal

Keep the rows that have a match in another table, each once, with SEMI_JOIN; keep the rows that have none with ANTI_JOIN; and compare two records of the same things, row for row, with FULL_JOIN. On the way, ask Hive for a map join, a join that copies a small table to every worker, with broadcast=True, and see why RIGHT_JOIN is refused.

When you'd use it

If you want the rows that have a match in another table, such as the jobs that had an event on a day or the runs that raised an alert, use SEMI_JOIN. A plain JOIN gives a row for each match, so a job with two events comes out twice; SEMI_JOIN keeps it once, and adds none of the other table's columns.
If you want the rows with no match, such as the jobs with no event on a day or the rows a copy is missing, use ANTI_JOIN.
If you have two records that should agree, such as the runs ops.job_runs holds and the runs the scheduler says it started, compare them with FULL_JOIN. Each run comes out once, whichever side has it.
If you haven't yet, read Build and run a first statement and Count, join and stay safe first, which shows JOIN, LEFT_JOIN and the repeated-rows Warning.

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_events.event_id.

Its tables: job_events (references/ops__job_events.py), job_owners
(references/ops__job_owners.py), job_runs (references/ops__job_runs.py), region_budgets
(references/ops__region_budgets.py), region_costs (references/ops__region_costs.py),
scheduler_runs (references/ops__scheduler_runs.py), team_notes (references/ops__team_notes.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_events import job_events
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners
from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.ops__region_budgets import region_budgets
from references.ops__region_costs import region_costs
from references.ops__scheduler_runs import scheduler_runs
from references.ops__team_notes import team_notes

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_events", "job_owners", "job_runs", "region_budgets", "region_costs", "scheduler_runs", "team_notes"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

4. Import the tables

from references import ops

5. See JOIN repeat the jobs

Goal: see why a plain join is the wrong tool here. Which of the jobs ops.job_owners lists on 2026-09-24 had an event that day? Join the events, and a job comes out once for each event:

repeated = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.team),
    FROM(job_owners),
    JOIN(job_events, ON=all_of(equals(job_events.job_id, job_owners.job_id),
                               equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")),
         many_matches=True),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
run_query(repeated, query)  # -> DataFrame

Done when job 1 comes out twice. Then step 6.

6. Keep each job with an event once, with SEMI_JOIN

Goal: each job with an event, once. SEMI_JOIN keeps each row that has a match, once, however many rows match it, so it needs no many_matches= and gives no repeated-rows Warning. It is written LEFT SEMI JOIN. The events' day goes inside its ON=, since only ON= may read its table's columns:

with_an_event = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.team),
    FROM(job_owners),
    SEMI_JOIN(job_events, ON=all_of(equals(job_events.job_id, job_owners.job_id),
                                    equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24"))),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(with_an_event)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_owners.job_id,
  job_owners.team
FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
LEFT SEMI JOIN ops.job_events AS job_events
  ON job_events.job_id = job_owners.job_id AND job_events.dt = '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24';

run_query(with_an_event, query)

Result, computed in pandas, not by running this Hive

job_idteam

1data

2finance




Pasted into your own notebook, this run_query, and the one of with_an_event under Check it worked, stops with a RuntimeError saying the Example database can't run a LEFT SEMI JOIN: sqlglot's own small executor doesn't know it. The result above is worked out in pandas instead. Hive at work runs it.

Since a semi join never repeats rows, the rows keep their Key, ops.job_owners' job and day: a write of these rows into a Saved table with that Key is let through, as Keys explains.

Done when jobs 1 and 2 each come out once. Then step 7.

7. Keep each job with no event, with ANTI_JOIN

Goal: each job with no event that day. ANTI_JOIN keeps the rows that have no match. Hive 3 has no anti join of its own, so the Toolbox writes a LEFT JOIN, then a WHERE that keeps the rows where the column ON= matched is NULL: a job with an event has its job_id there.

no_event = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.team),
    FROM(job_owners),
    ANTI_JOIN(job_events, ON=all_of(equals(job_events.job_id, job_owners.job_id),
                                    equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24"))),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)
text = show_hive(no_event)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  job_owners.job_id,
  job_owners.team
FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
LEFT JOIN ops.job_events AS job_events
  ON job_events.job_id = job_owners.job_id AND job_events.dt = '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24' AND job_events.job_id IS NULL;

run_query(no_event, query)

Python shows

4web




The events' day is inside ON= here too: an event on another day doesn't count as a match.

Done when jobs 3 and 4 come out. Then step 8.

8. Read each record's days in a Derived table

Goal: each side of the full join, its Partitions already filtered. FULL_JOIN pairs the rows that match and keeps the rows of each side that have none, with NULL in the other side's columns. Each side keeps all of its rows, so its partitions can't be filtered in WHERE, which would throw away the other side's unmatched rows, nor in ON=, which keeps every row anyway: each side reads its own days in a Derived table first, a Statement named so another Statement can read from it.

recorded = derived("recorded", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(job_runs.run_id),
    FROM(job_runs),
    WHERE(between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
))
started = derived("started", statement(  # -> Table
    SELECT(scheduler_runs.run_id),
    FROM(scheduler_runs),
    WHERE(between(scheduler_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")),
))

Done when recorded and started build without a refusal. Then step 9.

9. Compare the two records with FULL_JOIN

Goal: every run once, with where it was found. fill_null takes the run_id from whichever side has one, and if_else says where each run was found:

both_records = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(fill_null(recorded.run_id, started.run_id), "run_id"),
           AS(if_else(is_null(started.run_id), "only in job_runs",
                      if_else(is_null(recorded.run_id), "only in scheduler_runs",
                              "in both")), "found")),
    FROM(recorded),
    FULL_JOIN(started, ON=equals(started.run_id, recorded.run_id)),
)
text = show_hive(both_records)

Hive, ready to paste
WITH recorded AS (
  SELECT
    job_runs.run_id
  FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  WHERE
    job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
), started AS (
  SELECT
    scheduler_runs.run_id
  FROM ops.scheduler_runs AS scheduler_runs
  WHERE
    scheduler_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
)
SELECT
  COALESCE(recorded.run_id, started.run_id) AS run_id,
  CASE
    WHEN started.run_id IS NULL
    THEN 'only in job_runs'
    ELSE CASE WHEN recorded.run_id IS NULL THEN 'only in scheduler_runs' ELSE 'in both' END
  END AS found
FROM recorded
FULL JOIN started
  ON started.run_id = recorded.run_id;

run_query(both_records, query)
