Done when runs 99 and 100 are each found on one side only. Then step 10.

10. Keep only the differences

Goal: the runs only one side holds. A WHERE on either side's columns is refused after a FULL_JOIN, since it would throw away rows the join kept, unless it is is_null(...), which keeps a side's rows with no match. So the runs only one side holds:

differences = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(AS(recorded.run_id, "in_job_runs"), AS(started.run_id, "in_scheduler_runs")),
    FROM(recorded),
    FULL_JOIN(started, ON=equals(started.run_id, recorded.run_id)),
    WHERE(any_of(is_null(recorded.run_id), is_null(started.run_id))),
)
run_query(differences, query)

Python shows

in_job_runsin_scheduler_runs

NULL100.0

99.0NULL




pandas shows a whole-number column with a missing value as decimals, with NaN for NULL.

A FULL_JOIN's rows have no Key the Toolbox can see, since either side's run_id may be NULL: to write them into a Saved table, a real table your own Statements write, group them by the run_id that fill_null makes, with GROUP_BY(fill_null(recorded.run_id, started.run_id)), which gives them that Key.

Done when the result holds runs 100 and 99 alone. Then step 11.

11. Send the "map_joins" Session setting

Goal: Hive ready to read a map join's hint. broadcast=True, on JOIN, LEFT_JOIN, SEMI_JOIN or ANTI_JOIN, asks for a map join: the small table, here one day of ops.job_owners, is copied whole to every worker, so each worker matches the big table's rows where they are, without first sorting them and sending them across the cluster. The Toolbox writes it as a hint after SELECT: a comment, /*+ ... */, that Hive reads as a request. Hive ignores the hint unless the Session setting "map_joins", a set of Hive SET lines that hold for the rest of the session, is sent first, since one of its lines sets hive.ignore.mapjoin.hint to false, so send it before the Statement:

run_execute(hive_settings("map_joins"), execute)

Python prints
execute: SET hive.auto.convert.join=true
execute: SET hive.auto.convert.join.noconditionaltask=true
execute: SET hive.ignore.mapjoin.hint=false

Done when execute shows the three SET lines. Then step 12.

12. Ask for a map join with broadcast=True

Goal: a join that copies the small table to every worker. Give the small table's join broadcast=True:

per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "events")),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
         broadcast=True),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)
text = show_hive(per_team)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT /*+ MAPJOIN(job_owners) */
  job_owners.team,
  COUNT(*) AS events
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = job_events.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = job_events.dt
  AND job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  job_events.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_owners.team;

run_query(per_team, query)

Python shows

teamevents

data2

finance1




The rows are the same with it or without it: it only changes how the work is done. Usually you don't need the hint, though: with "map_joins" sent, Hive makes map joins by itself, from the tables' sizes and Statistics, what the warehouse has counted about them. Ask for it only for a table you know is small: copied to every worker, a big table can run them out of memory.

Done when the Hive starts SELECT /*+ MAPJOIN(job_owners) */. Then check it worked, below.

    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)

Common mistakes

Reading a SEMI_JOIN's table outside its ON=

SEMI_JOIN adds none of the joined table's columns, so asking for one is refused. To show each event's type, JOIN the events, a row for each; to test it, put the test in ON=.

event_types = statement(
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_events.event_type),
    FROM(job_owners),
    SEMI_JOIN(job_events, ON=all_of(equals(job_events.job_id, job_owners.job_id),
                                    equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24"))),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  SELECT reads job_events.event_type, a column of job_events, which SEMI_JOIN only tests.
  Why it matters: SEMI_JOIN(job_events, ...) keeps each row that has a match in job_events, once, and adds none of its columns: Hive lets only its ON= read them.
  Usual fix:      To test the rows of job_events, put the test in SEMI_JOIN(job_events, ON=...). To show its columns, use JOIN(job_events, ON=...), which gives a row for each match.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Filtering an ANTI_JOIN's partitions in WHERE

Every column of an ANTI_JOIN's table is NULL in the rows it keeps, so WHERE can't test one, its partition columns included. They go inside ON=, as in the steps:

careless = statement(
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id),
    FROM(job_owners),
    ANTI_JOIN(job_events, ON=equals(job_events.job_id, job_owners.job_id)),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"), equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")),
)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  WHERE reads job_events.dt, a column of job_events, which ANTI_JOIN only tests.
  Why it matters: ANTI_JOIN(job_events, ...) keeps only the rows with no match in job_events, so every job_events column is NULL in them: a test of one outside ON= would only ever see NULL, and showing one would show only NULL.
  Usual fix:      To test the rows of job_events, put the test in ANTI_JOIN(job_events, ON=...). To show its columns, use LEFT_JOIN(job_events, ON=...), which keeps the rows with a match too.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

A WHERE on a FULL_JOIN's table

Testing a side's column in WHERE throws away the rows the other side has and it lacks, so it is refused. Pick each side's rows in its own Derived table instead.

only_late_runs = statement(
    SELECT(recorded.run_id, AS(started.run_id, "started")),
    FROM(recorded),
    FULL_JOIN(started, ON=equals(started.run_id, recorded.run_id)),
    WHERE(more_than(recorded.run_id, 100)),
)

Python stops with this message
GuardRefused:
  What happened:  WHERE has recorded.run_id > 100, a condition on recorded, and FULL_JOIN(started) keeps the rows of both sides that have no match.
  Why it matters: A row with no match in recorded has NULL in every recorded column, so this WHERE throws it away again, and some rows FULL_JOIN kept are lost without a word.
  Usual fix:      Pick each side's rows, and filter its partition columns, in a derived(...) table of its own, then FULL_JOIN the two. In WHERE, is_null(...) of a column keeps the rows with no match on that side.
  Opt-out:        FULL_JOIN(started, ON=..., keeps_only_matches=True)

Writing a RIGHT_JOIN

A RIGHT JOIN keeps every row of the table it joins. The Toolbox writes none: swap the two tables, putting the one whose every row you want in FROM, and LEFT_JOIN the other.

RIGHT_JOIN(job_owners, ON=equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id))

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  RIGHT_JOIN(job_owners, ...): the Toolbox doesn't write a RIGHT JOIN.
  Why it matters: A RIGHT JOIN keeps every row of job_owners and only the matching rows of the tables before it. LEFT_JOIN gives the same rows with the tables swapped, and reads in order: the table whose every row is kept comes first, in FROM.
  Usual fix:      Put job_owners in FROM(job_owners), and the table now in FROM in LEFT_JOIN(..., ON=...), with the same ON=.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Next

Count, join and stay safe: JOIN, LEFT_JOIN and the repeated-rows Warning.
Keys: which joins keep a table's Key.
Reconcile two tables: EXCEPT, the other way to compare two records, row by row.
The gallery's Worked examples of SEMI_JOIN, ANTI_JOIN, FULL_JOIN and RIGHT_JOIN.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, JOIN, SEMI_JOIN, ANTI_JOIN, FULL_JOIN, RIGHT_JOIN, WHERE, GROUP_BY, statement, derived, equals, is_null, more_than, between, any_of, all_of, count_rows, if_else, fill_null, show_hive, run_query, run_execute, hive_settings, project.add_references




23. Move a Saved table to its own folder Intermediate

Goal

Move a Saved table that shares its folder with other tables into a folder of its own, with the admin module: copy it whole into a new table at its own folder, check the copy, then drop the old table and give the new one its name. A Saved table is a real table in your warehouse that your own Statements, the queries you build with the Toolbox, write. Along the way, meet the admin module: the changes to your own Saved tables that the Toolbox writes and checks for you.

When you'd use it

If check_table_reference says a Saved table isn't at its own folder, move it, as the steps below do.
If a table was made by hand in a folder it shares with other tables, move it before writing it again: two tables in one folder read each other's files, and an INSERT OVERWRITE of a Partition in one can delete the other's files for that Partition.
If create_table made a table before 5.0, a managed table, whose files the warehouse keeps where it chooses, move it to the folder of its own its location= names.
If you have not saved a table or checked a Table reference yet, do Save a table and Keep the Project in order first; Choose how a Saved table is stored explains the folder of its own.


Steps

1. Start from the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project, your folder of scripts at work, holding a Saved table. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, into the folder it runs in, which Python's path holds already, as Jupyter's does for a notebook's own folder:

import shutil
from pathlib import Path
import sqlglot_composer
download = Path(sqlglot_composer.__file__).parents[1]  # -> Path
_ = shutil.copytree(download / "example_projects" / "intermediate", ".", dirs_exist_ok=True)

warehouse.py

Done when the folder you work in holds the Project's admin/, references/ and tables/ folders. Then step 2.

2. Import the admin module

Goal: the admin module, beside the everyday names. The admin module holds the changes to your own Saved tables: dropping one or some of its Partitions, adding columns, setting its properties, and moving it. The star import leaves it out, so import it by name, and it is plain where a notebook changes a table:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import admin, project
from warehouse import query, execute
from references import mart
job_day_facts = mart.job_day_facts  # -> Table

Done when job_day_facts is set. Then step 3.

3. Find the table that shares a folder

Goal: know which table to move. mart.job_day_facts, each job's events, runs and minutes per day beside its team, is one of the Project's Saved tables. Its Table reference, the file that describes the table, references/mart__job_day_facts.py, gives it its own folder, /data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts.

The Example database holds no Saved table, so a stand-in query plays the warehouse at work: it answers DESCRIBE FORMATTED for a mart.job_day_facts that was made by hand in /data/your_team/tables, a folder other tables share:

import pandas as pd
def query_at_work(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    return pd.DataFrame([
        ("job_id", "bigint", ""), ("team", "string", ""), ("events", "bigint", ""),
        ("runs", "bigint", ""), ("minutes", "bigint", ""),
        ("# Partition Information", None, None), ("dt", "string", ""),
        ("# Detailed Table Information", None, None),
        ("Location:", "hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/tables", None),
        ("Table Type:", "EXTERNAL_TABLE", None),
    ], columns=["col_name", "data_type", "comment"])
check_table_reference(job_day_facts, query=query_at_work)  # -> Verdict

Python shows
mart.job_day_facts differs from its Table reference.
Problems:
  - it is at hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/tables, not at /data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts, the folder location= names: change the line to location="hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/tables", if that is the own folder of the table. A folder that isn't a table's own may hold other tables' files: two tables in one folder read each other's files, and an INSERT OVERWRITE of a Partition in one can delete the other's files for that Partition. Move it with admin.move_to_own_folder(t, query=...).

Here /data/your_team/tables isn't mart.job_day_facts' own folder, so the line to change isn't the Table reference's: the table is the one to move.

Done when the check names the folder the table shares. Then step 4.

4. Check its rows first

Goal: rows worth copying. The move copies the table as it reads it. Where two tables shared a folder, each read the other's files too, so a Partition may hold rows that aren't its own. Before you move it, check its Key, the columns that name one row, on its newest Partition, with check_key(job_day_facts, query=query, where=equals(job_day_facts.dt, "2026-09-24")), and look at a Partition you know. If the rows are mixed, rebuild the table in place of moving it: drop it with admin.drop_table, create it with create_table, and write it again from its sources, as Rewrite some partitions does.

Done when the Key check finds nothing wrong and a Partition you know holds only its own rows. Then step 5.

5. Build the move

Goal: the first two Statements of the move. The move comes in two calls, so that the old table can't be dropped before its copy is checked. admin.move_to_own_folder sends DESCRIBE FORMATTED through your query to find where the table is, and gives back the first two steps, as a list of Statements, in order. show_hive prints each one, after what it does:

moves = admin.move_to_own_folder(job_day_facts, query=query_at_work)  # -> list[Statement]
text = show_hive(moves)

Hive, ready to paste
-- 1 of 2: moves[0]
-- admin.move_to_own_folder(mart.job_day_facts, query=...) moves mart.job_day_facts to its
-- own folder, /data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts. This first step creates
-- mart.job_day_facts_moving there, and the next copies the table's rows into it. Then
-- admin.check_the_copy compares the two and gives the steps that drop mart.job_day_facts and
-- give the copy its name. Send them in order, one at a time.
-- The old table's files stay where they are, since its folder may hold other tables' files
-- too: ask whoever looks after the folder before deleting any.
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE IF NOT EXISTS mart.job_day_facts_moving (
  job_id BIGINT,
  team STRING,
  events BIGINT,
  runs BIGINT,
  minutes BIGINT,
  PRIMARY KEY (job_id, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  dt STRING
)
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts';

-- 2 of 2: moves[1]
-- Copies all of mart.job_day_facts's rows into mart.job_day_facts_moving.
-- It fills dt from the rows, which needs the Session setting "dynamic_partitions": send
-- run_execute(hive_settings("dynamic_partitions"), execute) before it.
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts_moving PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  job_day_facts.job_id,
  job_day_facts.team,
  job_day_facts.events,
  job_day_facts.runs,
  job_day_facts.minutes,
  job_day_facts.dt
FROM mart.job_day_facts AS job_day_facts;

The new table is made at the folder the Table reference names, under a name of its own while the old table still has the table's name. The copy is one INSERT OVERWRITE, which fills each Partition from the rows' own dt, so sent twice it is written once.

Done when moves holds the CREATE and the INSERT OVERWRITE. Then step 6.

6. Check the copy, then get the steps that finish

Goal: the steps that drop the old table, given only once the copy holds every row. Once both steps have run, admin.check_the_copy counts each Partition's rows in both tables, through your query, and stops the move with a message, before anything is dropped, if any Partition differs or is missing from the copy. When they match, it gives back the steps that finish the move. The comparison's Hive starts WITH old_counts, as its entry in the Admin log shows. The stand-in query answers it with no row, as the warehouse does when every Partition matches:

def query_after_the_copy(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    if hive.startswith("WITH old_counts"):  # the count of each Partition's rows
        return pd.DataFrame(columns=["dt", "old_rows", "copied_rows"])
    return query_at_work(hive)
finish = admin.check_the_copy(job_day_facts,
                              query=query_after_the_copy)  # -> list[Statement]

It writes admin_log.md

# Admin log

Each Admin statement, Unchecked statement, Statement holding an Unchecked expression or Unchecked derived table, and Project action the Toolbox has run, oldest first. The Toolbox only ever adds to this file. Its Unchecked statements, expressions and derived tables are the list of what the Toolbox should learn to write next.

## 2026-09-25 09:00:00, Admin statement

- Call: `admin.check_the_copy(mart.job_day_facts, query=...)`
- Tables named: mart.job_day_facts, mart.job_day_facts_moving
- Script: a notebook, at commit 1a2b3c4
- Result: worked

```sql
WITH old_counts AS (
  SELECT
    COALESCE(CAST(job_day_facts.dt AS STRING), '__HIVE_DEFAULT_PARTITION__') AS dt,
    COUNT(*) AS row_count
  FROM mart.job_day_facts AS job_day_facts
  GROUP BY
    COALESCE(CAST(job_day_facts.dt AS STRING), '__HIVE_DEFAULT_PARTITION__')
), copied_counts AS (
  SELECT
    COALESCE(CAST(job_day_facts_moving.dt AS STRING), '__HIVE_DEFAULT_PARTITION__') AS dt,
    COUNT(*) AS row_count
  FROM mart.job_day_facts_moving AS job_day_facts_moving
  GROUP BY
    COALESCE(CAST(job_day_facts_moving.dt AS STRING), '__HIVE_DEFAULT_PARTITION__')
)
SELECT
  old_counts.dt,
  old_counts.row_count AS old_rows,
  copied_counts.row_count AS copied_rows
FROM old_counts
LEFT JOIN copied_counts
  ON copied_counts.dt = old_counts.dt
WHERE
  copied_counts.row_count IS NULL
  OR copied_counts.row_count <> old_counts.row_count
```

text = show_hive(finish)

Hive, ready to paste
-- 1 of 3: finish[0]
-- Sets external.table.purge to false on mart.job_day_facts, so that dropping it next leaves
-- the files in its folder, which other tables may read.
ALTER TABLE mart.job_day_facts SET TBLPROPERTIES (
  'external.table.purge'='false'
);

-- 2 of 3: finish[1]
-- Drops the old mart.job_day_facts: its files stay in the folder it shared. Can't be undone:
-- admin.check_the_copy gives it only once the copy holds every row.
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS mart.job_day_facts;

-- 3 of 3: finish[2]
-- Gives mart.job_day_facts_moving the name mart.job_day_facts. Its files stay at its own
-- folder, /data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts. If this step fails, send it alone again.
ALTER TABLE mart.job_day_facts_moving RENAME TO mart.job_day_facts;

The first keeps the old files when the old table is dropped, since the folder it shared holds other tables' files too. Only then is the old table dropped, and the new one renamed: a renamed table keeps its folder.

Done when finish holds the three steps: keep the files, drop, rename. Then step 7.

7. Send the steps at work

Goal: the move made in your warehouse. At work, send the Session setting the copy needs, then each step in turn with run_execute, then the check, then the steps it gives. A step that fails raises, so the function stops there, and the steps after it aren't sent; the check raises when a Partition's rows differ, before anything is dropped. Here it is a function that takes your own query and execute, so that the move can be sent again; it isn't called on this page, since the Example database is never written to:

from collections.abc import Callable
def move(t: Table, query: Callable[[str], pd.DataFrame],
         execute: Callable[[str], None]) -> None:
    run_execute(hive_settings("dynamic_partitions"), execute)
    for step in admin.move_to_own_folder(t, query=query):
        run_execute(step, execute)
    for step in admin.check_the_copy(t, query=query):
        run_execute(step, execute)

Each step run_execute sends, and each check, adds an entry to the Admin log, the Project's admin/admin_log.md: when, the call, such as admin.move_to_own_folder(mart.job_day_facts, query=...)[1] for the copy, the Hive, the script and its git commit, and whether it worked. show_hive adds nothing to it.

If a step before the drop fails, fix what it says and send the move again from the first: creating the new table again does nothing, and the copy is written again whole.
If the check says a Partition is missing from the copy, one was written since the copy, so send the move again.
If only the last step, the rename, fails, send it alone again with run_execute(finish[-1], execute), from the finish the check gave: once the old table is dropped, the check can't be made again, since the table it reads is gone.


Done when the Admin log holds an entry that worked for each step. Then step 8.

8. Move the other tables

Goal: every Saved table that shared the folder at its own. Move each of them the same way, one at a time, each to its own folder. Once none of them reads the old folder, its files are left over: ask whoever looks after the folder before deleting any, since other teams' tables may read it too.

Done when check_table_reference says each of those tables matches its Table reference. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

Once the steps have run, the table is at its own folder. A stand-in query plays the warehouse again, now describing the moved table:

def query_after_the_move(hive: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    return pd.DataFrame([
        ("job_id", "bigint", ""), ("team", "string", ""), ("events", "bigint", ""),
        ("runs", "bigint", ""), ("minutes", "bigint", ""),
        ("# Partition Information", None, None), ("dt", "string", ""),
        ("# Detailed Table Information", None, None),
        ("Location:", "hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts", None),
        ("Table Type:", "EXTERNAL_TABLE", None),
    ], columns=["col_name", "data_type", "comment"])
check_table_reference(job_day_facts, query=query_after_the_move)

Python shows
mart.job_day_facts matches its Table reference.

The check made sure each Partition holds as many rows in the new table as in the old. You can also check a Partition against where its rows come from. The Example database doesn't hold mart.job_day_facts, but its notebook's preview() gives the rows its write() saves, from the tables the Example database does hold. project.load gives a Table notebook's logic, its functions, as Save a table shows:

logic = project.load("mart.job_day_facts")  # -> the notebook's functions

no table named mart.job_day_facts_moving is left in the warehouse.


Common mistakes

Moving a table someone else owns

The admin module changes only your own Saved tables, the ones whose Table reference has location=. Any other is refused before anything is sent:

from references import ops
admin.move_to_own_folder(ops.job_events, query=query_at_work)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  admin.move_to_own_folder(...) was given ops.job_events, which isn't one of your Saved tables: its Table reference has no location=.
  Why it matters: It changes a table, or what the warehouse keeps about it, so it takes only your own Saved tables, each in a folder of its own, its location=. A Table reference with no location= is a table someone else owns, and changing it could break their work.
  Usual fix:      If ops.job_events is yours, add location=... to its Table(...) call, its own folder, such as location="/data/your_team/mart.db/daily_runs". If it isn't yours, ask whoever owns it.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Move only tables whose Table reference has location=; ask the owner of any other table.

Moving a table that is at its own folder already

Once a table is at its own folder, there is nothing to move, and a move built again is refused, so a notebook that moves a table can't move it twice:

admin.move_to_own_folder(job_day_facts, query=query_after_the_move)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  admin.move_to_own_folder(mart.job_day_facts, query=...): mart.job_day_facts is at its own folder already, hdfs://namenode:8020/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts.
  Why it matters: There is nothing to move.
  Usual fix:      Leave it where it is; check_table_reference(t, query=...) says whether anything else differs.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Dropping the table without the check

admin.move_to_own_folder gives no step that drops anything: the drop comes only from admin.check_the_copy, once the copy holds every row. A DROP written by hand, or admin.drop_table, sent before the check, can lose Partitions the copy doesn't hold. Send the drop only as admin.check_the_copy gives it.

Next

How a Saved table is stored, and why it needs a folder of its own: Choose how a Saved table is stored.
Check every Table reference against its table: Keep the Project in order.
Write some of a table's Partitions again from their sources: Rewrite some partitions.
The admin module's other changes, each with its own entry in the gallery: admin.drop_table, admin.drop_partitions, admin.add_columns, admin.set_properties, admin.move_to_own_folder and admin.check_the_copy.


Toolbox names used: Table, check_table_reference, show_hive, run_query, run_execute, hive_settings, admin.move_to_own_folder, admin.check_the_copy, project.load




24. Reconcile two tables Intermediate

Goal

Find every row where two tables that should agree don't. EXCEPT gives the rows of one Statement, one query, that another Statement doesn't return, so running it both ways round lists what only the first table has, then what only the second has. When both come back empty, the tables agree.

When you'd use it

If a Saved table, a real table your own Statements write, should hold the rows of the table it was copied from, reconcile the two.
If you changed a table's Statement, reconcile the table before and after the change.
If your team's table and another team's record the same thing, reconcile the two.
If you have not built a Statement yet, do Build and run a first statement first; if you have not read a Derived table yet, do Share Building blocks first.


Steps

1. Start from the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project, your folder of scripts at work, holding the tables to compare. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy here, from the download the Toolbox came in, into the folder it runs in, which Python's path holds already, as Jupyter's does for a notebook's own folder:

import shutil
from pathlib import Path
import sqlglot_composer
download = Path(sqlglot_composer.__file__).parents[1]  # -> Path
_ = shutil.copytree(download / "example_projects" / "intermediate", ".", dirs_exist_ok=True)

Done when the folder you work in holds the Project's admin/, references/ and tables/ folders. Then step 2.

2. Add the second table's Table reference

Goal: both tables readable from Python. A Table reference is the file that describes one table: its columns, its Partitions and its Key. ops.job_owners, which the Project reads already, keeps each job's team on each day. ops.jobs holds each job's team as it is today: add its Table reference, as Add your tables' references shows. Its TODO lines can wait, since EXCEPT compares whole rows and reads no Key:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from sqlglot_composer import project
from warehouse import query, execute
project.add_references(["ops.jobs"], query=query)

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_events.event_id.

Its tables: job_events (references/ops__job_events.py), job_owners
(references/ops__job_owners.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), region_budgets
(references/ops__region_budgets.py), region_costs (references/ops__region_costs.py), team_notes
(references/ops__team_notes.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_events import job_events
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__region_budgets import region_budgets
from references.ops__region_costs import region_costs
from references.ops__team_notes import team_notes

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_events", "job_owners", "jobs", "region_budgets", "region_costs", "team_notes"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.ops__jobs import jobs, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = jobs.job_name
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or

#     <alias> = equals(jobs.job_name, <value>)

from references import ops
jobs, job_owners = ops.jobs, ops.job_owners  # -> Table, Table

On a day when no job has changed teams since, the two should agree.

Done when jobs and job_owners are set. Then step 3.

3. Say what should agree

Goal: one Statement for each table, returning the columns that should agree, under the same names. The Toolbox matches the two Statements' columns by name, not by their place in SELECT, so "job_id" is compared with "job_id" and "team" with "team":

teams_today = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(jobs.job_id, jobs.team),
    FROM(jobs),
)
def teams_on(day: str) -> Statement:
    return statement(
        SELECT(job_owners.job_id, job_owners.team),
        FROM(job_owners),
        WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, day)),
    )

Done when teams_today and teams_on each return job_id and team. Then step 4.

4. List the rows only the first table has

Goal: what ops.jobs holds that ops.job_owners doesn't. EXCEPT keeps the rows of the first Statement it is given that the second doesn't return. Two rows count as the same when every column is alike:

only_in_jobs = EXCEPT(teams_today, teams_on("2026-09-17"))  # -> Statement
text = show_hive(only_in_jobs)

Hive, ready to paste
SELECT
  jobs.job_id,
  jobs.team
FROM ops.jobs AS jobs
EXCEPT
SELECT
  job_owners.job_id,
  job_owners.team
FROM ops.job_owners AS job_owners
WHERE
  job_owners.dt = '2026-09-17';

run_query(only_in_jobs, query)  # -> DataFrame

Goal: the other half. The same two Statements the other way round give what ops.job_owners held that ops.jobs doesn't:

only_in_owners = EXCEPT(teams_on("2026-09-17"), teams_today)  # -> Statement
run_query(only_in_owners, query)

Together they say the two disagree on one job, job 3, report_build: ops.jobs says finance and ops.job_owners said data on 2026-09-17. report_build moved from data to finance on 2026-09-18, and ops.jobs knows only today's team. Run both directions: one alone shows only half of that.

Done when the result holds job 3, data. Then step 6.

6. Count the rows of each

Goal: the same number of rows on both sides. EXCEPT ignores how many times a row appears: a row held twice in one table and once in the other counts as matching. So count each Statement's rows too, reading it as a Derived table, a Statement given a name so another Statement can read from it:

import pandas as pd
def rows_of(s: Statement) -> pd.DataFrame:
    counted = derived("counted", s)
    return run_query(statement(SELECT(AS(count_rows(), "rows")), FROM(counted)), query)
rows_of(teams_today)

4




rows_of(teams_on("2026-09-17"))

run_query(EXCEPT(teams_today, teams_on("2026-09-24")), query)

run_query(EXCEPT(teams_on("2026-09-24"), teams_today), query)

Python shows

Before you go on, check that:

each direction of EXCEPT on 2026-09-17 gives one row, job 3;
each direction on 2026-09-24 gives an empty DataFrame;
rows_of gives the same count for both Statements.


Common mistakes

A column named differently in each

Say the second Statement names its team "owner_team". The Toolbox matches columns by name, so it refuses the pair as you build it, naming the column, where Hive, which matches them by their place, would compare them without a word:

EXCEPT(teams_today, statement(
    SELECT(job_owners.job_id, AS(job_owners.team, "owner_team")),
    FROM(job_owners),
    WHERE(equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-17")),
))

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  EXCEPT(...)'s 2nd Statement doesn't return team, which the 1st returns, and returns owner_team, which the 1st doesn't.
  Why it matters: The Toolbox stacks the rows column by column, matching each column by its name, so every Statement must return the same names.
  Usual fix:      If owner_team is the same column as team, name it alike with AS, such as AS(..., "team"); else return the same columns from each Statement, leaving out a column only some of them have.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Name the column alike in both with AS, as its fix says. A column of a different kind in each, such as a number in one and text in the other, is refused the same way.

Comparing a column with no value

EXCEPT counts two NULLs as alike, so a job with no owner in both tables agrees. equals doesn't: in SQL nothing equals NULL. Compare tables row by row with EXCEPT, which counts missing values as alike.

Reading every partition of a big table

Each Statement passed to EXCEPT is checked like any other: a read of a partitioned table must filter at least one of its partition columns, as teams_on filters ops.job_owners to one day. Reconcile a few Partitions, slices of the table that share one value of its partition columns, at a time, and loop over them in Python.

Next

Semi-, anti- and full joins: FULL_JOIN, which pairs the two records' rows and says which side each was found in.
Keys: the columns that name one row of a table, which a reconciliation often checks too.
Check data quality: counts and checks on one table.
The gallery's EXCEPT, INTERSECT, UNION and UNION_ALL.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, WHERE, statement, Statement, derived, EXCEPT, equals, count_rows, show_hive, run_query, project.add_references




25. Keep reads and joins fast Intermediate

Goal

Keep Statements, the queries you build with the Toolbox, fast on big tables: read only the Partitions you need, keep a Saved table's files few and large, give Hive the Statistics it plans joins from, let it copy a small table to every worker, and see with explain what Hive will do before you run a Statement. A Partition is a slice of a table, the rows that share one value of each column it is partitioned by; a Saved table is a real table your own Statements write; Statistics are what the warehouse has counted about a table's Partitions. Along the way, let the warehouse find a Saved table's Partitions again with repair_partitions. Each of these is safe to send again.

When you'd use it

If a Statement or a run of your tables gets slow, follow the steps below.
If a Saved table grows to millions of rows a Partition, follow the steps below.
If you want an index: Hive has none to add. Choose how a Saved table is stored says why, under "Why there are no indexes", and shows how to give a big table Partitions and Buckets; this how-to is what to do once the table exists.
If you have not joined tables or saved a table yet, do Count, join and stay safe and Save a table first.


Steps

1. Start from the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project, your folder of scripts at work, holding a Saved table. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, into the folder it runs in, which Python's path holds already, as Jupyter's does for a notebook's own folder:

import shutil
from pathlib import Path
import sqlglot_composer
download = Path(sqlglot_composer.__file__).parents[1]  # -> Path
_ = shutil.copytree(download / "example_projects" / "intermediate", ".", dirs_exist_ok=True)

Done when the folder you work in holds the Project's admin/, references/ and tables/ folders. Then step 2.

2. Name the tables

Goal: the tables the steps read, by short names. mart.job_day_facts, each job's events, runs and minutes per day beside its team, is one of the Project's Saved tables, partitioned by dt:

from sqlglot_composer import *
from warehouse import query, execute
from references import mart, ops
job_day_facts = mart.job_day_facts  # -> Table
job_events, job_owners = ops.job_events, ops.job_owners  # -> Table, Table

Done when job_day_facts, job_events and job_owners are set. Then step 3.

3. Read only the Partitions you need

Goal: each read opens as few folders as the job allows. A table's Partitions are folders, here one per day, and a Statement that filters a partition column opens only the folders it picks. Every Statement on a partitioned table already filters one, as the Load limits, the checks that refuse a read too big for the cluster, insist. So the first rule of a fast read is one you keep anyway: filter as tightly as the job allows, a day rather than a week. On a table partitioned by two columns, such as ops.region_costs by region and then dt, a Statement that filters both opens fewer folders still.

Done when each of your Statements filters its partition columns to the days the job needs. Then step 4.

4. Keep files few and large

Goal: Partitions held in a few large files. Every file costs Hive a little to open, so a Partition held in a thousand small files reads far more slowly than the same rows in a few large ones. Small files come from writes that many workers share. A Session setting is a Hive SET line that holds for the rest of the session. The Session setting "merge_small_files" has each write merge its files before it finishes; "statistics_on_write" has each write count the Partitions it writes, for the next step. Send them before your writes, once per session:

settings = hive_settings("merge_small_files", "statistics_on_write")  # -> SessionSettings
print(to_hive(settings))

Hive
SET hive.merge.mapfiles=true
SET hive.merge.mapredfiles=true
SET hive.merge.tezfiles=true
SET hive.stats.autogather=true
SET hive.stats.column.autogather=true

run_execute(settings, execute)

Python prints
execute: SET hive.merge.mapfiles=true
execute: SET hive.merge.mapredfiles=true
execute: SET hive.merge.tezfiles=true
execute: SET hive.stats.autogather=true
execute: SET hive.stats.column.autogather=true

run_execute sends each SET line through your execute, as a call of its own, and gives back nothing; the Example database's execute prints each one. They hold only while your query and execute keep one session across their calls.

Done when your execute has been given the five SET lines. Then step 5.

5. Give Hive the Statistics it plans joins from

Goal: Partitions Hive has counted. Hive picks how to join two tables from their Statistics: how many rows and bytes each Partition holds. Without them it guesses, and may sort two big tables to join them where copying a small one would do. A Partition written with "statistics_on_write" is counted as it is written; count one written without that setting with compute_statistics, and add columns=True to count each column's values too, which Hive reads to order several joins:

count_day = compute_statistics(job_day_facts,
                               partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"})  # -> Statement
print(to_hive(count_day))

Hive
ANALYZE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-24') COMPUTE STATISTICS

print(to_hive(compute_statistics(job_day_facts, partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"},
                                 columns=True)))

Hive
ANALYZE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt = '2026-09-24') COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS job_id, team, events, runs, minutes

columns=[job_day_facts.job_id, job_day_facts.team] counts only the columns you name, such as those you join or filter on, which reads less. partition= names the Partition to count. Left out, it counts every Partition the table holds, which reads the whole table:

print(to_hive(compute_statistics(job_day_facts)))

Hive
ANALYZE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt) COMPUTE STATISTICS

At work, a function counts the Partitions you wrote before, one by one, through your own execute; sending one again counts it again, which does no harm:

def count_days(days: list[str]) -> None:
    for day in days:
        run_execute(compute_statistics(job_day_facts, partition={"dt": day}), execute)

Done when each Partition you wrote without "statistics_on_write" has had its ANALYZE TABLE sent. Then step 6.

6. Let Hive copy a small table to every worker

Goal: map joins where a small table meets a big one. Joining a big table to a small one, such as the events of a day to the owners of that day, is fastest when Hive copies the small table to every worker, which then joins its share of the big one without sorting it: a Map join, or broadcast join. The Session setting "map_joins" lets Hive choose one by itself, from the tables' Statistics, so it works best once both tables have them. Send it before your Statements, as the settings above:

print(to_hive(hive_settings("map_joins")))

Hive
SET hive.auto.convert.join=true
SET hive.auto.convert.join.noconditionaltask=true
SET hive.ignore.mapjoin.hint=false

run_execute(hive_settings("map_joins"), execute)

Python prints
execute: SET hive.auto.convert.join=true
execute: SET hive.auto.convert.join.noconditionaltask=true
execute: SET hive.ignore.mapjoin.hint=false

Nothing in the Statement changes: Hive decides when it plans it. Step 8 shows how to see what it decided.

Where Hive still sorts both sides, JOIN(job_owners, ON=..., broadcast=True) asks it by name to copy job_owners to every worker; Semi-, anti- and full joins shows how. Hive heeds that request only after "map_joins", whose last line, hive.ignore.mapjoin.hint=false, tells it to.

Done when your execute has been given the three SET lines of "map_joins". Then step 7.

7. Keep Buckets for big joins

Goal: Buckets only where they pay off. Where two big tables are joined on the same column on every run, Buckets spread each Partition's rows into a fixed number of files by that column, so the join pairs file with file. They pay off only on tables of tens of millions of rows a Partition, and changing them means writing the table again: Choose how a Saved table is stored shows how.

Done when you have decided, for each big join, whether its tables reach tens of millions of rows a Partition. Then step 8.

8. See Hive's Plan with explain

Goal: how Hive will run a Statement, before you run it. A Plan is that: which tables Hive reads, with which filters, and how it joins them. explain writes EXPLAIN before exactly the Hive run_query would send, and run_query gives back Hive's Plan for it as text, without running the Statement. Here each team's events on 2026-09-24, each event joined to its job's owner that day:

events_per_team = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_owners.team, AS(count_rows(), "events")),
    FROM(job_events),
    JOIN(job_owners, ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_events.job_id),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, job_events.dt),
                               equals(job_owners.dt, "2026-09-24"))),
    WHERE(equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")),
    GROUP_BY(job_owners.team),
)
plan = explain(events_per_team)  # -> Statement
print(to_hive(plan))

Hive
EXPLAIN SELECT
  job_owners.team,
  COUNT(*) AS events
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = job_events.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = job_events.dt
  AND job_owners.dt = '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  job_events.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_owners.team

At work, print the Plan your query gives back, print(run_query(plan, query)). Hive's Plan is a tree of steps, each reading what the steps below it give. Look for:

"TableScan", once for each table read, with the filter it reads it with;
"Map Join Operator" where Hive copies the small table to every worker, or "Merge Join Operator" where it sorts both sides first;
each step's "Statistics:" line, the rows Hive expects, and "Basic stats: NONE" or "Column stats: NONE" where it has none to go by: count that table's Partitions with compute_statistics, if it is yours, or ask its owner.


The Example database can't give a Plan: it runs Hive on sqlglot's own small executor, which makes none. to_hive(explain(...)) still shows what you would send at work.

Done when the Hive starts with EXPLAIN SELECT. Then step 9.

9. Find the Partitions a Saved table lost

Goal: every Partition of a Saved table on the warehouse's list. The warehouse keeps a list of each table's Partitions, and reads only the folders on it. When a table is made again where its old files stayed, since it was dropped without purge=True, or when files are copied into its folder, their Partitions are missing from the list until repair_partitions finds them:

repair = repair_partitions(job_day_facts)  # -> Statement
print(to_hive(repair))

Hive
MSCK REPAIR TABLE mart.job_day_facts

run_execute(repair, execute)

Python prints
execute: MSCK REPAIR TABLE mart.job_day_facts

It writes admin/sent.csv too.

It only adds Partitions; it deletes no file, so it is safe to send again. Like compute_statistics, it goes to your execute; the Example database's execute only prints its first line, since its tables are made up and fixed.

Done when your execute has been given MSCK REPAIR TABLE mart.job_day_facts. Then check it worked, below.

Check it worked

explain explains exactly the Hive run_query sends, so the Plan is of the Statement you run:

to_hive(plan) == "EXPLAIN " + to_hive(events_per_team)

Python shows
True

The Statement itself still gives its rows:

run_query(events_per_team, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

teamevents

data2

finance1




At work, compare the Plan before and after a change: send compute_statistics for a table, then explain the same Statement again, and the "Statistics:" lines of its TableScan should say "Basic stats: COMPLETE" where they said NONE; send "map_joins" first, and a join to a small table should be a "Map Join Operator". Then time the Statement itself.

Before you go on, check that:

to_hive(plan) is "EXPLAIN " followed by the Statement's own Hive;
run_query(events_per_team, query) gives data 2 and finance 1;
at work, the Plan's TableScan lines say "Basic stats: COMPLETE" once you counted the table.


Common mistakes

Counting a table you only read

compute_statistics and repair_partitions change what the warehouse keeps about a table, so they take only your own Saved tables, Table references with location=. A table another team owns is refused:

compute_statistics(job_events, partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"})

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  compute_statistics(...) was given ops.job_events, which isn't one of your Saved tables: its Table reference has no location=.
  Why it matters: It changes a table, or what the warehouse keeps about it, so it takes only your own Saved tables, each in a folder of its own, its location=. A Table reference with no location= is a table someone else owns, and changing it could break their work.
  Usual fix:      If ops.job_events is yours, add location=... to its Table(...) call, its own folder, such as location="/data/your_team/mart.db/daily_runs". If it isn't yours, ask whoever owns it.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Ask the team that owns it to keep its Statistics.

Naming the Partition without partition=

compute_statistics names the Partitions to count by their partition columns, so the value alone is refused:

compute_statistics(job_day_facts, "2026-09-24")

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  compute_statistics(job_day_facts, partition="2026-09-24"): partition= is a dict of partition columns and their values.
  Why it matters: It names Partitions by their partition columns: each column it names, with one value of it.
  Usual fix:      Write it like compute_statistics(job_day_facts, partition={"dt": ...}), or leave partition= out: then compute_statistics covers every value of each partition column given none.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write partition={"dt": "2026-09-24"}, as the steps do.

Sending Session settings to an execute that forgets them

A Session setting holds for the rest of a session. If your execute opens a new session for each call, each SET is gone before your Statement runs, and the Plan shows no change. A read goes through your query and a SET through your execute, so make both use one connection that keeps its session for every call.

Next

Give a big table a second partition column, or Buckets: Choose how a Saved table is stored.
Ask for a map join on one join, with broadcast=True: Semi-, anti- and full joins.
Keep each Statement's reads small, with Load limits: Count, join and stay safe.
Every option in one place: the gallery's explain, compute_statistics and repair_partitions entries.


Toolbox names used: SELECT, AS, FROM, JOIN, WHERE, GROUP_BY, statement, equals, all_of, count_rows, to_hive, run_query, run_execute, hive_settings, repair_partitions, compute_statistics, explain




26. Declare a change made in Python Intermediate

Goal

Change some rows in pandas, in a function of your own, save them to a table with your own call, and declare the change to the Toolbox with python_step. A Python step is such a change, made outside any Statement, the query the Toolbox builds, and declared with the columns it read and the table it wrote. Declared, it lets the Lineage of your tables, the record of which columns feed each result, show where the change lives, rather than stopping at the gap, and project.run_tables write the tables that read it after it.

When you'd use it

If a step is easier in pandas than in a Statement, make it a Python step. Here it is how many minutes longer each run took than its job's usual run, the median, which pandas works out in one line.
If you need the Toolbox to move the rows, use a Statement instead: for a Python step you pull the rows with a Statement, change them with your function, and save them with your work's own call. python_step only tells the Toolbox which columns the change read, which table it wrote, and which function made it, so whoever reads the Lineage can open that function and see what it does.
If you have not saved a table, made a Building block or exported Lineage yet, do Save a table, Share Building blocks and Follow lineage first.


Steps

Goal: a Project, your folder of scripts at work, to make the change in. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. It writes building_blocks/run_speed.py

"""run_speed - TODO: say in one line what one row of it is.

Fill in every <...> in the code below this text, brackets and all: Python stops at one left
in. They are:
    <SOURCE_NAME> and <SOURCE_TABLE>: the table it reads, by its file in references/ and its
        name there, such as ops__job_runs and job_runs.
    <GROUP_COLUMN>: the column each row is for, such as job_id.
    <NUMBER_COLUMN>: a column of numbers to add up, such as duration_mins.
    <TOTAL_NAME>: the name of what it adds up to, in quotes, such as "minutes".

Why: TODO: which tables' notebooks read it, so they can never work it out two ways.

project.new_block("run_speed") wrote this file from admin/templates/building_block.py. A
Building block is a piece several notebooks share; this one is a Derived table, a Statement
given a name, which each Statement reading it writes at its top, as WITH run_speed AS (...). In a
notebook's logic cell, import it, and read it like a table, given the rows it reads, such
as READS, the condition at the top of a saved_table notebook's logic cell:

    from building_blocks.run_speed import run_speed


    def preview() -> Statement:
        per_group = run_speed(READS)
        return statement(SELECT(per_group.<GROUP_COLUMN>, per_group.row_count),
                         FROM(per_group))
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>  # the Table reference it reads


def run_speed(reads: Condition) -> Table:  # named as its file is
    """One row per group of the rows `reads` picks: its rows, and its total.

    `reads` is a condition on the table it reads, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24").
    On a partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns.
    """
    return derived(
        "run_speed",  # the name the Hive gives it, the same as its file's
        statement(
            SELECT(
                <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
                # to keep each Partition's rows apart, add its partition column here and in
                # GROUP_BY, such as job_runs.dt
                AS(count_rows(), "row_count"),
                # add a line like the next for each number to add up
                AS(sum_of(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<NUMBER_COLUMN>), <TOTAL_NAME>),
            ),
            FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>),
        ),
    )

4. Write the change in it

Goal: your function, importable. Write your function over the Template's code:

The file building_blocks/run_speed.py, after you write your function in it

"""run_speed - how each run's minutes compare with its job's usual run.

Why: mart.run_speed saves these numbers, worked out in pandas, which does a median per
group in one line.
"""

import pandas as pd


def add_minutes_over_median(runs: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """runs, with minutes_over_median: the minutes each run took over its job's median
    run, the job's usual one."""
    median = runs.groupby("job_id")["minutes"].transform("median")
    return runs.assign(minutes_over_median=runs["minutes"] - median)

from building_blocks.run_speed import add_minutes_over_median

ended = statement(  # -> Statement
    SELECT(job_events.event_id, job_events.job_id, job_events.minutes, job_events.dt),
    FROM(job_events),
    WHERE(between(job_events.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24"), ends_a_run),
)
runs = run_query(ended, query)  # -> DataFrame

"""mart.run_speed - one row per run that ended: its minutes over its job's usual run."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

run_speed = Table(
    "mart.run_speed",
    columns={
        "event_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "minutes": "int",
        "minutes_over_median": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["event_id"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/run_speed",
)

Done when references/mart__run_speed.py names the table's five columns. Then step 8.

8. Fill in its Table notebook

Goal: the notebook that pulls, changes and saves the rows. A Table notebook is the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create and write its Saved table. Its logic cell says what the change reads and writes: rows() is the Statement that pulls the rows, and write() gives the change, declared with python_step. The cell after it pulls the rows, changes them and saves them, with your API's own write call, since the Toolbox has no call that saves a DataFrame:

The file tables/mart__run_speed/mart__run_speed.ipynb, after you fill it in

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.run_speed
#
# Each run that ended on the days READS picks, with the minutes it took over its job's
# usual run, the median. The change is made in pandas, by add_minutes_over_median in
# `building_blocks/run_speed.py`, and the rows are saved by your own call, in cell 3.
#
# The code cells: 1 the Project and your query; 2 the logic, which starts `# logic`; 3
# pull, change and save the rows; 4 its lineage.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_lineage, run_query
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    FROM, SELECT, WHERE, PythonStep, Statement, create_table, equals, python_step, statement,
)
from building_blocks.run_speed import add_minutes_over_median
from references.mart__run_speed import run_speed  # this table's own Table reference
from references.ops__job_events import ends_a_run, job_events

# The rows write() reads.
READS = equals(job_events.dt, "2026-09-24")


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.run_speed from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(run_speed, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> Statement:
    """The rows the change reads: each event READS picks that ends a run."""
    return statement(
        SELECT(job_events.event_id, job_events.job_id, job_events.minutes, job_events.dt),
        FROM(job_events),
        WHERE(READS, ends_a_run),
    )


def write() -> PythonStep:
    """Step 2: the change, declared: what it reads, and the table it writes. Cell 3 makes
    it."""
    return python_step(
        add_minutes_over_median,
        reads=[job_events.event_id, job_events.job_id, job_events.minutes, job_events.dt],
        writes=run_speed,
    )

# %%
# 3. Pull the rows, change them in pandas, and save them with your own call
import my_api  # your team's API, as warehouse.py uses it

changed = add_minutes_over_median(run_query(rows(), query))
my_api.write_table(changed, "mart.run_speed", partition_by=["dt"])

# %%
# 4. Lineage: a new pair in lineage/, named by the time and this table, beside the ones before
export_lineage(rows(), write())

Done when the notebook's logic cell has rows() and write(). Then step 9.

9. Declare the step

Goal: the change, declared as a PythonStep. python_step takes your function itself, not what it gave back, the columns it read, each through its Table reference (a Table reference stands for every one of its columns), and the table it wrote. It gives back a PythonStep, and sends nothing. project.load runs the notebook's logic cell, as project.run_tables does, so its write() is here too:

speed = project.load("mart.run_speed")  # -> the notebook's functions
speed_step = speed.write()  # -> PythonStep
speed_step

Python shows
<Python step: building_blocks/run_speed.py:add_minutes_over_median, line 10; writes mart.run_speed>

It read where the function lives from the function itself: its file, from the Project's folder, its name, and the line its def is on. show_hive prints what it is, as comment lines, since it has no Hive:

text = show_hive(speed_step)

Hive, ready to paste
-- Python step: changed in Python by building_blocks/run_speed.py:add_minutes_over_median,
-- line 10. It reads ops.job_events.event_id, ops.job_events.job_id, ops.job_events.minutes
-- and ops.job_events.dt, and writes mart.run_speed. Nothing is sent for it: your own call
-- saves its rows.

Done when show_hive names add_minutes_over_median and line 10. Then step 10.

It writes references/mart.py

"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.budget_overruns.job_id.

Its tables: budget_overruns (references/mart__budget_overruns.py), job_day_costs
(references/mart__job_day_costs.py), job_day_facts (references/mart__job_day_facts.py),
job_events_checks (references/mart__job_events_checks.py), job_notes
(references/mart__job_notes.py), run_speed (references/mart__run_speed.py), team_days
(references/mart__team_days.py), team_failures (references/mart__team_failures.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.mart__job_events_checks import job_events_checks
from references.mart__job_notes import job_notes
from references.mart__run_speed import run_speed
from references.mart__team_days import team_days
from references.mart__team_failures import team_failures

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["budget_overruns", "job_day_costs", "job_day_facts", "job_events_checks", "job_notes", "run_speed", "team_days", "team_failures"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

The file references/mart__slow_runs.py, after you fill it in

"""mart.slow_runs - one row per run that took over 5 minutes more than its job's usual run."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

slow_runs = Table(
    "mart.slow_runs",
    columns={"event_id": "bigint", "job_id": "bigint", "minutes_over_median": "double",

The file tables/mart__slow_runs/mart__slow_runs.ipynb, after you fill it in

# %% [markdown]
# # mart.slow_runs
#
# Each run that took more than 5 minutes over its job's usual run, from mart.run_speed.
#
# The code cells: 1 the Project and your query; 2 the logic, which starts `# logic`; 3
# try it; 4 its lineage.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_lineage, show_hive
from warehouse import query

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    FROM, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, WHERE, Statement, create_table, equals, more_than,
    statement,
)
from references.mart__run_speed import run_speed
from references.mart__slow_runs import slow_runs  # this table's own Table reference

# The rows write() reads.
READS = equals(run_speed.dt, "2026-09-24")


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.slow_runs from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(slow_runs, may_exist=True)


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: save the runs READS picks that took over 5 minutes more than usual."""
    return statement(
        INSERT_OVERWRITE(slow_runs),
        SELECT(run_speed.event_id, run_speed.job_id, run_speed.minutes_over_median,
               run_speed.dt),
        FROM(run_speed),
        WHERE(READS, more_than(run_speed.minutes_over_median, 5)),
    )

# %%
# 3. Try it: the Hive of each step
show_hive([create(), write()])

# %%
# 4. Lineage: a new pair in lineage/, named by the time and this table, beside the ones before
export_lineage(write())

Done when mart.slow_runs' Table reference and notebook are filled in. Then step 11.

11. Write the tables in order

Goal: each table written after the tables it reads. project.run_tables writes each table before the tables that read it. mart.slow_runs reads what the Python step wrote, so it comes after it; with no execute, it prints each one in order and sends nothing:

project.run_tables(tables=["mart.slow_runs", "mart.run_speed"])

Python prints
-- 1 of 2: mart.run_speed
-- Python step: changed in Python by building_blocks/run_speed.py:add_minutes_over_median,
-- line 10. It reads ops.job_events.event_id, ops.job_events.job_id, ops.job_events.minutes
-- and ops.job_events.dt, and writes mart.run_speed. Nothing is sent for it: your own call
-- saves its rows.
-- 2 of 2: mart.slow_runs
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.slow_runs PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  run_speed.event_id,
  run_speed.job_id,
  run_speed.minutes_over_median,
  run_speed.dt
FROM mart.run_speed AS run_speed
WHERE
  run_speed.dt = '2026-09-24' AND run_speed.minutes_over_median > 5;

Python shows
Printed 2 writes in order, and sent nothing. Pass execute= to run them.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
SELECT
  job_events.event_id,
  job_events.job_id,
  job_events.minutes,
  job_events.dt
FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
WHERE
  job_events.dt = '2026-09-24' AND job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail')
```

## speed_step

A Python step: a change made in Python, outside any Statement, and saved by your own call. The Toolbox can't see inside it, so its columns can't be followed through it, and it never moved its data. It reads ops.job_events.event_id, ops.job_events.job_id, ops.job_events.minutes and ops.job_events.dt, and writes mart.run_speed.

Changed in Python by `building_blocks/run_speed.py:add_minutes_over_median`, line 10. Open the function there to see what the change does.

## statement_3

It reads the Saved table mart.run_speed, written by speed_step above. It writes the Saved table mart.slow_runs, replacing each partition its rows fill.

Every column that is calculated rather than copied, in the order it is calculated.

None: every column is copied.

| --- | --- |

| `dt` | mart.run_speed.dt ← speed_step: Python step |

The columns that tell each step's rows apart, as the Toolbox works them out. A column in one is marked "in the Key" in the chart.

- statement_3: Key (event_id), since run_speed's rows are told apart by event_id.
- mart.slow_runs' Key (event_id) holds: each row written has its own.

### Hive as submitted

```sql
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.slow_runs PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  run_speed.event_id,
  run_speed.job_id,
  run_speed.minutes_over_median,
  run_speed.dt
FROM mart.run_speed AS run_speed
WHERE
  run_speed.dt = '2026-09-24' AND run_speed.minutes_over_median > 5
```

Common mistakes

Running the step

A Python step only declares a change; the rows are yours to save. run_execute refuses it:

run_execute(speed_step, execute)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  run_execute was given a Python step, which sends nothing.
  Why it matters: It sends Hive through your query or execute, and a Python step has none: python_step(...) only declares a change you make in Python and save with your own call, so Lineage can show it. The Toolbox never moves its data.
  Usual fix:      Save the rows with your own call, as you do now, and pass the step to export_lineage(...) with your Statements. show_hive(step) prints comment lines saying what it is, and in a Table notebook's write() project.run_tables passes over it.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Save the rows with your own call, and pass the step to export_lineage.

Passing what the function gave back

add_minutes_over_median(runs) is the changed DataFrame, not the function that changed it:

python_step(add_minutes_over_median(runs), reads=[job_events.minutes],
            writes=ops.job_events)

Python stops with this message
TypeError:
  What happened:  python_step was given a DataFrame, where the function that made the change goes.
  Why it matters: Lineage names where the change lives, so you can open it: the file, the name and the first line of the function, which only a function written with def has.
  Usual fix:      Pass the function itself, without calling it: add_margin, not add_margin(rows), as in python_step(add_margin, reads=[sales.price, sales.cost], writes=margins).
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Pass the function's name alone, with no brackets after it.

A lambda

A lambda has no name, so the Lineage couldn't say where to find it:

python_step(lambda rows: rows, reads=[job_events.minutes], writes=ops.job_events)

Python stops with this message
ValueError:
  What happened:  python_step was given a lambda.
  Why it matters: Lineage names where the change lives, so you can open it: the file, the name and the first line of the function, which only a function written with def has. A lambda has no name to find it by.
  Usual fix:      Write the change as a function with def and a name, in a Building block (project.new_block(...) makes one), and pass it: python_step(add_margin, reads=[sales.price, sales.cost], writes=margins).
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

Write the change with def and a name, in a Building block that project.new_block made.

27. Hand-written Hive with your Table references Intermediate

Goal

Send Hive you write by hand, for what no Clause function writes, with your Table references' names dropped into it by an f-string: unchecked_hive(f"SELECT {ops.jobs.job_id} FROM {ops.jobs}"). A Table reference is the file that describes one table, its columns, its Partitions and its Key; a Clause function, such as SELECT or WHERE, writes one part of a Statement, a query the Toolbox checks. Hive written by hand is an Unchecked statement: each name in it is spelt as its Table reference spells it, run_query sends the text as you wrote it, and Lineage, the record of which columns feed each result, shows it as one step over the tables it reads.

When you'd use it

If you have Hive already, such as a colleague's query, and need it run today, send it as an Unchecked statement, then rewrite it in Clause functions when you have time.
If you need a command the Toolbox doesn't write, such as SHOW PARTITIONS, write it by hand.
If you need a shape of query the Toolbox doesn't write, such as a subquery inside WHERE, write it by hand.
If the Clause functions write what you need, use them: no Guard, Warning or Load limit looks inside hand-written Hive.
If you have no Project or Table references yet, do Start a Project, Add your tables' references and Build and run a first statement first; Follow lineage shows the Lineage it is drawn in.


Steps

1. Start from the Intermediate Example project

Goal: a Project, your folder of scripts at work, to write the Hive in. Work in a copy of the Intermediate Example project, at its top: copy example_projects/intermediate/ from the download to a folder of your own, copy the sqlglot_composer folder into it, as a Project holds it, and open a notebook there. This page makes the same copy in its first step, from the download the Toolbox came in, into the folder it runs in, which Python's path holds already, as Jupyter's does for a notebook's own folder:

It writes references/ops.py

"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_events.event_id.

Its tables: job_events (references/ops__job_events.py), job_owners
(references/ops__job_owners.py), jobs (references/ops__jobs.py), region_budgets
(references/ops__region_budgets.py), region_costs (references/ops__region_costs.py), team_notes
(references/ops__team_notes.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_events import job_events
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners
from references.ops__jobs import jobs
from references.ops__region_budgets import region_budgets
from references.ops__region_costs import region_costs
from references.ops__team_notes import team_notes

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_events", "job_owners", "jobs", "region_budgets", "region_costs", "team_notes"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

"""ops.jobs - TODO: say in one line what one row is."""
from sqlglot_composer import Table

jobs = Table(
    "ops.jobs",
    columns={
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "job_name": "string",
        "team": "string",
        "region": "string",
    },
    key=None,  # TODO: the columns that pick out one row, such as key=["job_id"]
    does_not_add_up=[],  # TODO: columns that are averages, ratios or distinct counts
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.ops__jobs import jobs, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = jobs.job_name
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(jobs.job_name, <value>)

It writes admin/registry.csv too.

from references import ops

query and execute come from the Project's warehouse.py, which in the Example project calls the Example database; yours calls your warehouse.

Done when from references import ops runs without an error. Then step 3.

3. Read a table's text and a column's text

Goal: know what an f-string drops in. A Table reference turned into text, as an f-string turns it, is its name as FROM writes it, with the name Hive calls it by after AS. A column's is that name, a dot, and the column:

str(ops.jobs)  # -> str

Python shows
'ops.jobs AS jobs'

str(ops.jobs.job_id)  # -> str

Python shows
'jobs.job_id'

A column is jobs.job_id, not ops.jobs.job_id, though you reach it in Python as ops.jobs.job_id: Hive names a column by its table's name after AS, and refuses a name of three parts in a SELECT. So the table's text goes after FROM or JOIN, where the AS belongs, and the column's anywhere a column goes.

Done when the table's text ends with AS jobs and the column's is jobs.job_id. Then step 4.

4. Write the Hive in an f-string

Goal: the hand-written Hive, its names spelt by your Table references. Here is Hive handed to you: the jobs that had an event on 2026-09-24, found with a subquery inside WHERE, a shape the Toolbox writes as a SEMI_JOIN instead. Put f before the string, and each name in {...}:

jobs, job_events = ops.jobs, ops.job_events  # -> Table, Table
busy_jobs = unchecked_hive(  # -> UncheckedStatement
    f"SELECT {jobs.job_id}, {jobs.job_name} FROM {jobs} "
    f"WHERE {jobs.job_id} IN (SELECT {job_events.job_id} FROM {job_events} "
    f"WHERE {job_events.dt} = '2026-09-24')")
text = show_hive(busy_jobs)  # -> str

Hive, ready to paste
-- Unchecked statement, written by hand: no Guard, Warning or Load limit looked inside
SELECT jobs.job_id, jobs.job_name FROM ops.jobs AS jobs WHERE jobs.job_id IN (SELECT job_events.job_id FROM ops.job_events AS job_events WHERE job_events.dt = '2026-09-24');

run_query(busy_jobs, query)  # -> DataFrame

Python shows

job_idjob_name

2invoice_sync

```sql
SELECT jobs.job_id, jobs.job_name FROM ops.jobs AS jobs WHERE jobs.job_id IN (SELECT job_events.job_id FROM ops.job_events AS job_events WHERE job_events.dt = '2026-09-24')
```

The text is sent as you wrote it: the Toolbox checks none of it. What the f-string gives you is the spelling: a column renamed in its Table reference is renamed here too, and a name mistyped, such as jobs.job_idd, stops at once with a message naming the table's columns, rather than reaching the warehouse.

Done when the run gives jobs 1 and 2. Then step 5.

5. Send it with the call that suits it

Goal: each piece of Hive sent through the right one of your two calls. You choose the call by the function you call: run_query sends the text through your query, which gives back a DataFrame, as for a SELECT, SHOW or DESCRIBE; run_execute sends it through your execute, which gives back nothing, as for a CREATE or ALTER. A view, which the Toolbox doesn't write, is created through your execute; the Example database's prints the first line it is given, and changes nothing:

finance_jobs = unchecked_hive(  # -> UncheckedStatement
    f"CREATE VIEW IF NOT EXISTS mart.finance_jobs AS "
    f"SELECT {jobs.job_id}, {jobs.job_name} FROM {jobs} WHERE {jobs.team} = 'finance'")
run_execute(finance_jobs, execute)

Python prints
execute: CREATE VIEW IF NOT EXISTS mart.finance_jobs AS SELECT jobs.job_id, jobs.job_name FROM ops.jobs AS jobs WHERE jobs.team = 'finance'
